  [G1.1] Essential facts (personal details) stated in the report are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   John Sullivan, age 69; Kim Sullivan, age 65. Both married. Three grown-up children, all financially independent. Both in good health.

    File:     Fact find [P5] shows a spouse date of birth of 07/05/1960, age 65, consistent with Kim Sullivan age 65. The fact find income section [P11] references an employed individual at CDH Recruitment. Client agreement [P3] references 'Sullivan'. No explicit date of birth for John Sullivan is present in the retrieved passages. The figure-match check shows age 65 corroborated in [P5] but age 69 for John was not matched (nearest is 75 in [P12]). No passage explicitly states John's age as 69 or his date of birth. No passage explicitly corroborates the three grown-up children or the good health assertions from an independent KYC source (fact find passages retrieved do not show a dependants section confirming three children or a health section).

    Discrepancies:

      - John Sullivan's stated age of 69 is not corroborated by any retrieved passage; the nearest numeric match is 75 in [P12] which is an age used in the cashflow chart axis labels, not a client age record.

      - No date of birth for John Sullivan (C1) appears in any retrieved passage, so the age of 69 cannot be verified against a DOB.

      - The three grown-up children assertion and both-clients-in-good-health assertion appear only in the suitability report itself; no independent KYC/fact-find passage in the evidence pack corroborates these.

      - The fact find [P5] shows a Family and Dependants entry with DOB 07/05/1960, age 65, which aligns with Kim Sullivan, but no equivalent entry for John Sullivan's personal details (name, DOB, age) is visible in the retrieved passages.

    Kim Sullivan's age of 65 is corroborated by [P5] which records DOB 07/05/1960 and age 65. The guard noting that age may differ by 1 year where the fact find predates the report is noted, but here the ages match exactly. John Sullivan's age of 69 cannot be verified: the figure-match check found no passage recording age 69 for the client, and no DOB is available to derive it. The value 75 in [P12] is a cashflow chart axis label (69/65 at start, 74/70, 79/75 etc.) and is not a client age record — it cannot corroborate or contradict John's stated age of 69. The absence of John's personal details from the retrieved KYC evidence means the report's assertion of age 69 is unsupported. The three children and health assertions are stated only in the suitability report with no independent corroboration in the fact find passages retrieved. These are material personal facts that should be supported by KYC documentation. The marital status (married) for both clients is not directly contradicted by any evidence but is also not explicitly corroborated in the retrieved passages beyond the inference from the spouse relationship shown in [P5]. Overall, the primary concern is the absence of corroborating evidence for John Sullivan's age of 69 and the lack of KYC support for the dependants and health statements. These are not contradictions but unsupported assertions, which under the decision logic constitute a Potential Concern.

  [G1.2] Tax status stated in the report is consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts on page 20 that the client has not triggered the MPAA and does not have a lower annual allowance. The report also references the Annual Allowance as £60,000 per annum for 2024/2025 and mentions that high earners may have a lower annual allowance. The income tax band, territorial profile and specific annual allowance applicable to this client are not stated in the extracted model (paths absent from extraction). The report's capacity for loss section (P22) states the client is in receipt of a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, earns around £300 per week net as an HGV driver, and receives £20 per week from the Sea Cadets. The fact find (P17) records highest rate of income tax paid as 0% and total annual self-employed gross profit/salary as £0.00, with net basic monthly income of £1,200.00.

    File:     The fact find [P17] records: highest rate of income tax paid = 0%, total annual self-employed gross profit/dividend/salary = £0.00, net basic monthly income = £1,200.00 (employed). No document in the pack records the client's income tax band explicitly, territorial profile or annual allowance position. None of the provider documents (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, Scottish Widows, People's Pension) contain a statement about the client's MPAA status. The report's own narrative in P19 states 'You have not yet triggered the MPAA, and you do not have a lower annual allowance' but this appears within the suitability report itself, not in an independent supporting document. The suitability report in P19 also states the Annual Allowance is currently £60,000 per annum 2024/2025 as a generic legislative statement.

    Discrepancies:

      - The MPAA-not-triggered assertion in the report is made without any corroborating evidence from an independent case file document (fact find, provider letter or HMRC record) confirming MPAA status.

      - The client's income tax band is not recorded in the report (path absent from extraction) and is not corroborated by any evidence document, yet tax treatment is relied upon in the recommendation (e.g. Scottish Widows encashment net receipt of ~£170 implying 20% marginal rate, drawdown income taxed at marginal rate).

      - The fact find records highest rate of income tax paid as 0%, but the suitability report narrative implies income tax will apply to drawdown withdrawals at the client's marginal rate and calculates a net encashment of ~£170 (implying a non-zero tax rate). A 0% tax rate recorded in the fact find and a non-zero marginal rate applied in the report are potentially inconsistent.

      - No territorial profile is recorded in the extracted model or in any evidence passage, so it cannot be confirmed whether Scottish or rUK income tax bands apply, which is relevant to the encashment and drawdown tax calculations.

    The report's assertion that the MPAA has not been triggered is a client-specific statement that should be supported by an independent document such as the fact find or a provider confirmation. No such corroborating passage exists in the pack; the only source is the suitability report itself, which is the document under review. This leaves the assertion unsupported. More materially, the fact find records the highest rate of income tax paid as 0%, yet the report applies a non-zero marginal tax rate when calculating the net proceeds of the Scottish Widows encashment (~£170, implying 25% tax-free and 75% at a marginal rate above 0%), and the report's general discussion of drawdown confirms income withdrawals will be taxed at the client's marginal rate. This is a potential internal inconsistency between the fact find tax figure and the tax treatment assumed in the report. The guard on generic legislative statements applies to the £60,000 annual allowance figure — that is a general statement, not a client-specific application, so no finding arises from it alone. However, the MPAA assertion and the mismatch between the 0% tax rate in the fact find and the non-zero rate implied by the encashment calculation are client-specific and do require corroboration. The territorial profile is absent, preventing confirmation that rUK (rather than Scottish) bands are correctly applied, but this is a secondary gap. The primary concern is the 0% tax rate in the fact find versus the implied non-zero marginal rate used in the report's tax calculations.

  [G1.3] Employment details are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Client 1 (JS) is employed full-time as an HGV driver through an agency, earning approximately £300 per week net, with an intended retirement age of 71. Client 2 (Kim/KS) is employed, with an intended retirement age of 66.

    File:     Fact Find (P5) records Client 1 as Employed, Occupation: HGV Driver, Employer: CDH Recruitment. Fact Find income table (P13) records 'Basic Income, Employed, CDH Recruitment, Monthly, £0.00 gross, £1,200.00 net'. Cashflow baseline (P3) shows Employment income for Client 1 of £16,640 per annum. The suitability report cashflow section (P15) states 'You receive £300 per week as an HGV driver'. No intended retirement age for either client is recorded in the fact find or any supporting evidence document retrieved. The fact find (P5) records a spouse date of birth of 07/05/1960 (age 65 at the time of the fact find, consistent with Kim's retirement age of 66 being imminent).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the employer is an 'Agency (unspecified)' but the fact find names the employer as 'CDH Recruitment' — a specific named employer rather than an unnamed agency.

      - The report states Client 1 earns 'around £300 per week net'; the fact find income table records net basic monthly income of £1,200.00 (which annualises to £14,400 p.a.), whereas £300/week net annualises to £15,600 p.a. — a difference of £1,200 p.a. The cashflow baseline shows employment income of £16,640 p.a. for Client 1, which is closer to £320/week, not £300/week, adding a further inconsistency.

      - The intended retirement ages of 71 (Client 1) and 66 (Client 2) are not corroborated by any evidence document in the pack — no fact find field or supporting document records these figures.

    Three discrepancies are identified. First, the employer name: the report says 'Agency (unspecified)' but the fact find clearly names 'CDH Recruitment'. This is a factual mismatch between the report and the evidence, though it does not directly affect the financial recommendation. Second, the weekly income figure: £300/week net (report) equates to approximately £1,300/month or £15,600/year, but the fact find records net monthly income of £1,200 (£14,400/year), and the cashflow baseline uses £16,640/year — three different figures are in circulation for the same income stream. Income underpins affordability and the cashflow modelling, so this inconsistency is material. The guard notes that a later file note may supersede an earlier fact find, but no such later document is in the pack to resolve the discrepancy. Third, neither retirement age (71 for JS, 66 for Kim) is supported by any document in the evidence pack. The risk profile document (P19) records an investment period of 2 years and an investment of £106,000, which is consistent with a near-term retirement but does not state a retirement age. The absence of evidential support for the retirement ages is a concern because the intended retirement age drives the cashflow modelling horizon and the critical yield calculation. The employer name mismatch is moderate in isolation, but the income inconsistency and unsupported retirement ages are material given their role in the advice. Overall outcome is Potential Concern.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   C1 (John) earns £300/week net as HGV driver, £20/week from Sea Cadets volunteer role, and receives full State Pension of £230/week. C2 (Kim) earns approximately £1,100/month net and will receive full State Pension of around £230/week at State Pension age. Household expenditure is approximately £1,700/month covering rent, utilities, food, transport, entertainment and insurance. Total assets £6,000 (cash savings), no liabilities.

    File:     Fact Find [P10] shows C1 income: Basic Income (employed, CDH Recruitment) £1,200/month net, State Pension £230/month. Total net monthly income shown as £1,430. Fact Find [P10] shows total calculated monthly household expenditure of £1,718. Detailed expenditure breakdown in [P1] includes Rent £600, Council Tax £200, Electricity £120, Water £60, Food & Personal Care £300, Car/Travelling £150, Building Insurance £25, Entertainment £100 — summing to £1,555 with further items not shown, total £1,718. Savings of £6,000 confirmed in [P13]. No liabilities recorded. Cashflow modelling [P9] uses state pension income of £11,973 per annum (£230.25/week) and Kim's income of £1,100/month.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states C1 earns £300/week net (≈£1,300/month) as an HGV driver. The fact find [P10] records C1 basic employment income as £1,200/month net — a difference of approximately £100/month (7.7%), exceeding the 5% tolerance.

      - The report states an additional £20/week income from a Sea Cadets volunteer role. The fact find [P10] income table does not show any Sea Cadets or volunteer income line; total net monthly income is recorded as £1,430 which corresponds to £1,200 employment + £230 state pension only, with no volunteer income included.

      - The report states household expenditure is approximately £1,700/month. The fact find [P1] calculates total monthly household expenditure as £1,718 — a difference of £18 (1.1%), within the 5% rounding tolerance but the exact figures differ.

      - The fact find [P10] records State Pension as £230/month (monthly frequency), while the report states £230/week. These are materially different values: £230/month vs £230/week (£997/month equivalent). However, the cashflow in [P9] uses £11,973 per annum which equates to £230/week (£11,973 ÷ 52 = £230.25), suggesting the fact find frequency label 'Monthly' for £230 may be a data-entry error in the system rather than a genuine conflict with the cashflow modelling.

      - The report states combined pension value of approximately £110,000 (page 3 narrative) whereas arithmetic sum of all five plans is £116,998.47 and the capacity for loss section (per extraction) states approximately £106,000 — three different figures for the same total.

    Two material issues arise. First, C1's employment income: the report states £300/week net (annualising at 52 weeks = £15,600/year, or £1,300/month), but the fact find [P10] records the net basic monthly income as £1,200. The guard warns that weekly figures annualise at 52 not 4.33×12, so £300/week = £1,300/month, not £1,200/month. The fact find figure of £1,200/month is not reconcilable with £300/week even after applying the correct annualisation — the difference is £100/month (7.7%), exceeding the 5% tolerance. This is a material mismatch on an income figure. Second, the Sea Cadets volunteer income of £20/week stated in the report does not appear in the fact find income table at all, and the fact find total monthly income of £1,430 (= £1,200 + £230 state pension) leaves no room for it. This is an unsupported income item. The £230 state pension frequency discrepancy between the fact find (£230/month) and the report/cashflow (£230/week) is likely a fact-find data-entry labelling error given that the cashflow correctly uses £11,973/year (≈£230/week), but the labelling inconsistency remains notable. The household expenditure difference (£1,700 report vs £1,718 fact find) is within rounding tolerance and is not a material concern. Assets (£6,000 cash) and liabilities (£nil) are consistent across sources. The combined pension value inconsistency (£110,000 vs £106,000 vs arithmetic £116,998.47) is already flagged as an internal inconsistency and represents a further mismatch, though that is partly addressed under other checks. The primary findings for this requirement are: (1) C1 employment income £300/week (report) vs £1,200/month (fact find) — material mismatch exceeding 5% tolerance; (2) Sea Cadets £20/week income not supported by fact find evidence.

  [G1.5] Assets and liabilities are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts cash savings of £6,000 (joint, C1/C2) under Assets & Liabilities (page 3, quote: 'You hold savings of around £6,000'). No liabilities are stated in the extracted model (/financialPosition/liabilities[] is absent). The report also states a combined pension value of 'approximately £110,000' (page 3) and 'pension funds totalling approximately £106,000' (page 4, Capacity for Loss section). The individual pension values cited are: People's Pension £6,601.55, Scottish Widows £211.32, Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002.00.

    File:     Fact Find [P6] records cash savings of £6,000.00 (Cash Account, JS). The Fact Find liabilities section [P6] shows no liabilities recorded. The cashflow baseline [P5] records Savings (JS, Cash Account) opening value £6,000 and total liabilities of £0. The cashflow proposed scenario [P9] records Liquid Assets £6,000 and total pensions £116,997 (sum of all five plans). The cashflow baseline asset tree [P17] shows total assets £122,997 (Liquid Assets £6,000, Pensions £116,997). The cashflow proposed scenario asset tree [P9] shows total assets £122,997 (Liquid Assets £6,000, Pensions £116,997). Individual pension values in the cashflow [P5]: Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002, Peoples Pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211, total £116,997.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states combined pension value of 'approximately £110,000' on page 3, but the arithmetic sum of all five individual plan values is £116,998.47 (per derived figures) and the cashflow tool records £116,997. Neither matches £110,000.

      - The report states 'pension funds totalling approximately £106,000' on page 4 (Capacity for Loss). The actual sum of all five plans is £116,998.47 / £116,997. Neither matches £106,000.

      - The two internal report figures for combined pension value disagree with each other: £110,000 (page 3) vs £106,000 (page 4).

      - The report's page 3 figure of '~£110,000' for combined pensions appears to match the transfer value of the three switching plans only (Aviva £3,744.36 + Zurich £103,439.24 + Standard Life £3,002.00 = £110,185.60), not the combined value of all five plans, suggesting it conflates the new plan transfer value with the total existing pension value.

      - No liabilities are stated in the canonical model; however the Fact Find [P6] and cashflow [P5] both record nil liabilities, so this is consistent rather than a discrepancy.

      - Cash savings of £6,000 is consistent across report, fact find and cashflow — no discrepancy on this figure.

    The cash savings figure of £6,000 is well supported: the suitability report, fact find [P6], cashflow baseline [P5] and proposed scenario [P9] all state £6,000. No discrepancy exists on this point. Liabilities: the report is silent on liabilities, the fact find [P6] records no liabilities, and the cashflow [P5] records liabilities total of £0. This is internally consistent and raises no concern. The material issue lies with the pension totals. The derived figures confirm the arithmetic sum of all five individual plan values is £116,998.47, matching the cashflow tool's £116,997. The report makes two internal assertions about the combined pension value that both conflict with this sum and with each other: 'approximately £110,000' (page 3) and 'approximately £106,000' (page 4). The £110,000 figure corresponds to the transfer value of the three plans being switched (£110,185.60), not the combined value of all five plans. Using this figure as the combined value in the narrative is misleading and arithmetically incorrect. The £106,000 figure on page 4 matches no computable total from the individual plan values and has no supporting basis in any evidence passage. These are internal inconsistencies within the report itself, confirmed by the derived figures, and both conflict with the cashflow evidence. The guards note that an assets table excluding pensions is normal, but here the report explicitly discusses combined pension value in narrative sections, so the guard does not apply. The valuation-date guard is noted but all sources appear to reference the same point-in-time values. The two contradictory figures for total pension value (one used in the narrative context, one used in the capacity-for-loss section) constitute a material internal inconsistency — the capacity-for-loss conclusion relies on the £106,000 figure which is unsupported, and the page 3 narrative misrepresents the total by using the transfer-only subtotal.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   Client 1 (JS) is in receipt of his full State Pension of £230 per week. Client 2 (Kim/KS) will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age. Both entitlements are described as full. The report also states (in the cashflow section, P5) that JS receives state pension income of £11,973 per annum and Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67.

    File:     P9 (suitability report narrative) corroborates £230 per week for JS in payment and £230 per week for Kim on reaching State Pension age. P5 (cashflow section of suitability report) states £11,973 per annum for JS and £11,973 per annum for Kim from age 67. P1 (intelliflo baseline scenario) records a State Pension Age of 66 for both JS and KS. P15 (fact find) contains a 'State Pension Entitlement' section but the fields for Basic State Pension amount, Additional State Pension, BR19 Projection etc. are blank — no figures are entered. No separate state pension forecast or HMRC/DWP document is present in the pack.

    Discrepancies:

      - £230 per week x 52 = £11,960 per annum, but the report states £11,973 per annum in the cashflow section — a difference of £13 per annum that cannot be explained by rounding alone.

      - The report states Kim will receive her State Pension from 'State Pension age' in the narrative (P9) but the cashflow section (P5) states 'from age 67', whereas the intelliflo planning tool (P1) records KS's State Pension Age as 66. There is a one-year discrepancy between the cashflow assumption (age 67) and the planning tool input (age 66).

      - The fact find (P15) State Pension Entitlement section is entirely blank — no amounts, no BR19 projection, no supporting figures — meaning there is no independent documentary evidence in the file to corroborate either the £230 per week or the £11,973 per annum figures.

    Two material issues arise. First, the weekly-to-annual conversion does not reconcile: £230 x 52 = £11,960, but the report asserts £11,973 per annum in the cashflow section (P5). This £13 difference is small in absolute terms but represents an internal arithmetic inconsistency within the report's own figures and cannot be attributed to rounding (rounding £230 x 52 to the nearest pound yields £11,960, not £11,973). Second, the age at which Kim's State Pension commences is stated as age 67 in the cashflow section (P5) but the intelliflo planning baseline scenario (P1) records her State Pension Age as 66. The guard notes that State Pension age and retirement age are different things and can be conflated; however, both P1 and P5 are explicitly labelled as State Pension inputs/outputs, so this is a genuine discrepancy between two documents in the pack rather than a conflation of different concepts. Third, the fact find State Pension Entitlement section (P15) is blank, providing no independent corroboration of the £230 per week figure for either client. The only passage that corroborates £230 per week is P9, which is itself the suitability report — it is not independent evidence. No BR19, HMRC forecast or DWP document appears in the pack. The 'evidence absent' rule therefore applies: state pension entitlement is named explicitly by this check and no forecast is on file. The combination of the internal arithmetic mismatch (£11,960 vs £11,973), the age discrepancy (66 vs 67 for Kim), and the absence of any independent supporting document means this group cannot be cleared as No Issue.

  [G1.7] Existing products (pensions, investments and protection plans) are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Five existing pension arrangements: (1) People's Pension 6197175/PP, fund/transfer value £6,601.55, contributions £1,193.33 PA (client) and £897.13 PA (employer); (2) Scottish Widows ZU4225575, fund/transfer value £211.32, no contributions; (3) Aviva SP50163762, fund/transfer value £3,744.36, no contributions, fund: Aviva pension my future focus consolidation (risk 2); (4) Zurich P10026-719-001/DL, fund/transfer value £103,439.24, no contributions, fund: Managed AP (risk 7); (5) Standard Life D2301334000, fund/transfer value £3,002, no contributions, fund: SL at retirement universal (PP 10 year) pension fund (risk 4). Combined value stated as approximately £110,000 on page 3 and approximately £106,000 on page 4. Charge comparison shows Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8) but charges table shows +0.26% impact for EA5 (page 11). EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) not Standard Life's (£3,002).

    File:     Aviva policy info (SP50163762): plan value and transfer value both £3,744.36 as at 22 September 2025, fund: Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2. Zurich policy info (P10026-719-001/DL): fund value £103,439.24 (Managed AP accumulation, 492.05 units at £210.221), as at 24 September 2025. Standard Life policy info (D2301334000): current transfer value £3,002.02 (transfer charge £0.00), fund: SL At Retirement Universal (PP10 Year) Pension Fd 100%. People's Pension (6197175/PP): contribution history evidenced but no explicit total fund value statement visible in retrieved passages. Scottish Widows (ZU4225575): no fund value statement retrieved. Fact find shows a combined pension value of £116,555.45 as at 23/07/2026 (Aviva Platform new plan AV2936864-001 shown separately). Derived figures confirm sum of five individual plan values = £116,998.47, matching neither the £110,000 nor the £106,000 narrative totals. EA5 charge rows use a fund value implying ~£103,439, which is Zurich's value, not Standard Life's £3,002.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report page 3 states combined pension value 'approximately £110,000'; arithmetic sum of five stated individual values is £116,998.47 — a difference of ~£6,998.

      - Report page 4 (Capacity for Loss) states 'pension funds totalling approximately £106,000'; arithmetic sum is £116,998.47 — a difference of ~£10,998.

      - The two narrative totals in the report itself differ: approximately £110,000 (page 3) vs approximately £106,000 (page 4).

      - Report page 8 states Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. new plan is cheaper); charges table page 11 shows EA5 impact as +0.26% (new plan more expensive). These are directionally and quantitatively contradictory.

      - EA5 charge rows (existing annual charge £186.19 at 0.18%, impact £268.94 at 0.26%) imply a fund value of ~£103,439 — Zurich's value — not Standard Life's £3,002; the Standard Life charge comparison rows are computed on the wrong plan's fund value.

      - Standard Life provider statement shows transfer value £3,002.02 (to the penny); report states £3,002 (rounded, though within rounding tolerance).

      - No fund value statement for People's Pension or Scottish Widows retrieved from provider documents to corroborate £6,601.55 and £211.32 respectively.

      - Performance chart labels on page 13 swap instruments A and D between the two charts on the same page (first chart: A=Aviva My Future Focus, D=Zurich Managed AP; volatility chart: A=Zurich Managed AP, D=Aviva My Future Focus).

      - Critical yield table (page 18) labels mid growth rate as 2.95%; page 11 narrative refers to 'a mid-growth rate of 2.94%'.

    Several material issues are identified. First, the report contains two different internal narrative totals for the combined pension value (£110,000 on page 3 and £106,000 on page 4), and neither matches the arithmetic sum of the five individual plan values stated in the same report (£116,998.47). This is a self-contradiction on a figure the report relies on and requires no external evidence to establish — it is a High severity finding in its own right. Second, the Standard Life charge comparison is demonstrably wrong: the EA5 charge rows (£186.19/0.18% existing and £268.94/0.26% impact) imply a fund base of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's £3,002. This means the page 8 narrative ('0.08% more expensive') and the page 11 table figure (+0.26%) are both unreliable for Standard Life, and they contradict each other. This is a material arithmetic error affecting the charge/cost comparison for EA5. Third, Aviva (£3,744.36 as at 22/09/2025) and Zurich (£103,439.24 as at 24/09/2025) are corroborated to the penny by provider statements. Standard Life (£3,002.02 provider vs £3,002 report) is within rounding tolerance. People's Pension and Scottish Widows values are not corroborated by provider documents in the evidence pack — this is a missing evidence concern but the values do appear in the suitability report's own table. The fund name for Aviva ('Aviva pension my future focus consolidation') aligns with the provider statement ('Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2'). Zurich fund 'Managed AP' aligns with 'Managed AP (accumulation)' in the provider statement. Standard Life fund name aligns with provider statement. The performance chart label swap (page 13) and the 2.94%/2.95% growth rate discrepancy are lower-severity internal inconsistencies. The guard on values moving between dates applies to the Aviva and Zurich comparisons, but since the report values match the provider statements to the penny at their respective statement dates, no concern arises there. The missing People's Pension and Scottish Widows provider statements mean those two values are unsupported by category E evidence. Overall, the combination of the self-contradictory combined total figures, the EA5 charge rows computed on the wrong fund value producing contradictory cost-saving claims, and two plan values unsupported by provider documentation constitutes material concern.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report contains the following internally inconsistent figures and labels: (1) Page 3 states combined pension value is 'approximately £110,000'; page 4 states 'pension funds totalling approximately £106,000'; arithmetic sum of all five individual values is £116,998.47 — none of the three agree. (2) Page 9 states the new Aviva Pension Portfolio total transfer value is £110,185, which is consistent with the three switching plans summing to £110,185.60, but the page 3 narrative of 'approximately £110,000' covers all five plans whose true sum is £116,998.47. (3) Page 8 states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving from switching); page 11 charges table shows the impact for Standard Life as '+0.26%' (an increase in cost). (4) Page 7 states Aviva existing plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution'; page 11 table shows '-0.06%' (consistent directionally). (5) Page 13 first performance chart labels A=Aviva My Future Focus Consolidation, D=Zurich Managed Pn AP; the volatility chart key on the same page swaps them: A=Zurich Managed Pn AP, D=Aviva My Future Focus Consolidation. (6) Page 11 states new Aviva plan is '0.24% less expensive' on a like-for-like basis, but '4.24% more expensive' including adviser fees, with monetary tolerance jumping from £264 to £4,672. (7) Page 11 narrative refers to 'a mid-growth rate of 2.94%'; page 18 critical yield table labels it '2.95%'. Additionally, derived figures confirm: EA5 (Standard Life, current value £3,002) charge rows are computed on a fund value of ~£103,438 (Zurich's value), making the EA5 charge comparison unreliable.

    File:     This requirement compares the report against itself. No external supporting documents are assessed here. The derived figures computed from the report's own extracted values confirm: the five individual plan values sum to £116,998.47; EA5 charge rows (£186.19 at 0.18% and impact £268.94 at 0.26%) both imply a fund value of ~£103,438, which is Zurich's value, not Standard Life's value of £3,002; the EA5 impact of +0.26% in the table directly contradicts the page 8 narrative of -0.08% saving for Standard Life.

    Discrepancies:

      - (1) Combined pension value stated as 'approximately £110,000' on page 3, 'approximately £106,000' on page 4, and arithmetic sum of all five individual values is £116,998.47 — three different figures, none reconciling.

      - (2) Page 3 narrative describes 'approximately £110,000' as covering all five plans, but the true sum of all five is £116,998.47; the £110,185 figure on page 9 covers only the three switching plans (£110,185.60), not all five.

      - (3) Page 8 states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (switching saves 0.08%); page 11 charges table shows Standard Life impact as '+0.26%' (switching costs more, not less) — a direct sign and magnitude contradiction.

      - (4) EA5 (Standard Life, current value £3,002) charge rows in the table imply a fund value of ~£103,438 (Zurich's value), confirming the Standard Life charge comparison is computed on the wrong plan value, making the +0.26% figure itself unreliable.

      - (5) Page 13 first performance chart: A=Aviva My Future Focus Consolidation, D=Zurich Managed Pn AP; volatility chart key on the same page: A=Zurich Managed Pn AP GTR in GB, D=Aviva My Future Focus Consolidation — labels A and D are swapped between the two charts on the same page.

      - (6) Page 11 mid-growth rate referred to as '2.94%' in narrative; page 18 critical yield table labels it '2.95%' — a marginal inconsistency of 0.01%.

      - (7) Page 11 presents both '0.24% less expensive' (like-for-like) and '4.24% more expensive' (including adviser fees) without reconciliation; monetary tolerance figures jump from £264 to £4,672.

    All discrepancies are internal to the report and confirmed by the report's own extracted values and the derived figures. No external documents are required or assessed. The most material issues are: (a) The three-way inconsistency in combined pension values (£110,000 / £106,000 / £116,998.47) — none of the three figures agrees with the arithmetic sum of the individually stated plan values. This is a self-contradiction on a figure that underpins the recommendation context, rated High. (b) The Standard Life charge contradiction — page 8 asserts switching saves 0.08%; page 11 table shows switching costs 0.26% more. The derived figures further confirm that the EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,438) rather than Standard Life's £3,002, meaning the charge comparison for EA5 is built on the wrong base entirely. This directly affects the recommendation rationale and is rated High. (c) The chart label swap on page 13 (A and D reversed between the two charts) is a material presentation error that could cause a reader to attribute the wrong performance and volatility to the wrong fund, rated Moderate. (d) The 2.94% vs 2.95% mid-growth rate is a marginal rounding inconsistency, rated Low. (e) The like-for-like vs including-adviser-fee presentation is not strictly contradictory (they measure different things) but the lack of reconciliation is a presentation concern; as it relates to costs and charges it falls partly within CHK-007, but the failure to reconcile two figures stated in the same section of the same report is also an internal consistency issue. The date guard does not apply to any of these discrepancies as none involves an 'as at' date differential. Together, the High-severity items — the wrong total pension values used throughout and the Standard Life charge row computed on the wrong fund value — are material to the report's factual accuracy and the figures relied upon in the recommendation narrative.

 

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Income sources: (1) Alan earns ~£300/week net from employment; (2) Alan receives £20/week from Sea Cadets volunteer role; (3) Alan receives full State Pension of £230/week; (4) Kim earns ~£1,100/month net; (5) Kim will receive full State Pension of ~£230/week at State Pension age. Monthly household expenditure: £1,700/month. Combined pension value stated as 'approximately £110,000' on page 3 and 'approximately £106,000' on page 4 (Capacity for Loss). Arithmetic sum of five individual plan values is £116,998.47.

    File:     Fact find [P2] shows Kim's basic income as £1,200/month net (not £1,100) and State Pension as £230/month (not £230/week). Total net monthly income per fact find is £1,430. Fact find [P1] shows total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 (not £1,700). Fact find [P2] records Alan's employment income as £300/month (though labelled as 'Basic Income'). The £20/week Sea Cadets income does not appear in the fact find income table. The derived arithmetic sum of the five pension plan values is £116,998.47, matching neither the page 3 '£110,000' nor the page 4 '£106,000' figures.

    Discrepancies:

      - Kim's income: report states £1,100/month net; fact find [P2] records £1,200/month net — a £100/month difference.

      - State Pension frequency for Kim: report states £230/week; fact find [P2] records £230/month — frequency mismatch (£230/week = ~£996/month vs £230/month are materially different figures).

      - Alan's State Pension frequency: report states £230/week; fact find [P2] records £230/month — same frequency mismatch.

      - Monthly expenditure: report states £1,700/month; fact find [P1] records £1,718/month calculated total — £18 difference.

      - £20/week Sea Cadets income: stated in report but not evidenced in the fact find income table.

      - Total pension value page 3 ('approximately £110,000') does not match arithmetic sum of individual plan values (£116,998.47) — understated by ~£6,998.

      - Total pension value page 4 ('approximately £106,000') does not match arithmetic sum of individual plan values (£116,998.47) — understated by ~£10,998.

      - Pages 3 and 4 of the report give two different combined pension values: £110,000 vs £106,000 — internal inconsistency of ~£4,000.

    Multiple discrepancies are identified. First, Kim's income: the report says £1,100/month net but the fact find [P2] records £1,200/month net — a clear £100/month shortfall in the report. This is a material factual mismatch between the suitability report and the supporting evidence. Second, State Pension frequency: the report states both Alan and Kim receive/will receive £230/week in State Pension. The fact find [P2] records £230/month for Alan's State Pension (under the State Pension row). £230/week would equate to approximately £11,960/year whereas £230/month equates to £2,760/year — these are materially different figures. The full new State Pension is approximately £221-£230/week as of 2025, so the weekly figure in the report is plausible for Alan who is retired, but the fact find records it as monthly. For Kim it is a future entitlement and again the report says £230/week vs the fact find recording £230/month. This frequency discrepancy is material. Third, monthly expenditure: the report states £1,700/month but the fact find calculated total is £1,718/month [P1]. The rounding guard covers minor differences; £18 is small but noted. The report's figure of £1,700 aligns with the client's stated need of £1,700 net per month income in retirement [P5, P12], which may explain why this figure is used — it is the income goal rather than the actual current expenditure. However, as a stated expenditure figure it differs from the fact find total. Fourth, the £20/week Sea Cadets income is not corroborated in the fact find income table [P2], creating an unsupported assertion. Fifth, the combined pension total inconsistencies are internal contradictions already identified: page 3 says ~£110,000, page 4 says ~£106,000, and the arithmetic sum is £116,998.47. None of these three figures agree. The £110,000 figure appears to correspond to the transfer value of the three switching plans (£110,185.60) rather than the combined value of all five plans, suggesting the narrative on page 3 conflates the transfer total with the combined total. The most material issues are the Kim income mismatch (£1,100 vs £1,200/month) and the State Pension frequency discrepancy (weekly vs monthly), both of which affect the financial position assessment. The internal pension total inconsistencies are also material as they appear in two different parts of the report with different values, neither matching the arithmetic sum.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Cashflow was modelled. Assumptions: surplus income saved 0%; general expenditure inflation 2%; savings inflation 2%; state pensions inflation 2.5%; Aviva personal pensions growth 5%; projection end age 100; critical yield retirement age 75; low growth rate 0.00%; mid growth rate 2.95% (also referenced as 2.94% on page 11); high growth rate 5.88%; high inflation stress test 6%; market crash stress test -30%. Cashflow inputs include John's HGV income £300/week, Kim's income £1,100/month, state pension £11,973 p.a. each, retirement expenditure ~£1,700/annum. Pension inputs in cashflow: Aviva transfer £3,744, Zurich transfer £103,439, Standard Life £3,002. Combined pension value described as 'approximately £110,000' on page 3 and 'approximately £106,000' on page 4. Arithmetic sum of five individual plans = £116,998.47. Critical yield table mid growth rate label: 2.95%; page 11 narrative: 2.94%. EA5 (Standard Life, value £3,002) charges row computed on Zurich's value (~£103,439) not Standard Life's value. Standard Life charge impact shown as +0.26% in table vs saving of 0.08% stated in narrative.

    File:     Intelliflo proposed scenario [P7] shows asset tree: Savings £6,000; Peoples pension £6,601; Scottish Widows £211; New Aviva pension £110,185. Total pensions in baseline [P15] = £116,997 (five plans: Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002, Peoples Pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211). Cashflow assumptions in proposed scenario [P12]: surplus income 0%, general inflation 2%, state pension 2.5% — consistent with report. Pension switch report [P16/P17] shows Zurich standalone mid growth rate 2.95%, critical yield table consistent. [P19] shows Standard Life investment charge 0.18%.     Discrepancies:

      - Report page 3 states combined pension value 'approximately £110,000'; arithmetic sum of all five plans is £116,998.47; neither matches the other.

      - Report page 4 states pension funds 'approximately £106,000'; this differs from both the page 3 figure (£110,000) and the arithmetic sum (£116,998.47).

      - Mid growth rate stated as 2.95% in critical yield table (page 18) but as 2.94% in page 11 narrative — two different figures for the same assumption within the same report.

      - EA5 (Standard Life, current value £3,002) annual charge and impact rows are arithmetically computed on a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not £3,002 — the wrong plan's value is used in the charges comparison for Standard Life.

      - Standard Life charge impact shown as +0.26% in charges table (page 11) but narrative on page 8 states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' — directional and magnitude contradiction.

      - People's Pension (£6,601.55) appears in intelliflo asset tree [P7] but is not listed as a cashflow input in the suitability report's cashflow section (only Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002 are listed).

      - Performance chart labels on page 13 swap A/D between the two charts (first chart: A=Aviva My Future Focus, D=Zurich; second chart key: A=Zurich, D=Aviva).

    Multiple internal arithmetic and consistency failures are confirmed by the derived figures and the evidence pack, without needing external documents. (1) The combined pension total is stated inconsistently as ~£110,000 (page 3) and ~£106,000 (page 4); neither matches the arithmetic sum of the five individually stated plan values (£116,998.47). This is a self-contradiction within the report on a figure used in the capacity-for-loss and recommendation context. (2) The mid growth rate is cited as 2.94% (page 11) and 2.95% (page 18 table) — a minor but real internal inconsistency. The 2.95% label is corroborated by [P11] and [P16/P17]; the 2.94% figure is also present in [P16]. This is a rounding/presentation difference but it is a genuine internal discrepancy. (3) The EA5 (Standard Life) charges rows are arithmetically derived from a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), confirmed by the derived figures. This means the charges comparison for Standard Life is computed on the wrong plan entirely, making the +0.26% impact figure unreliable. The narrative on page 8 claiming a 0.08% saving for Standard Life then contradicts the table's +0.26% increase — a material directional and magnitude mismatch on a figure used to justify the recommendation. The market crash guard and growth rate guard do not apply here as these are not prescribed provider rates but report-internal figures. The as-at date guard does not resolve these since they appear in the same report. The modelling gap guard is not triggered as cashflow was confirmed as run (wasCashflowModelled=true). The People's Pension omission from the cashflow inputs list is a factual gap in the report narrative; the intelliflo model [P7] includes it, but the suitability report's cashflow section does not list it, creating an unexplained discrepancy between what the model appears to include and what the report discloses as its inputs. The performance chart label swap is a presentation confusion but does not affect the financial figures. The most material issues are: (a) the three different combined pension totals (all self-contradictory); (b) EA5 charges computed on the wrong plan value, making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable and contradicting the narrative saving claim; (c) the People's Pension omission from cashflow inputs. These constitute Potential Concern at High severity as they involve figures that either drive the recommendation (charges comparison) or contradict the report internally on the same figure in two places.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.4, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7). The fact find records household expenditure as £1,718 per month; the report rounds this to 'approximately £1,700 per month' — a minor rounding difference but the report's stated income objective and expenditure figure use the same £1,700 figure, which could obscure that current expenditure already exceeds the stated retirement income target. No explicit priority ranking (1, 2, 3) appears in the fact find or file notes — the priority order is asserted in the report but the supporting documents record objectives as a narrative list without numbered priority. The fact find objective category label is 'Income at retirement' / 'Retirement Planning' rather than a separately enumerated lifestyle-maintenance objective — the first objective (maintain lifestyle) is implicit in the expenditure figure rather than separately stated as a distinct goal in the fact find. 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order

  Potential Concern — severity Low

    Report:   Three client-stated objectives in priority order: (1) Maintain current lifestyle and afford ongoing household expenditure throughout lifetime — current household expenditure approximately £1,700 per month; (2) Retire within two years with a household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) Consolidate pension arrangements and ensure they are appropriately structured for retirement, preferring a streamlined approach rather than maintaining five separate plans with different providers. All three marked as client-stated (isClientStated: true).

    File:     Fact find (B) records under Retirement Planning goals: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The fact find also records calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00 and a regular income target at retirement of £1,700.00. The suitability report itself (I) repeats these objectives verbatim. The cashflow/planning tool (F) shows a retirement event for John at age 71 in 2027 (i.e. approximately 2 years from the advice date of 2025). File note (C) confirms the client's priority is 'to consolidate, get a better handle on his pensions and be ready for retirement with all the options.' The client declaration in the fact find confirms objectives are those identified and prioritised in the document.

    Discrepancies:

      - The fact find records household expenditure as £1,718 per month; the report rounds this to 'approximately £1,700 per month' — a minor rounding difference but the report's stated income objective and expenditure figure use the same £1,700 figure, which could obscure that current expenditure already exceeds the stated retirement income target.

      - No explicit priority ranking (1, 2, 3) appears in the fact find or file notes — the priority order is asserted in the report but the supporting documents record objectives as a narrative list without numbered priority.

      - The fact find objective category label is 'Income at retirement' / 'Retirement Planning' rather than a separately enumerated lifestyle-maintenance objective — the first objective (maintain lifestyle) is implicit in the expenditure figure rather than separately stated as a distinct goal in the fact find.

      - The file note states the main priority is consolidation and being ready for retirement; the report ranks retirement income as priority 2 and consolidation as priority 3, whereas the file note wording could suggest consolidation/readiness is the primary driver discussed.

    All three substantive objectives stated in the report are traceable to the evidence. The retirement income target of £1,700 net per month and the two-year retirement horizon are directly recorded in the fact find (P12) and corroborated by the cashflow tool retirement event (P8). The consolidation objective is recorded in the fact find (P12) and reinforced in the file note (P10). The lifestyle/expenditure objective is supported by the £1,718 monthly expenditure figure in the fact find (P7), and the client declaration (P1) confirms objectives are those identified in the fact find. The rounding of £1,718 to £1,700 is minor and within the adviser-paraphrase guard. The absence of an explicit numbered priority ranking in the fact find is a low-level concern — the report asserts a specific priority order (1, 2, 3) but the fact find records these as a narrative without enumeration. The file note characterises consolidation/retirement readiness as 'the main priority', which sits in mild tension with the report ranking it third; however this is not a material contradiction as all three objectives are recorded and the file note language is not prescriptive about ranking. No vulnerability factors recorded in the evidence appear to affect this check materially. Overall, the objectives are well-evidenced and the report's assertions are substantively supported, with only a low-level concern about the asserted priority order lacking explicit evidential corroboration.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The canonical model records: one objective with a termYears of 2 years; three objectives with timeHorizons of LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm; and two intended retirement ages of 71 (JS) and 66 (KS). Target dates and target ages are absent from the extracted model; the extraction confirms these paths are not present in the document.

    File:     [P10] (intelliflo proposed scenario) records JS retire age 71 in 2027 and KS retire age 66 in 2026, corroborating both retirement ages. [P16] (intelliflo baseline scenario) records the same retirement events. [P11] (risk profile report) records an investment period of 2 years and an investment of £106,000, corroborating the 2-year term. The risk profile document also shows ATR questionnaire produced risk level 4, overridden to 5. The pension switch report [P1] uses a retirement age of 75 for the Aviva plan illustration. [P15] (Zurich policy info) states the Zurich plan has a selected retirement age of 65 and a planned retirement age of 75. [P12] (Aviva policy info) refers to age 75 as the decision deadline. The Defaqto switch report throughout [P1]–[P9] projects values 'at age 75'. [P24] (suitability report) makes references to stress tests and cashflow projections running to age 86 (JS) and 88 (KS) per ONS life expectancy, implying a planning horizon far exceeding 2 years. The cashflow models in [P18]–[P19] run to 2060 (age 104/100), again far exceeding a 2-year horizon.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report records one objective with a term of 2 years, but the switch report and policy illustrations use age 75 as the projection/retirement age throughout, implying a horizon of approximately 6 years from the client's current age of 69 — not 2 years.

      - The risk profile document [P11] records an investment period of 2 years, which matches the canonical model term, but the investment used (£106,000) corresponds to the pension consolidation, whose natural horizon is retirement at age 71 (2 years away) — this is internally consistent for JS, but the switch projections run to age 75, creating a mismatch between the risk profile term and the switch illustration term.

      - The switch report projects maturity values 'at age 75' for all three transferred plans, yet JS's stated retirement age is 71 and the report's investment term is 2 years. A 6-year illustration horizon (to age 75) is inconsistent with both a 2-year term and a retirement target of age 71.

      - The Zurich plan [P15] has a contractually fixed selected retirement age of 65 and a planned retirement age of 75, neither of which aligns with the client's intended retirement at 71.

      - Retirement ages of 71 (JS) and 66 (KS) are corroborated in [P10] and [P16], but KS's retirement age of 66 is not matched in the arithmetic check (the check reports 66 as corroborated, and 71 as unmatched — nearest is 55 at 22.5% away — meaning the file supports 66 but the evidential basis for 71 as a retirement age rests only on the cashflow planning tool, not on any policy document or fact-find extract in the passages).

      - The cashflow model runs to age 104/100 (2060), the suitability report references ONS life expectancy to age 86/88, and the risk profile term is 2 years — no single consistent planning horizon is applied across all documents.

      - Three timeHorizon values (LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm) are asserted in the canonical model but no passage explicitly labels individual objectives with these horizon categories or explains which goal each applies to, meaning client authorship of the priority and horizon assignments is not evidenced.

    Two guards are relevant. First, a pension may have a long investment horizon while the income objective is short — the 2-year term in the risk profile and canonical model aligns with JS retiring at 71 (approximately 2 years from the advice date of September 2025), so the short-term characterisation of the pension consolidation is defensible as the horizon to the income-access event. Second, selected retirement age on a plan is a contractual default, not necessarily the client's intended date — so the Zurich contractual age of 65 and the age-75 Aviva policy deadline do not by themselves contradict the client's intended retirement at 71.

   

    However, these guards do not fully resolve the discrepancies. The switch report consistently projects to age 75 across all three transferred plans. This is 4 years beyond JS's stated retirement target of 71 and 6 years beyond the 2-year investment term used to justify the risk profile at level 5. The third guard directs that the spread of all horizons in the file should be collected before judging: the file contains horizons of 2 years (risk profile), to age 71 (cashflow retirement event), to age 75 (switch illustrations), and to age 86–104 (cashflow life expectancy / end of plan). The switch report's use of age 75 as the projection age is the most material concern: it is the document that provides the critical yield and projected maturity values underpinning the recommendation, and it is running on a longer horizon than either the stated term or the stated retirement age. This inflates projected values and reduces critical yields compared with a correct age-71 projection, which could make the switch appear more favourable than it is. This constitutes a material inconsistency between the term used to justify the risk profile/strategy and the term used in the switch analysis. Additionally, the three timeHorizon labels (LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm) are unattributed to specific goals in the evidence, and no passage documents client agreement to these horizon assignments. The 2-year term in the risk profile is corroborated [P11], and the retirement ages of 71 and 66 are corroborated in the cashflow tool [P10], [P16], but JS's retirement age of 71 is not matched in the passage arithmetic check, suggesting it does not appear explicitly in policy documents — its evidential basis is the planning tool only. On balance, the dominant concern is the switch report using age 75 rather than age 71 as the projection horizon, which directly affects the suitability analysis outputs. Severity is assessed as High: the investment term justifying the risk profile (2 years / age 71) contradicts the term used in the switch analysis (age 75), which is the document supporting the recommendation.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) an emergency fund of £6,000 in liquid cash savings, confirmed sufficient by the client to cover unexpected emergencies; (2) a planned access consideration for pension funds noting that withdrawal or transfer will usually take 10-15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes; and (3) a statement that the client is comfortable with the pension consolidation concept because they have liquid cash should they require instant access funds.

    File:     The fact find (P19) records a cash savings asset of £6,000 dated 22/09/2025. The cashflow planning documents (P1, P5, P13) record 'Savings JS Cash Account £6,000' as a liquid asset in both baseline and proposed scenarios, with liquid assets of £6,000 shown as the opening value throughout the plan. No document in the pack records any ad-hoc access requirement beyond the emergency fund, nor any planned withdrawals other than retirement income. The cashflow model (P24) confirms no shortfall is projected and that state pension income will cover bills in retirement, with the pension allowed to keep growing for large ad-hoc spends. The fact find (P11) records monthly expenditure of £1,718 against net monthly income of £1,430, producing a monthly deficit of £288, which is not addressed in the report's liquidity discussion.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts the emergency fund is 'confirmed sufficient' but does not quantify sufficiency against monthly expenditure. The fact find (P11) shows a monthly household expenditure of £1,718 and a monthly deficit of £288, meaning the £6,000 emergency fund covers approximately 3.5 months of expenditure — this tension is not acknowledged in the report's sufficiency statement.

      - The report's planned access entry refers only to pension administration timescales (10-15 working days) and does not identify any specific planned withdrawal date, amount or purpose beyond eventual retirement — this is a generic process note rather than a specific access requirement.

      - No ad-hoc access requirements beyond the emergency fund are recorded in either the report or the file, which is consistent but notable given the ongoing monthly deficit shown in the fact find.

    The £6,000 emergency fund figure is corroborated consistently across the suitability report, the fact find (P19) and the cashflow planning documents (P1, P5, P13). The client's confirmation of sufficiency is stated in the report and is not directly contradicted by any document. However, the fact find (P11) records a monthly cash flow deficit of £288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), which means the client is already drawing on savings or has other income not captured in the fact find. The report does not acknowledge this deficit when asserting that the emergency fund is sufficient, which is a gap in the liquidity narrative. At £6,000 against £1,718 monthly outgoings, the fund represents roughly 3.5 months of expenditure — a borderline position that the report treats as unproblematic without explanation. The planned access entry is a generic administrative note (10-15 working days) rather than a client-specific access requirement, which is consistent with no specific planned withdrawal being identified. The cashflow model's statement that state pension income will cover bills in retirement and the pension can grow for large ad-hoc spends (P24) provides some supporting context for the liquidity position post-retirement, but the current pre-retirement deficit is not addressed. No vulnerability factors identified in the pack materially alter this assessment. Overall, the core emergency fund figure is evidenced, but the sufficiency assertion is weakly supported given the undisclosed monthly deficit, making this a low-to-moderate concern.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts five foreseeable life changes: (1) John expects to retire within the next two years (estimated ~2027-10-14), ceasing HGV driver and Sea Cadets volunteer income; (2) Kim intends to continue working until age 66 then retire fully, at which point her employment income ceases and State Pension commences; (3) household income target in retirement is £1,700 net per month; (4) from 6 April 2027 unused DC pensions and death benefits will be included in the taxable estate for IHT; (5) minimum pension access age rises from 55 to 57 from 6 April 2028.

    File:     The fact find (category B) records that John 'thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month' and classifies the time horizon as 'Short Term'. The suitability report itself (category I, P6) states 'You expect to retire within the next two years, while Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully.' The cashflow tool proposed scenario (category F, P8) shows timeline events: Kim retires age 66 in 2026 and John retires age 71 in 2027, consistent with the two-year horizon from report date October 2025. The baseline scenario (P1) shows the same retire events. The fact find income section (P18) records Kim's basic income at £1,200/month and State Pension at £230/month. The fact find expenditure section (P19) records calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718, very close to the £1,700 figure cited as the retirement income target. No entry in the fact find or file notes records the IHT legislative change (6 April 2027) or the minimum pension age change (6 April 2028) as client-specific foreseeable changes — these are legislative facts noted in the report.

    Discrepancies:

      - The cashflow proposed scenario (P8) shows Kim retiring at age 66 in 2026, which is one year from the report date (October 2025), not at some future unspecified date when she reaches 66 — her DOB is 07/05/1960 making her currently 65, so age 66 in 2026 is consistent. However, John's retirement event in the cashflow tool is shown as age 71 in 2027 (P8), yet his DOB is 07/06/1956 making him currently 69, meaning age 71 would be 2027 — broadly consistent with 'within two years' but the cashflow models retirement at age 71 not age 70 as might be expected for a ~2-year horizon from a 69-year-old at October 2025.

      - The report's foreseeable changes item 3 (household income target £1,700 net per month) is categorised under foreseeableChanges rather than as a separate objective — it overlaps with the objectives section and the fact find records £1,718 calculated monthly expenditure (P19) versus the £1,700 figure used throughout the report, a minor £18 discrepancy.

      - The IHT legislative change (6 April 2027) and minimum pension access age change (6 April 2028) are recorded in the report as foreseeable changes but are not client-disclosed life events — they are statutory changes applying to everyone. The fact find contains no client-level disclosure of these as personal foreseeable changes.

      - The fact find income changes section (P18) records 'No' to expected income changes in the foreseeable future for both clients, which is in tension with the report's assertion that John's employment income will cease within two years (a material income change). Kim's income changes are similarly not flagged in the fact find income changes field.

    The core foreseeable life changes — John retiring within approximately two years, Kim retiring at age 66, and the £1,700 net per month household income target — are all well-supported by the evidence. The fact find (P9) records these in John's own words; the suitability report (P6) restates them accurately; and the cashflow modelling tool (P8) reflects them in the timeline events (Kim retire age 66 in 2026, John retire age 71 in 2027). The £18 difference between the fact find's calculated expenditure (£1,718, P19) and the £1,700 figure used in the report is immaterial and likely a rounding/simplification. The cashflow retirement age for John being 71 rather than 70 is consistent with a retirement date approximately two years from October 2025 (i.e. late 2027 when John would be 71), so this is not a genuine discrepancy. Regarding the income changes field in the fact find recording 'No' for expected income changes: this is a mild inconsistency, as retirement within two years represents a significant income change, but it is a form-completion gap rather than a substantive contradiction of the life change itself, which is clearly evidenced elsewhere. The IHT legislative change and minimum pension access age change are correctly identified by the guard as legislative changes affecting everyone and are not client-specific foreseeable changes; their presence in the report's foreseeableChanges array is therefore not a suitability concern for this check. No vulnerability factors are recorded in the file that materially alter this assessment — both clients are noted as in good health (P6). Overall the material foreseeable life changes are evidenced and consistent; the minor discrepancies noted do not rise to a material concern.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report records three objectives in priority rank order (1, 2, 3). From the narrative in the report these are: (1) ensure the client can continue to afford their lifestyle through their lifetime; (2) retire in 2 years' time with a household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) obtain a clearer understanding of pension arrangements and ensure they are appropriately structured for retirement, preferring a more streamlined approach. No target dates or target amounts are extracted into the canonical model for the objectives. The investment term used to justify risk and the critical yield analysis runs to age 75 (approximately 6 years from the client's current age of 69). The fund recommendation is described as aligned to 'retirement objectives in isolation as you requested'. The cashflow modelling runs the plan to age 100/104.

    File:     P4 confirms the three objectives as stated in the report. P11 (proposed scenario cashflow) shows the client (JS) retiring at age 71 in 2027 — i.e. in approximately 2 years — and the partner (KS) retiring at age 66 in 2026. P8 (critical yield table) shows projections to age 75 as the maturity point. P9 and P5 (baseline cashflow) show the plan running to age 104/100 with liquid assets and accessible pensions sustained throughout. P3 states the fund recommendation is made 'in line with your stated attitude to investment risk' and references 'retirement objectives in isolation as you requested'. P10 (file note) confirms that the main priority for the client is 'to consolidate, get a better handle on his pensions and be ready for retirement with all the options'. The cashflow modelling (P11, P9, P5) shows no in-year deficits throughout the plan horizon, suggesting the income objective of £1,700 net per month is met within the modelled scenario. No target amounts are evidenced for any objective. There is no evidence of an access need that would conflict with the investment term to age 75.

    Discrepancies:

      - The canonical model does not record any targetDate or targetAmount for any of the three objectives; the extraction confirms these are absent from the report, so no quantified target can be cross-checked against evidence.

      - The report uses age 75 as the maturity/planning point for critical yield purposes (P8), but the cashflow modelling runs to age 100/104 (P9, P5, P11) — these are different time horizons used for different purposes within the same report, though not necessarily conflicting.

      - P11 shows JS retiring at age 71 in 2027 (approximately 2 years from the report date of 2025) and KS retiring at age 66 in 2026, which aligns with the stated objective of retiring 'in 2 years time'; however the critical yield projection uses age 75 as the endpoint, not the stated retirement date — the investment term to age 75 is presented as justifying the risk profile, but the actual access/retirement point is circa age 71, a 4-year difference.

      - No priority conflict is visible between the three ranked objectives in the sense of two objectives claiming the same capital simultaneously; the lifestyle/income and consolidation objectives are complementary rather than competing.

    The three stated objectives are evidenced in the file (P4, P10) and are consistent between the report and the supporting documents. The priority ranking (1, 2, 3) is recorded in the report and no evidence contradicts it. The objectives are not generic boilerplate: P4 records a specific income target (£1,700 net per month) and a specific timeframe ('2 years time'), and P10 records client-specific language about consolidation. The cashflow modelling (P11) corroborates the retirement timing. No structural conflict exists between the objectives in the sense that two objectives claim the same capital or one objective's growth horizon outlasts another's stated access date — the consolidation and income objectives are complementary. However, there is a moderate concern: the critical yield analysis and risk-profile justification both use age 75 as the investment term (P8), whereas the stated retirement objective is in approximately 2 years (age 71 per P11). The report does not explain why age 75 is used as the planning horizon when the client intends to access the fund at retirement in approximately 2 years. This is a potential mismatch between the term used to justify the recommended risk level and the actual stated access horizon. The report states the fund strategy is based on 'retirement objectives in isolation as you requested' (P3) but does not reconcile the age 75 projection endpoint with the age 71 retirement date. This is a moderate concern rather than a high-severity one because: the cashflow modelling does model income withdrawals from retirement (P11, P9), so the broader planning does account for the earlier access; the critical yield to age 75 is used for comparison purposes and may reflect a conservative assumption; and there is no evidence of locked capital or a product feature that would prevent access at age 71. The absence of targetDate and targetAmount fields in the canonical model means a precise term-versus-horizon check cannot be completed. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved evidence that would materially affect this assessment. The recommendation to use Power of Attorney (P1) is a forward-looking planning point, not a current vulnerability driver affecting suitability. CHK-010 assesses vulnerability separately.
