  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report asserts three objectives: OBJ2 (long-term) and OBJ3 (short-term, x2). All five recommendations are linked to OBJ2 and/or OBJ3. The single new plan (NP1 - Aviva Pension Portfolio) is a personal pension with a selected retirement age of 75, accepting a transfer value of £110,185 with no regular contributions. The client prefers Flexi Access Drawdown when benefits are taken. Projections run to age 75 (low: £97,600, mid: £115,000, high: £135,000). The report notes state pension income will cover bills in retirement, allowing the pension to keep growing for large ad hoc spends. Retirement expenditure is expected to be approximately £1,700 per month. The cashflow runs to age 100 and no shortfall is projected. The report also notes the IHT treatment change from April 2027. No emergency fund or liquidity requirement is separately stated. No foreseeable life changes beyond retirement are noted in the recommendations section.

    File:     The fact find (B) records monthly household expenditure of £1,718 and a current monthly disposable income of -£288 (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718), indicating a current monthly shortfall. The policy research note (G) records a retirement income need of £1,700 net per month. The intelliflo cashflow baseline (F) shows employment income of £16,640 (client) and £13,200 (combined) with total regular expenditure of £24,001 in year one. The proposed scenario cashflow (F) confirms the plan runs to age 100, no shortfall projected, state pension covers bills in retirement, pension allowed to grow for large ad hoc spends. The Zurich policy info (E) confirms retirement date 7 June 2031 (age 75). The Aviva policy info (E) confirms drawdown is available from age 55 (57 from April 2028) and the plan must remain in pension until minimum pension age; income drawdown not possible after age 75 but funds can remain invested post-75 in drawdown. The People's Pension (E) confirms small pot access rules and FAD availability. The suitability report (I) links fund selection to 'retirement objectives in isolation as you requested' and states the cashflow uses a retirement age of 75. The critical yield table uses retirement age 75 throughout. No evidence of a separately documented emergency fund or liquidity reserve. No foreseeable life changes other than retirement are disclosed in any document.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report assigns OBJ3 as 'ShortTerm' but all five recommendations are a long-term pension consolidation; no recommendation explicitly addresses a short-term objective in a way that is distinguishable from the long-term pension goal, raising a question about what OBJ3 concretely is and how a pension consolidation serves it in the short term.

      - The current monthly cash flow shows a deficit of -£288/month (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718) yet the report does not address how near-term living costs are met or whether an emergency fund exists; no liquidity provision is identified in the recommendations.

      - The report states retirement expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month but the fact find records current monthly expenditure of £1,718 and current monthly income of £1,430, meaning the client is already spending more than they earn; the report does not reconcile this gap or address how the shortfall is currently funded.

      - The cashflow omits the People's Pension (£6,601.55, retained) from the listed inputs (only Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002 are listed), which may understate projected retirement assets, though the People's Pension is retained rather than transferred.

      - The mid growth rate is stated as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative of the suitability report but 2.95% in the critical yield table (page 18) and in the pension switch report; this is a minor internal inconsistency already noted in the extraction.

      - The report presents the recommendation as serving OBJ3 (short-term) without specifying what that short-term objective is or explaining how a long-term pension consolidation with a retirement age of 75 meets a short-term need.

    The core appropriateness assessment can be performed. The recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio is a personal pension consolidating three existing pensions with a retirement age of 75, projections to age 75, and Flexi Access Drawdown as the intended decumulation method. The evidence (Zurich illustration, cashflow model, suitability report) consistently supports a retirement age of 75 and the FAD access mechanism. For a client aged 69 (born June 1956), the pension is already past the minimum pension age, so there is no access restriction that conflicts with the stated goal horizon — the guard regarding access mechanism applies here and no locked-capital concern arises. The state pension and cashflow evidence support the assertion that retirement income needs are broadly met, and the cashflow modelling (to age 100, no shortfall) corroborates the long-term planning horizon. However, two concerns remain after applying the guards. First, the linkage of all five recommendations to OBJ3 (short-term) is unexplained. The report does not define OBJ3 with sufficient specificity to assess whether a long-term pension consolidation genuinely serves a short-term objective, or whether this is boilerplate linkage. The guard requires a specific objective to be named; OBJ3's short-term classification against a pension consolidation is not evidenced. Second, the current monthly cash flow deficit (-£288/month) is not addressed anywhere in the recommendations. The fact find evidences that the client is currently spending £288/month more than their income, yet no emergency fund, liquidity buffer or near-term access plan is mentioned. The report's assertion that state pension will cover bills in retirement does not address the present shortfall. This is a material gap: access needs and foreseeable near-term financial pressure are not addressed. The People's Pension omission from the cashflow inputs is noted but does not materially change the no-shortfall conclusion given its small value (£6,601). The 2.94%/2.95% discrepancy is immaterial. No vulnerability characteristics are evidenced in the pack that would further affect this assessment. Overall, the recommendations are broadly appropriate for the long-term retirement objective, but the short-term objective linkage is unsupported and the current cash flow deficit and absence of any liquidity/emergency fund provision represent a material gap in the suitability analysis.

  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts three client-stated objectives: (1) maintain current lifestyle and afford ongoing household expenditure throughout lifetime; (2) retire within two years with a household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) consolidate pension arrangements and ensure they are appropriately structured for retirement. Planning assumptions include: cashflow runs to age 100, all surplus income spent (0% saved), general expenditure inflation 2%, savings inflation 2%, state pensions inflation 2.5%, Aviva personal pensions growth 5%, retirement age used in critical yield 75, low/mid/high growth rates 0.00%/2.95%/5.88%, high inflation stress test 6%, market crash stress test -30%, cashflow input John HGV income £300 per week.

    File:     The fact find (B) records the client's retirement planning goal as: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' The fact find also records a Regular Income (Today's Money) target of £1,700.00 and a time horizon of Short Term. The file notes (C) confirm the client is in receipt of a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, continues to work as an HGV driver earning around £300 per week net, and receives an additional £20 per week from the Sea Cadets. The fact find records calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00 and a current monthly disposable income of -£288.00. An emergency fund / cash savings of £6,000 is recorded. The intelliflo planning proposed scenario (F) is present in the file. The critical yield retirement age assumption of 75 is stated only in the suitability report; no supporting document in the pack corroborates why 75 was used as the crystallisation age when the stated goal is retirement in approximately 2 years (i.e. around age 69-70 given DOB 07/06/1956 and fact find completed 19/09/2025). The policy research notes (G) confirm consolidation as the primary client priority: 'The main priority for the client is to consolidate, get a better handle on his pensions and be ready for retirement with all the options.'

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report uses retirement age 75 in the critical yield calculation, but the stated client goal is to retire within 2 years (i.e. approximately age 69-70 based on DOB 07/06/1956 and meeting date 18/09/2025). No document in the pack explains or supports the use of age 75 as the retirement age assumption in the critical yield.

      - The cashflow modelling assumption uses a pension growth rate of 5% for the Aviva personal pensions, but the mid growth rate used in the critical yield is 2.95% (or 2.94% per the page 11 narrative). These are different rates applied in different tools within the same report with no reconciliation or explanation evidenced in the file.

      - The mid growth rate is stated as 2.95% in the critical yield section (page 18) but as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative — a minor internal inconsistency within the report itself, noted but not directly contradicted by the file evidence.

      - The 0% surplus savings assumption is asserted in the report but no file note or fact find entry is present in the pack confirming the client explicitly agreed to this assumption being applied in the cashflow model.

      - The policy research suitability matrix (G) leaves blank the field 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' for each of the Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows and Standard Life recommendations, meaning no link between the individual recommendations and the stated objectives is documented in the pre-advice research file.

    Three of the three stated objectives are substantively evidenced. Objective 2 (retire in 2 years, £1,700 net per month household income) is directly and explicitly corroborated in the fact find [P5] and the file notes [P1]. Objective 3 (consolidate pensions) is corroborated in [P5] and [P6]. Objective 1 (maintain lifestyle/afford household expenditure) is inferentially supported by the fact find expenditure data [P8] and the file notes [P1], and is consistent with the cashflow-to-age-100 approach. The guard that an objective inferred and confirmed at meeting is 'stated' applies, and the guard that a report may summarise several recorded needs as one objective applies to objective 1. These three objectives are adequately evidenced.

   

    However, a material planning assumption presents a genuine concern: the critical yield calculation uses a retirement age of 75, yet the client's stated and evidenced goal is retirement within 2 years (approximately age 69-70). No document in the pack explains why a retirement age of 75 was used — it is not drawn from a provider illustration, it is not recorded as a client preference, and it conflicts with the stated short-term horizon. This is not a false-positive explicable by any of the stated guards. Using age 75 in the critical yield when the plan is to retire at ~69-70 materially understates the critical yield required (more years of growth are assumed, reducing the hurdle), potentially making the recommendation appear more favourable than it is relative to the actual planning horizon. This is a material concern under the check's own severity guidance (investment term justifying the risk profile contradicts the stated goal horizon — High severity).

   

    The 0% surplus savings assumption is a cashflow modelling input; while explicit client confirmation is not evidenced in the file, this is a relatively standard modelling convention and the absence of a separate confirmation note, while imperfect, is a lower-order concern. The link between individual recommendations and specific objectives is absent from the pre-advice research notes [P23], which is consistent with moderate generic boilerplate concern but the suitability report itself does link recommendations to objectives narratively.

   

    No vulnerability indicators are noted in the available evidence that would materially alter this assessment.

   

    The dominant finding is the retirement age of 75 used in the critical yield, which is unsupported by and conflicts with the evidenced planning horizon of approximately 2 years to retirement.

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G3.1, G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.7, G3.8, G3.9, G3.10). The questionnaire result is 4 but the agreed rating adopted is 5 — an upward override of one point. The risk profile report's 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss' discussion sections record 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning the formal risk profiling tool carries no written justification for the override beyond the tool's standard agreed-level output. The justification exists in the suitability report narrative (category I) and the client's verbal acknowledgement is recorded there, but it is absent from the risk profile document itself. The risk profile report (D) uses a 2-year investment period and a £106,000 investment amount in its projections, whereas the suitability report and cashflow model reference a 10-year investment horizon and a transfer value of approximately £110,185. The projection inputs in the risk profile tool therefore differ from the recommendation parameters. The report's CFL section states pension funds total 'approximately £106,000'; the arithmetic sum of the five individual plan values stated in the report itself is £116,998.47, and the cashflow model in [P1] and [P2] records pension assets of £116,997 — a discrepancy of approximately £11,000. 1 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G3.1] Risk rating and summary

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report states the ATR questionnaire produced a score of 4 (Cautious Balanced), but the agreed/adopted rating is 5 (Balanced) on an implied numerical 1-10 scale. The adviser overrode the questionnaire result upward because it 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite, and the client agreed to proceed at risk rating 5.

    File:     The risk profile report (category D) confirms the natural/questionnaire risk level is 4 (Cautious Balanced) and the agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced). The SIPP research report (category G) records the agreed risk level as 5 (Balanced). The suitability report itself (category I) confirms rating 5 – Balanced with an explicit client acknowledgement. The pension switch report (category G) also records the agreed risk level as 5. Both recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc and CT Universal MAP Balanced C) carry an individual risk rating of 5 in the suitability report. The risk profile report notes no additional justification text in the 'Agreed risk level' section beyond what the tool recorded. The suitability report records the client's acknowledgement that rating 5 is higher than the questionnaire result and that they are happy to accept this.

    Discrepancies:

      - The questionnaire result is 4 but the agreed rating adopted is 5 — an upward override of one point. The risk profile report's 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss' discussion sections record 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning the formal risk profiling tool carries no written justification for the override beyond the tool's standard agreed-level output. The justification exists in the suitability report narrative (category I) and the client's verbal acknowledgement is recorded there, but it is absent from the risk profile document itself.

      - The risk profile report (D) uses a 2-year investment period and a £106,000 investment amount in its projections, whereas the suitability report and cashflow model reference a 10-year investment horizon and a transfer value of approximately £110,185. The projection inputs in the risk profile tool therefore differ from the recommendation parameters.

    The agreed rating of 5 (Balanced) is corroborated by the risk profile report [P1, P9], the SIPP research report [P2, P6, P8], the pension switch report [P5], and the suitability report [P4, P10]. The questionnaire result of 4 is corroborated by [P1] and [P17]. Both sit on the same implied 1-10 numerical scale, so the guard against cross-scale comparison does not apply. The upward override from 4 to 5 is an adviser adjustment. Per the decision logic, a questionnaire result overridden upward requires evidenced justification and client acknowledgement. The suitability report [P4] records the client's explicit acknowledgement and the rationale (questionnaire did not fully reflect risk appetite; client not reliant on pension funds; seeks best growth potential over 10 years; balanced approach agreed). The client's acknowledgement is therefore present in the suitability report. However, the risk profile document (category D) records 'No additional notes were recorded' in both the 'Agreed risk level' and 'Capacity for loss for the goal' discussion sections [P13, P17], meaning the override rationale is not captured in the formal risk profiling tool — only in the suitability report narrative. The recommended funds each carry a risk rating of 5 [P10], matching the agreed rating. The recommended funds are described as 'risk-profiled solutions' designed to remain within the agreed risk level. The KIID for one of the recommended funds (CT Universal MAP) shows a SRRI of 4 [P18, P20], which is one point below the agreed rating of 5; however, this is a different scale (SRRI 1-7 per KIID regulation) and the guard against cross-scale comparison applies — a SRRI 4 on a 1-7 KIID scale is not directly comparable to a 4 or 5 on the adviser's 1-10 scale, so this does not constitute a mismatch. The discrepancy regarding the risk profile tool's investment period (2 years, £106,000) versus the actual recommendation horizon (10 years, ~£110,185) suggests the projections shown to the client in the risk profiling tool may not have reflected the actual investment parameters, which is a moderate concern regarding the robustness of the agreed-level discussion. Overall, the agreed rating is evidenced and the override rationale and client acknowledgement are present in the suitability report, but the absence of any supporting notes in the risk profile tool itself means the file-level evidencing of the override is thin. The investment period discrepancy in the risk profile tool adds to the concern. Severity is assessed as Moderate: the override rationale exists in the suitability report but is unsupported by the formal risk profiling document, and the projection inputs used at the point of agreement differ from the recommendation.

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report rates the client's capacity for loss as 'Medium'. The assessment basis stated in the report is: (1) John receives a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, earns approximately £300 per week net as an HGV driver, and receives £20 per week from the Sea Cadets; (2) Kim contributes £1,100 per month from employment and will also receive a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week; (3) combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month, with savings of around £6,000 and no outstanding liabilities; (4) pension funds totalling approximately £106,000 form the basis of retirement provision; (5) the client confirmed any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living. The CFL is stated as separately assessed from ATR.

    File:     The risk profile report (category D) contains a 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' section but records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both the capacity for loss questionnaire responses section and the 'Capacity for loss for the goal' section. The CFL questionnaire responses themselves are present in [P10] (partial) and [P20], but the notes fields are blank. The agreed risk level section in [P17] records 'No additional notes were recorded'. The risk profile report records a natural ATR of 4 (Cautious Balanced) and an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced), but no written justification is recorded in the tool for the override. The investment amount used in the risk profile projections is £106,000 [P17], consistent with the report's CFL section figure. The suitability report narrative in [P11] contains the same income/expenditure/savings detail as the canonical model. The cashflow modelling in [P1] and [P2] shows liquid assets of £6,000 and total pensions of £116,997 (baseline) / £116,997 (proposed), not £106,000. The arithmetic sum of all five existing pension plans is £116,998.47, not £106,000 as stated in the CFL section.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report's CFL section states pension funds total 'approximately £106,000'; the arithmetic sum of the five individual plan values stated in the report itself is £116,998.47, and the cashflow model in [P1] and [P2] records pension assets of £116,997 — a discrepancy of approximately £11,000.

      - The risk profile tool's capacity for loss section records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both the CFL questionnaire notes and the capacity for loss for the goal notes [P20], meaning no written rationale or CFL rating ('Medium') is documented in the risk profile tool itself to corroborate the report's 'Medium' CFL label.

      - The report asserts the CFL is separately assessed from ATR, yet the risk profile tool contains no documented CFL outcome or rating — the CFL assessment as a standalone conclusion is absent from the supporting file.

      - The ATR questionnaire result of 4 was overridden to 5 without any justification recorded in the risk profile tool ('No additional notes were recorded' in agreed risk level section [P14]), though the suitability report does provide a narrative explanation [P11].

    Several issues arise on this check. First, the most significant concern is the pension fund total quoted in the CFL section. The report states 'pension funds totalling approximately £106,000' as part of the capacity for loss rationale. The derived figure from the report's own individual plan values is £116,998.47, and the cashflow model corroborates approximately £116,997. The figure of £106,000 appears to be the value used in the risk profile tool projections [P17], which represents only the three pensions recommended for transfer (£3,744.36 + £103,439.24 + £3,002 = £110,185.60) or possibly an earlier/rounded figure. Regardless, £106,000 is materially inconsistent with both the arithmetic sum of all five plans and the cashflow model — and the CFL section purports to describe the client's total retirement provision, so understating it by approximately £11,000 is a substantive inaccuracy in the assessment rationale. Second, the risk profile tool's CFL section contains no recorded notes, no documented CFL rating and no stated basis for arriving at 'Medium' [P20]. The income, expenditure and savings detail supporting the CFL is present only in the suitability report narrative, not in the risk profile document. While the report does provide financial reasoning (income, expenditure, surplus, savings, client confirmation) that goes beyond a mere restatement of ATR, the absence of any corroborating CFL documentation in the risk profile tool means the 'Medium' label is unsupported by category D evidence. Third, the ATR override from 4 to 5 is explained in the suitability report [P11] and the client's acknowledgement is recorded there, but the risk profile tool itself records 'No additional notes were recorded' in the agreed risk level section [P14], which weakens the evidential trail for the override. The guard against treating CFL as a restatement of ATR does not apply here because the report does provide independent financial reasoning; however, the absence of a documented CFL outcome in the risk profile tool is itself a concern. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. Overall, the pension fund total discrepancy in the CFL rationale (£106,000 vs £116,998.47) and the absence of any CFL rating or notes in the risk profile tool constitute material concerns.

  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk

  Potential Concern — severity Low

    Report:   The report states that the client is aware that funds may go up as well as down, and that the higher risk taken, the greater the potential for larger drops in value. No specific maximum acceptable loss percentage or monetary amount is stated. The canonical model confirms no quantified tolerance for loss figure is present in the report. The need-level risk path (/objectives[]/needLevelRisk) is absent from the extracted model.

    File:     The risk profile report (category D) records a questionnaire natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) and an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced), with no additional notes recorded in the capacity for loss questionnaire section or the agreed risk level discussion section. The SIPP research report (category G) confirms the agreed risk level is 5. The suitability report (category I, [P18]) states the client is not reliant on pension funds, wishes best growth potential over a 10-year period, and confirmed any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living. A stress test in the suitability report ([P23]) models an immediate market crash of -30% combined with high inflation (6%) and states there would be no shortfall. No quantified maximum acceptable loss figure or tolerance for loss percentage is recorded in any document. The risk profile report notes section for capacity for loss questionnaire discussion records 'No additional notes were recorded' [P24].

    Discrepancies:

      - No specific tolerance for loss figure (percentage or monetary amount) is quantified anywhere in the report or in any supporting document, despite the stress test modelling a -30% immediate loss scenario.

      - The need-level risk path (/objectives[]/needLevelRisk) is absent from the canonical model — it is unclear whether the report is silent on this or whether extraction failed; no passage in the file records a need-level risk assessment.

      - The risk profile report capacity for loss questionnaire discussion section records 'No additional notes were recorded' [P24], meaning there is no documented adviser narrative explaining how the capacity for loss assessment informed the tolerance for loss conclusion.

      - The stress test models a -30% market crash but the report does not translate this into a stated client tolerance for loss in percentage or monetary terms, leaving the link between the stress scenario and the agreed risk level unevidenced.

    The report contains only a generic market risk awareness statement ('funds may go up as well as down') without quantifying a tolerance for loss. This is a documentation gap rather than a contradiction between the report and the file — neither source quantifies it. The guard acknowledges that many firms do not separately quantify tolerance for loss, so absence alone is not automatically a contradiction. However, the report does model a large-loss scenario (-30% immediate crash, [P23]) without anchoring it to a stated client tolerance, and the capacity for loss questionnaire discussion section in the risk profile report ([P24]) records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning the adviser's reasoning connecting the capacity for loss assessment to the agreed tolerance is undocumented. The need-level risk path is absent from both the canonical model and the file; whether this represents report silence or extraction failure cannot be determined from the pack, so it is recorded as a missing input. No vulnerability factors are identified in the file that would alter this assessment. The overall finding is a low-severity documentation gap: tolerance for loss is not quantified where a large-loss stress scenario has been modelled, and the capacity for loss discussion section contains no adviser notes to support the tolerance conclusion.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The canonical model records an investment term of 10 years (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears = 10). The objectives array records time horizons of LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm, indicating mixed horizons across objectives. The agreed risk rating is 5 (Balanced), overriding a questionnaire score of 4.

    File:     The risk profile report (P3) shows the Investment Period used in the risk profiling tool as 2 years, with an investment of £106,000. The agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced) as confirmed in both the risk profile report (P2, P3) and the SIPP research report (P10). The SIPP research report (P9, P10) records the agreed risk level as 5. The pension switch report (P12) shows a retirement age of 75 and time to retirement of 5 years and 6 months for Standard Life. The cashflow modelling (P5) shows retirement events in 2026 (age 70 for JS) and 2027 (age 71 for KS). The figure 10 is corroborated in P9 (table of contents reference to page 10), but this is a page number, not a time horizon figure. No passage explicitly states a 10-year investment term as the agreed horizon used to support the risk profile.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report asserts an investment term of 10 years, but the risk profile report (P3) records the Investment Period used in the risk profiling projection as 2 years — a direct conflict between the term the report states and the term entered into the risk profiling tool.

      - The objectives array records mixed time horizons (LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm), yet only a single 10-year figure appears in the risk assessment section, with no reconciliation of how the short-term objectives align with a 10-year investment term used to support a risk rating of 5.

      - The pension switch report (P12) records time to retirement for Standard Life as 5 years and 6 months (retirement age 75), which is inconsistent with a stated 10-year investment term used to justify the risk profile.

      - The cashflow model (P5) projects retirement for JS in 2026 (age 70, approximately 1 year away) and KS in 2027 (age 71, approximately 2 years away), which is inconsistent with a 10-year investment horizon being used to support a higher risk rating of 5.

      - The figure 10 corroborated in P9 appears to be a page number reference in the table of contents, not a stated investment term, so it does not substantiate the 10-year horizon claim.

      - The agreed risk rating of 5 overrides a questionnaire result of 4; the report records the Investment Period for the risk profiling as 2 years, raising the question of whether a longer (10-year) horizon was the undocumented justification for the upward override — yet no such justification appears in the risk profile notes (P1: 'No additional notes were recorded' for both Investment target and Agreed risk level sections).

    The suitability report asserts a 10-year investment term at /riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears. The risk profile tool (P3) records the Investment Period as 2 years. These are directly contradictory. The cashflow model (P5) places retirement for the primary client (JS) in 2026, approximately 1 year from the report date of September 2025, and for KS in 2027 — both consistent with the 2-year period in the risk profiling tool, not with a 10-year horizon. The pension switch report (P12) records time to retirement as 5 years and 6 months for Standard Life. None of these evidence sources supports a 10-year term. The figure '10' that the RangeMatch check flags as corroborated in P9 is a page number in the table of contents ('Individual cumulative investment performance table 10'), not a time horizon — this corroboration is therefore spurious and cannot substantiate the report's assertion. The guard notes that money left invested through retirement can legitimately carry a longer horizon than the retirement date itself, but the report must say so. There is no such statement in the report or evidence pack. The risk profile notes fields for Investment target and Agreed risk level both record 'No additional notes were recorded' (P1), meaning there is no documented explanation for how a 10-year term was arrived at, nor how it reconciles with the 2-year projection period used in the risk tool or the imminent retirement dates. The agreed risk rating of 5 overrides a questionnaire score of 4. The upward override is recorded in the ambiguities section of the extraction, and the report states the adviser felt the questionnaire did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite. If the 10-year horizon was intended to support this override, it is not evidenced in the risk profile report. The combination of: (a) a 10-year term asserted in the report with no supporting evidence; (b) the risk profiling tool using 2 years; (c) retirement projected within 1-2 years in the cashflow model; (d) no adviser notes explaining how the longer horizon was agreed; and (e) an upward ATR override with no documented rationale — constitutes a material concern. The severity is assessed as High because the unsupported longer horizon appears to be the implicit justification for a higher risk profile, and the questionnaire result was overridden upward with the only potentially supporting factor (a long horizon) being contradicted by the evidence on file.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   Target risk rating 5. Two funds recommended: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 5, OCF 0.18%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk rating 5, OCF 0.29%). Strategy type: BespokePortfolio. Total allocation 100%. Asset allocation breakdown not extracted from the report.

    File:     The risk profile report (D) records a natural ATR of 4 (Cautious Balanced) from the psychometric questionnaire, but an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) following adviser discussion. The SIPP research report (G) confirms the agreed risk level is 5 and states 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' The research report lists the two funds at 50%/50%. The suitability report fund table shows both funds at risk rating 5, OCF 0.18% and 0.29% respectively. The HSBC fund factsheet (H) describes the fund's own internal risk profile as rated 3 on a 1–5 HSBC scale. The KIID for the HSBC fund (H) classifies it in SRRI category 4 (medium fluctuations). No KIID or factsheet in the pack assigns the HSBC fund a risk rating of 5 on any scale. The CT Universal MAP Balanced fund KIID (H) also shows SRRI category 4. The research report (G) shortlist confirms both funds are mapped to risk level 5 within the Defaqto/firm framework.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report assigns both funds a risk rating of 5. The HSBC fund factsheet (H) states the fund's own risk profile is rated 3 on the HSBC 1–5 scale. The KIID (H) places it in SRRI category 4. Neither the factsheet nor the KIID assigns it a rating of 5 on any provider scale.

      - The CT Universal MAP Balanced KIID (H) shows SRRI category 4, not 5, at the fund level — yet the report assigns it a risk rating of 5.

      - The questionnaire-derived natural ATR is 4 (Cautious Balanced) but the agreed/override rating used for the strategy is 5 (Balanced). The report records an upward override.

      - Asset allocation breakdown is absent from the extracted model; the report does not state the underlying asset class split explicitly, so alignment of asset allocation with risk level 5 cannot be directly verified.

    The core comparison — whether the strategy risk rating (5) matches the client's agreed risk rating (5) — is fully supported. The risk profile report [P22] records the agreed level as 5 Balanced, and the SIPP research report [P16] explicitly confirms 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' The 50/50 fund split totals 100% and is corroborated by both [P8] and [P11].

   

    The discrepancies between the fund-level risk ratings stated in the report (both 5) and the provider's own factsheet/KIID ratings (HSBC: HSBC-scale 3; SRRI 4; CT: SRRI 4) are explained by the guard: fund-level ratings from a provider are not necessarily on the firm's client risk scale. The Defaqto research framework maps these funds to risk level 5 within the firm's own scale, which is the scale used throughout the advice process. The research report [P14] shortlist shows both funds appearing in the risk level 5 cohort, and [P16] confirms the portfolio mapping. This is sufficient to explain the apparent mismatch between fund-factsheet ratings and the firm's rating of 5.

   

    The upward override from ATR 4 to agreed level 5 is recorded in [P22] and is explained there: the adviser discussed projections with the client and adjusted the risk level through an interactive process, resulting in an agreed level of 5. The report does not appear to assert that the questionnaire produced a score of 5 — the ambiguity is noted in the extraction but the override is documented in the risk profile report with a client-agreed outcome. This is a potential concern in its own right (upward override) but falls within CHK-003's scope; however, the override is documented and the client agreed it, mitigating the concern to moderate rather than high severity.

   

    The absence of an explicit asset allocation breakdown in the extracted model means direct verification of asset class splits against a risk-level-5 model cannot be performed. The KIID for the HSBC fund [P2] states the asset allocation under typical conditions as Equities 45–70%, Bonds 20–55%, Other 0–25%, which is consistent with a balanced/medium risk profile. No contradicting asset allocation is in evidence.

   

    Overall, the material elements of the strategy are supported. The chief residual concern is the upward override from questionnaire score 4 to agreed level 5: while documented, it is an upward adjustment and warrants flagging at Moderate severity.

  [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the client completed an ATR questionnaire producing a risk rating of 4 (Cautious Balanced), but the adviser felt this did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite. The agreed rating was increased to 5 (Balanced). The justification given is that the client confirmed they are not reliant upon their pension funds and wish for the best growth potential over a 10-year period, feeling a balanced approach would achieve this goal. The client acknowledged the override, confirmed they wished to increase their risk rating to 5 – Balanced investor, and stated they were aware this is higher than their questionnaire result and were happy to take on this risk with their pension.

    File:     The risk profile report (category D) records a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) from the ATR questionnaire completed on 18/09/2025, and an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced), consistent with the suitability report. The SIPP research report (category G) also records the agreed risk level as 5 (Balanced). However, the file note (category C, [P11]) contains a draft ATR wording section that states: 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire. You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6.' This is internally inconsistent within the file note itself — it records both 5 and 6 as the agreed/happy-to-proceed rating. The capacity for loss sections in the risk profile report record no additional notes in the relevant discussion fields. The ATR questionnaire responses in the risk profile report show cautious answers (e.g. 'Cautious' investment approach, 'Caution' association with risk, 'More cautious' self-description), which is consistent with the questionnaire yielding a 4 rather than a 5.

    Discrepancies:

      - The file note [P11] states 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6' in the same paragraph that records the agreed rating as 5, creating an internal contradiction within the supporting file — the suitability report states the agreed rating is 5 throughout, not 6.

      - The risk profile report [P1], [P3], [P14] records 'No additional notes were recorded' in the sections for Agreed risk level discussion, Capacity for loss questionnaire, and Capacity for loss for the goal — meaning the override conversation and capacity for loss rationale are not documented with any narrative in the risk profile tool itself, only the agreed level outcome is recorded.

      - The ATR questionnaire responses in [P14] consistently show cautious answers, yet the override moves the rating upward by one band with the justification being a growth objective and non-reliance on pension funds — no passage explicitly links the questionnaire responses to a documented discussion explaining why those responses understated the client's risk appetite.

    The suitability report's core override narrative — questionnaire produced 4, agreed rating is 5, client acknowledged and accepted the higher rating — is corroborated by the risk profile report [P2], [P6] and the SIPP research report [P16], [P20], both of which record the agreed level as 5 Balanced. The client acknowledgement language in the suitability report [P4] mirrors the file note [P11] almost exactly, confirming the conversation was recorded somewhere in the file. However, there are two concerns. First, [P11] (the file note) contains a material internal contradiction: within the same ATR wording block it states 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6' — a figure that appears nowhere in the suitability report or risk profile tool. This is not explicable as a formatting artefact and raises a question about whether the agreed rating was at some point 6 rather than 5, or whether a drafting error was carried into the file note without correction. The suitability report consistently states 5, and both the risk profile tool and SIPP research report record 5, so on balance the agreed rating is 5; but the unexplained '6' in the file note is a discrepancy that cannot be dismissed without explanation. Second, the risk profile tool records 'No additional notes were recorded' in every narrative section relevant to the override (agreed risk level discussion, capacity for loss questionnaire, capacity for loss for the goal). The justification for the upward override therefore rests entirely on narrative in the suitability report and file note, with no contemporaneous notes in the risk profiling tool itself. The override justification offered — non-reliance on pensions and a 10-year growth objective — is plausible but generic; it does not engage with the specifically cautious questionnaire answers (e.g. 'Cautious' investment approach, 'More cautious' self-description, discomfort at losses over £4,000 on £20,000). The absence of documented discussion notes in the risk profile tool, combined with the unexplained reference to rating 6 in the file note, means the override is not fully and cleanly evidenced. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability assessment outcome is recorded for either client (extraction confirms absence), so no material vulnerability consideration affects this finding. The finding is a Potential Concern at moderate severity: the override direction and client acknowledgement are recorded, but the file note contradiction (6 vs 5) and absence of discussion notes in the risk profiling tool are material gaps that require clarification.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) The agreed ATR risk rating is 5 (overridden upward from questionnaire result of 4), described as 'Balanced'. (2) The target risk rating for the investment strategy is 5. (3) Capacity for loss is rated 'Medium'. (4) The client confirmed he is not reliant on pension funds and wishes for best growth potential over a 10-year investment term. (5) Existing arrangements carry provider-scale risk ratings (9, 4, 2, 7, and 4 on Standard Life scale) that are explicitly flagged as not comparable to the client scale. (6) The questionnaire produced a rating of 4 but the adviser felt it 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite; the client agreed to proceed at 5 and acknowledged this is higher than the questionnaire result.

    File:     [P3] (Risk profile report, category D) confirms the natural/questionnaire ATR result was 4 ('Cautious Balanced'). [P3] and [P2] confirm the agreed risk level was 5 ('Balanced') following interactive discussion considering ATR, capacity for loss and required return. [P5] (Suitability Report, category I) states: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire. You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5.' The client acknowledged the higher risk and confirmed he is not reliant on pension funds over a 10-year period. [P1] and [P2] (SIPP research report, category G) confirm the agreed risk level is 5 and the selected funds are CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%), both mapped to risk level 5 Balanced. [P7] and [P18] (Pension switch reports, category G) confirm 'The client's agreed risk level is 5' for each switch. [P24] (Risk profile report appendix, category D) records the ATR questionnaire responses, showing cautious-leaning answers that produced the natural level of 4. The capacity for loss section in [P24] notes 'No additional notes were recorded' beyond the standard fields. [P5] provides the capacity for loss narrative: client aware values can rise and fall, confirmed underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living, supported by State Pension (~£230/week), employment income (~£300/week net), Sea Cadets income (~£20/week), and partner's income (~£1,100/month).

    Discrepancies:

      - The ATR questionnaire produced a natural risk level of 4, but the agreed/strategy risk rating is 5. The upward override is documented and the client acknowledged it, but it represents a departure from the questionnaire result without a fully evidenced independent justification beyond the adviser's view that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite.

      - The capacity for loss section in the risk profile report ([P24]) records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both the capacity for loss discussion and other notes fields, meaning the CFL rationale is only evidenced in the suitability report narrative ([P5]) and not independently corroborated by a standalone CFL assessment document.

      - The risk rating 7 attributed to one existing arrangement on the provider scale is not matched by any passage in the evidence pack (nearest figure is 7.12 in [P10] which is a volatility statistic, not a risk rating).

      - The capacity for loss is stated as 'Medium' in the canonical model but the risk profile report and suitability report do not use this label explicitly — the CFL is described narratively rather than assigned a formal rating label in the supporting documents.

    The core comparison — whether the strategy risk rating of 5 equals the agreed ATR rating of 5 — is satisfied: both the suitability report and the supporting documents (risk profile report [P3], SIPP research report [P1]/[P2], pension switch reports [P7]/[P18]) confirm the agreed level is 5 and the recommended funds are mapped to that level. The strategy does not exceed the agreed risk rating.

   

    However, the upward override from questionnaire result 4 to agreed rating 5 is a material concern under the decision logic ('questionnaire result overridden upward with no evidenced justification'). The justification given — that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite — is a bare assertion by the adviser with no independent supporting evidence. The risk profile report ([P3]) records the questionnaire result as 4 and the agreed level as 5, and confirms the override, but the override rationale in the supporting documents adds no more than a footnote referencing the interactive discussion and the client's agreement. The client's acknowledgement is documented in [P5], which is some mitigation, but the absence of any objective or documented basis for concluding the questionnaire underestimated the client's actual risk appetite means the override is not fully evidenced.

   

    The capacity for loss: the suitability report narrative ([P5]) provides a reasonable qualitative basis — diversified income sources, no reliance on pension funds, 10-year horizon. However, the risk profile document ([P24]) records no additional CFL notes, meaning the CFL assessment is self-contained within the suitability report and not independently corroborated. There is a moderate concern that the CFL is primarily a restatement of the client's income resilience rather than a stress-tested assessment of loss tolerance, and the 'Medium' label is only in the canonical model, not in any supporting document.

   

    The existing arrangements' risk ratings are all flagged as provider-scale and not comparable to the client scale, which is consistent with the guard on incomparable scales. No alignment claim is made for the existing arrangements, so no finding arises from the incomparability itself.

   

    The risk rating of 7 for one existing arrangement has no corroborating passage; while this is a factual gap, the existing arrangements are not the subject of the strategy alignment check and their ratings are explicitly marked as non-comparable.

   

    Vulnerability overlay: the canonical model flags that vulnerability assessment outcomes for both clients are absent from the documents (expectedButAbsent paths). No vulnerability is noted in the retrieved passages as bearing on the risk, CFL or strategy assessment, so no additional concern arises here for this check.

   

    On balance, the primary concern is the upward ATR override from 4 to 5 with limited evidenced justification beyond the adviser's assertion and the client's agreement. This is a High severity concern per the decision logic.

  [G3.8] Conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels across the file

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that the ATR questionnaire produced a score of 4 (labelled 'Cautious Balanced' on the questionnaire scale), but the agreed/override rating is 5 (labelled 'Balanced', on a 1–10 scale). The override is explained by the adviser's view that the questionnaire did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite, and the client acknowledged and agreed to proceed at risk rating 5 – Balanced. The description given for the Balanced profile is: 'you do not see yourself as a particularly cautious person and have no strong positive or negative associations with the notion of taking risk… you make investment decisions reasonably quickly and don't tend to be particularly anxious about those decisions.'

    File:     [P1] (Risk profile report) records the natural/questionnaire risk level as 4, labelled 'Cautious Balanced', with the description that the investor 'tend[s] to see yourself as quite a cautious person and are inclined to view risk negatively rather than as a source of opportunity.' [P12] and [P6] (Risk profile report) record the agreed risk level as 5, labelled 'Balanced', with the description matching the suitability report word-for-word. [P7] (SIPP research report) states: 'The client's agreed risk level is 5. The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' [P9] (Pension switch report) also states 'The client's agreed risk level is 5.' The recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C) are both mapped to risk level 5 per [P6] and [P7]. However, the KIIDs at [P17] and [P18] rate these funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced) at category 4 on a 1–7 SRRI/KIID scale. [P11] (File notes) records the existing Aviva fund at current risk 2, Zurich at current risk 7, and Standard Life at current risk 4 — but these appear to be Defaqto ratings for the existing funds, not the client's ATR. [P16] (Risk profile report appendix) shows the ATR questionnaire responses for John Sullivan, which are cautious-leaning (e.g. 'Cautious' investment approach, 'More than £4,000' loss anxiety on £20,000, 'More cautious' self-description) and produced the natural score of 4. No additional notes were recorded for the agreed risk level section in the risk profile report ([P13]).

    Discrepancies:

      - The questionnaire produced a score of 4 ('Cautious Balanced') but the agreed rating used in the recommendation is 5 ('Balanced') — an upward override of one notch on a 1–10 scale.

      - The risk profile report ([P13]) records 'No additional notes were recorded' in the Agreed risk level section, meaning no bespoke justification for the override is documented within that tool's own narrative fields, even though the suitability report provides a written rationale.

      - The recommended funds are rated category 4 on the 1–7 KIID/SRRI scale ([P17], [P18]), while the agreed client risk rating is 5 on a 1–10 scale — these are different scales and the labels/numbers do not map directly.

      - The investment period shown in the risk profile projections ([P12]) is '2 years', whereas the suitability report states a 10-year investment term/growth objective.

      - [P11] (file notes) records Zurich current fund risk as 7 (Defaqto scale) — a label that might be read alongside the client ATR of 5 without context that these are different scales measuring different things.

    The primary issue for this check (G3.8) is whether there are conflicting risk scores, descriptions or labels across the file that are unexplained or inconsistent. The comparison can be made in full.

   

    1. Questionnaire score 4 vs agreed rating 5: The guard explicitly states that a questionnaire result differing from the agreed rating is an override, not a conflict, and becomes a finding only where it is unexplained. Here, the suitability report provides a written justification ('didn't fully reflect your risk appetite'), a client-specific growth objective rationale (10-year growth, not reliant on pensions), and a recorded client acknowledgement. The risk profile tool in [P1]/[P12] also records the natural risk level as 4 and the agreed level as 5, confirming the override is documented in both the suitability report and the supporting tool. The absence of free-text notes in the risk profile tool's own narrative boxes ([P13]) is a gap in the tool record, but the suitability report itself carries the explanation and acknowledgement. This is consistent with the override being explained, though the tool record is thin.

   

    2. The 'Balanced' description used in the suitability report (agreed level 5) matches word-for-word the description in [P6] and [P12]. The 'Cautious Balanced' label for level 4 is consistent between [P1] and the report. No label conflict exists for the agreed rating itself.

   

    3. KIID risk categories (1–7 scale) vs client ATR (1–10 scale): The recommended funds are rated 4 on the 1–7 SRRI/KIID scale. The guard warns that a 5 on a 1–10 scale is not a 5 on a 1–7 scale and that these scales are not interchangeable. The suitability report does not explain the mapping between the 1–10 client risk rating and the 1–7 KIID ratings of the recommended funds. The research report ([P7]) confirms the portfolio risk mapping is 5 (on the Defaqto 1–10 scale), which matches the client's agreed rating. The KIID category 4 (on 1–7) is a fund-level regulatory disclosure on a different scale and does not conflict with the portfolio-level rating of 5 on the advisory scale — this is a known scale difference protected by the guard.

   

    4. Investment period discrepancy: [P12] shows the risk profile projection was run over '2 years' with an investment of £106,000, yet the suitability report states a 10-year investment term. This is a material inconsistency within the evidence: the cashflow/projection tool used a 2-year horizon, not 10 years, when illustrating outcomes to support the agreed risk level. This means the projection shown to the client to 'agree' the risk level did not model the stated investment term, which undermines the robustness of the risk level agreement process.

   

    5. Existing fund risk labels in file notes ([P11]) — Aviva 2, Zurich 7, Standard Life 4 — are Defaqto ratings for existing funds, not the client's ATR, and the file notes context makes this reasonably clear. These are not conflicting ATR scores.

   

    6. Vulnerability: No vulnerability flags are identified in the evidence that bear materially on this check.

   

    Conclusion: The override from 4 to 5 is explained and acknowledged. The scale difference between KIID categories and advisory risk ratings is protected by the guard. However, the risk profile projection tool was run over 2 years when the stated investment term is 10 years — this is a material discrepancy that means the projection underpinning the agreed risk level was modelled on the wrong time horizon, casting doubt on whether the risk level agreement was properly informed. This is a Potential Concern at Moderate severity.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts: (1) ATR questionnaire produced rating 4 (Cautious Balanced), overridden upward to agreed rating 5 (Balanced) on the basis that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite and the client agreed; (2) capacity for loss is 'medium', assessed separately from ATR, based on surplus income, savings of ~£6,000, no liabilities, pension funds of ~£106,000 (noting internal inconsistency: arithmetic sum of five plans is £116,998.47; page 3 says ~£110,000; page 4 says ~£106,000), and client confirmation that underperformance would not detrimentally affect daily living; CFL is described as 'consistent with agreed ATR level of 5'; (3) investment term is 10 years; (4) recommended portfolio is 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (risk rating 5, OCF 0.18%) and 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced C (risk rating 5, OCF 0.29%), both rated 5 on an unspecified scale, targeting risk rating 5 Balanced, benchmarked against IA Mixed Investment 20-60% Shares TR in GB, managed as a blend of active and passive; (5) the portfolio is part of the firm's CIP reviewed quarterly; (6) the funds reflect the risk profile individually and combined so that portfolio drift does not alter the risk level.

    File:     Category D risk profile report confirms: natural risk level 4 (Cautious Balanced) from ATR questionnaire completed 18/09/2025; agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) following interactive discussion considering ATR, capacity for loss and required return. The Defaqto SIPP research report (category G) confirms agreed risk level 5 Balanced and shows the recommended portfolio as 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc and 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C, both at risk level 5 on the Defaqto scale. The KIID for the HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (category H, passage P9) states: the Fund is rated 3 on HSBC's own internal 1-5 risk scale (where 1 is lower and 5 is higher risk within the Global Strategy range), and separately the SRRI Risk and Reward Indicator (regulatory scale 1-7) rates the fund at 4 (passage P1). The KIID also states the fund aims to provide growth over the long term defined as 5 years or more, and that the fund may not be appropriate for investors who plan to withdraw within 5 years. No KIID or fund factsheet for CT Universal MAP Balanced C is present in the pack confirming its risk rating on any scale. The Defaqto research confirms both funds are shortlisted at agreed risk level 5 on the Defaqto profiler scale.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report assigns both funds a risk rating of 5, using an unspecified scale label. The HSBC KIID assigns the fund a rating of 3 on HSBC's internal 1-5 Global Strategy scale, and a SRRI of 4 on the regulatory 1-7 scale — neither equals 5. The report does not identify which scale the '5' rating derives from, nor reconcile these figures.

      - The report does not name the risk scale on which the portfolio's target rating of 5 and individual fund ratings of 5 are expressed. Without a stated scale, it is not possible to confirm the '5' label in the report corresponds to the Defaqto 1-10 (implied) scale, the HSBC 1-5 internal scale, the regulatory SRRI 1-7, or another system.

      - The capacity for loss section states pension funds total 'approximately £106,000' but the arithmetic sum of the five stated individual plan values is £116,998.47 and the report elsewhere (page 3) states ~£110,000. Neither approximation matches the arithmetic sum.

      - The report describes CFL as 'consistent with agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5' — this formulation aligns CFL with ATR rather than providing an independent assessment of whether the client can absorb loss from the recommended strategy. No quantified loss scenario (e.g. a specific percentage or monetary loss the client could sustain) is set out.

      - No KIID or fund-level risk document for CT Universal MAP Balanced C is present in the evidence pack to independently corroborate its risk rating of 5 on any scale.

      - The ATR was overridden upward from 4 to 5. The report states this was because the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite and the client agreed, but no further documented evidenced justification (such as a record of the specific discussion, projection comparison or client-signed acknowledgement) is present in the pack beyond the risk profile report narrative.

    The core alignment between the agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) and the recommended portfolio is corroborated by the Defaqto research report (P3, P6, P12), which confirms the agreed level is 5 Balanced and the two recommended funds are both mapped at that level on the Defaqto profiler scale. The investment term of 10 years is consistent with the HSBC fund's stated minimum holding period of 5 years or more (P9, P1) — the fund's recommendation threshold is shorter than the client's term, so no mismatch arises under the stated guard. The capacity for loss assessment is substantive in that it references income, savings, absence of liabilities and a client confirmation, and it is assessed separately from ATR. However, the report's conclusion that CFL is 'consistent with agreed ATR level of 5' is a restatement linking CFL to ATR rather than an independent finding about the client's ability to absorb loss at the strategy's risk level; no quantified loss scenario is modelled. This is a moderate-severity gap per the severity guidance. The ATR override from 4 to 5 is documented in both the suitability report and the risk profile report (P15, P6), which record the interactive discussion process and the agreed outcome. The risk profile report describes the process as considering ATR, CFL and required return. However, no specific additional justification record (e.g. signed client acknowledgement or a documented projection comparison showing why 5 is more appropriate than 4) is in the pack beyond the narrative statement. This is a concern but not at the highest severity level since the override is directionally documented. The most material issue for this check is the risk scale ambiguity for the recommended funds. The report assigns both funds a rating of '5' without specifying the scale. The HSBC KIID (P9) identifies the fund as rated 3 on HSBC's internal 1-5 scale and the SRRI as 4 on the regulatory 1-7 scale. Per the guard, SRRI/risk-and-reward indicators run on their own scale and do not map onto the profiler's, so a mismatch between the SRRI (4) and the report's fund rating (5) is not itself a finding of misalignment. The Defaqto research confirms both funds are shortlisted at risk level 5 on the Defaqto profiler, which is the same scale as the agreed client profile. The report's fund-level rating of '5' therefore most plausibly derives from the Defaqto profiler scale, corroborated by P10 and P12. However, the report itself does not state this, leaving the scale unnamed. The absence of a KIID for CT Universal MAP Balanced C means no independent fund-level corroboration exists for that fund outside the Defaqto research tool. The pension fund total discrepancy (~£106,000 vs ~£110,000 vs £116,998.47 arithmetic sum) is an internal inconsistency already flagged in the extraction; it affects the precision of the CFL narrative but not the qualitative conclusion that the client has medium CFL. Taken together: the strategy risk rating equals the agreed rating on the Defaqto scale and is corroborated by the research report; the term is consistent; but the CFL is framed as a restatement of ATR rather than as an independent capacity finding, the risk scale is not named in the report, and the override justification evidence is thin. These are material gaps warranting a Potential Concern at moderate severity.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) ATR questionnaire produced a score of 4 ('Cautious Balanced'); the adviser overrode this upward to 5 ('Balanced') on the basis that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite; the client confirmed he wished to proceed at risk rating 5 and acknowledged it is higher than the questionnaire result. (2) CFL is assessed as 'medium', supported by: State Pension ~£230/week, HGV earnings ~£300/week net, Sea Cadets income ~£20/week, spouse Kim contributing ~£1,100/month, combined income meeting ~£1,700/month household expenditure, savings ~£6,000, no liabilities, and pension funds totalling ~£106,000. Client confirmed underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living. CFL is stated as 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5'. (3) Investment term is stated as 10 years. (4) Recommended strategy: 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (risk rating 5) and 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced C (risk rating 5), both rated 5, benchmarked against IA Mixed Investment 20-60% Shares TR in GB, described as a blend of active and passive, CIP-governed. (5) No specific maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount is stated. (6) The override justification references the client's stated desire for best growth potential over a 10-year period and a balanced approach.

    File:     [P1]/[P8]: The risk profile report (D category) records a natural risk level of 4 ('Cautious Balanced') from the ATR questionnaire completed 18/09/2025. [P8]: The agreed risk level is 5 ('Balanced'), with description matching the report. The risk profile document records the investment as £106,000 over 2 years — not 10 years. [P2]/[P5]: The SIPP research report (G category) also records agreed risk level 5 ('Balanced') with the same description, and lists the two recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc 50% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C 50%). [P16]: ATR questionnaire responses show the client described his approach as 'Cautious', and said more than £4,000 fall on £20,000 would make him feel anxious (i.e. >20% loss tolerance implied), associated 'investment risk' with 'Caution', described himself as 'More cautious'. CFL questionnaire responses are also recorded. [P4]: CFL questionnaire section notes 'No additional notes were recorded'. [P3]: Agreed risk level section notes 'No additional notes were recorded'. [P17]: Suitability report itself records the ATR override and client acknowledgement. [P18]: Fund table confirms 50/50 split, both rated 5. [P7]: File note records existing fund risk ratings: Aviva current risk 2, Zurich current risk 7, Standard Life current risk 4. [P22]: CT Universal MAP Balanced KIID classifies the fund in risk category 4 on its own SRI scale (medium fluctuations), with equity allocation 45–70%.

    Discrepancies:

      - The risk profile document (P8) records the investment period as 2 years, whereas the suitability report states the investment term is 10 years. These are materially different and the document used to justify the agreed risk level uses a 2-year horizon not a 10-year horizon.

      - The pension fund total stated in the CFL section of the report is 'approximately £106,000', whereas the arithmetic sum of the five individual plan values in the canonical model is £116,998.47, and the report's own page 3 states 'approximately £110,000'. The £106,000 figure used in the CFL assessment matches neither the arithmetic total nor the page 3 narrative figure and is understated relative to both.

      - The ATR questionnaire responses recorded in P16 show the client described himself as 'Cautious', associated risk with 'Caution', and described himself as 'More cautious' compared to others — responses more consistent with risk level 4 or below. The report states the questionnaire produced a 4 but the override justification ('didn't fully reflect your risk appetite') is not elaborated with specific reasoning beyond reference to the client's growth objective and non-reliance on pension funds. No additional notes were recorded in the risk profile document's agreed risk level or CFL sections (P3, P4).

      - The CT Universal MAP Balanced C fund is classified at risk category 4 on its own SRI (synthetic risk indicator) scale in the KIID (P22), whereas the suitability report assigns it risk rating 5. These are on different scales (KIID 1–7 vs adviser 1–10), creating a potential scale incompatibility, though not necessarily a contradiction.

      - No specific maximum acceptable loss percentage or monetary amount is stated anywhere in the report or supporting file for this client, leaving tolerance for loss unquantified.

      - The CFL assessment concludes CFL is 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5' — this links CFL to ATR rather than providing an independent assessment, raising the concern that CFL may be a restatement of ATR rather than a separately reasoned conclusion.

    Several issues arise from comparing the report's assertions with the evidence pack.

   

    1. Investment term discrepancy: The report states a 10-year investment term and uses this as part of the override justification ('best growth potential over a 10-year period'). However, the risk profile document (P8) records the investment period as 2 years and models the agreed risk level projections on that basis. This is a material inconsistency: the document used to evidence the risk level agreement used a 2-year horizon, not the 10-year horizon stated in the suitability report. This is not explained or reconciled anywhere in the pack.

   

    2. Pension fund total in CFL: The report states pension funds totalling approximately £106,000 in the CFL section. The arithmetic sum of the five plans is £116,998.47; the report's own page 3 states approximately £110,000. The £106,000 figure in the CFL section matches neither. This is an internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction metadata and is directly material to the CFL assessment, which relies on the pension asset figure as a key input. A lower figure makes the CFL appear more constrained than it is.

   

    3. ATR override: The ATR questionnaire responses (P16) show consistently cautious answers (describes approach as 'Cautious', associates risk with 'Caution', 'More cautious' than others). The natural level of 4 is therefore consistent with those responses. The override to 5 is justified by a generic statement that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite, with the only specific supporting factor being the client's desire for growth over 10 years and non-reliance on the pension. The risk profile document records 'No additional notes were recorded' in both the agreed risk level and CFL sections (P3, P4), meaning there is no documented elaboration of the override rationale beyond what the report itself states. This is a weak evidential basis for an upward override, particularly given the questionnaire responses. The client's acknowledgement is recorded (P17), but acknowledgement of a higher rating does not itself constitute justification for overriding a questionnaire result upward.

   

    4. CFL as restatement of ATR: The CFL conclusion states it is 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5'. While the CFL assessment does reference specific financial facts (income, expenditure, savings, no liabilities), the conclusion anchors on the ATR rating rather than standing independently. This is a moderate concern under the severity guidance.

   

    5. Fund risk ratings: The CT Universal MAP Balanced C is rated 4 on the KIID SRI scale (P22) and 5 on the adviser's 1–10 scale. These are different scales, so this is not a direct contradiction — the false-positive guard on incompatible scales applies — however, the report does not acknowledge that the two scales differ, and a reader could conflate them.

   

    6. Tolerance for loss: No quantified maximum acceptable loss is stated. Given the lack of loss scenario modelling in the risk sections, this is a low-severity gap.

   

    The most material concerns are: (a) the risk profile document using a 2-year investment period when the report relies on a 10-year term to justify the override; (b) the upward ATR override supported only by a generic statement with no additional file notes; and (c) the inconsistent pension fund total in the CFL section. These are not explained by any guard.
