CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The report characterises the client's knowledge as 'Limited', but the risk profile questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' across the key product-specific questions (stocks and shares, pension planning, unit trusts, EIS/VCTs, and the general investing self-assessment). 'Limited' overstates what the questionnaire captured. The suitability report's ATR section cites the Balanced investor description referencing 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters', which is inconsistent with the questionnaire recording 'No understanding / knowledge' across all investment-related categories. The risk profile report records no additional narrative notes in the investor experience section, meaning there is no recorded explanation or discussion on file to bridge the gap between the questionnaire responses and the 'Limited' characterisation used in the report. 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts the client's knowledge level as 'Limited' (per /knowledgeAndExperience/perClient[]/statedLevel). The report narrative in the capacity for loss section also states 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements'.

    File:     The risk profile questionnaire (category D) records the client as answering 'No understanding / knowledge' to multiple direct investor experience questions, including general investing, stocks and shares, pension planning, unit trusts, EIS/VCTs, and the overarching question 'When it comes to your experience and understanding of investing, how would you describe yourself?' The client answered 'Very little understanding / knowledge' only to the cash/savings/deposit accounts question. The client has not invested in any of the listed product types in the last 5 years except current/savings/cash accounts. No additional narrative notes were recorded in the investor experience or agreed risk level sections of the risk profile. The suitability report's ATR section references the Balanced investor profile description, which states 'If you have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters then you may be suited to a Balanced approach', while the underlying questionnaire records no such experience or understanding. No category C file note records any explanation provided to the client about the complexity of the recommended SIPP or the underlying balanced funds.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report characterises the client's knowledge as 'Limited', but the risk profile questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' across the key product-specific questions (stocks and shares, pension planning, unit trusts, EIS/VCTs, and the general investing self-assessment). 'Limited' overstates what the questionnaire captured.

      - The suitability report's ATR section cites the Balanced investor description referencing 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters', which is inconsistent with the questionnaire recording 'No understanding / knowledge' across all investment-related categories.

      - The risk profile report records no additional narrative notes in the investor experience section, meaning there is no recorded explanation or discussion on file to bridge the gap between the questionnaire responses and the 'Limited' characterisation used in the report.

      - No category C file note or equivalent record evidences that the client's comprehension of the recommended products (SIPP consolidation into Aviva Pension Portfolio invested in CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced funds) was tested or explained, notwithstanding that the client has no recorded experience of pension or investment products beyond workplace arrangements.

    The canonical model records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited'. The risk profile questionnaire in category D is the assessed evidence and records 'No understanding / knowledge' against the general investing self-assessment and every product-specific knowledge question relevant to the recommendation (pension planning, unit trusts, stocks and shares). The only question yielding a marginally higher response is cash/savings/deposits ('Very little understanding / knowledge'), which is not relevant to the recommended product. The report's use of 'Limited' therefore overstates what the assessment captured. The guard acknowledges that a meeting explanation could bridge this gap, but no narrative notes were recorded in the investor experience section of the risk profile report (P3), so there is no evidenced discussion that upgraded the client's knowledge. Furthermore, the suitability report's own ATR narrative (P15) quotes the Balanced investor description, which presupposes 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' — a description that directly contradicts the questionnaire responses. The recommended product is a SIPP consolidation invested in two balanced multi-asset funds. While these are not the most complex products available, they represent a step beyond anything the client has previously held or demonstrated understanding of. No record in category C or elsewhere documents that the features, risks or mechanics of the SIPP or the selected funds were explained to or understood by the client. The vulnerability overlay: P15 records no vulnerability indicators, and no vulnerability-related adjustments to the knowledge/experience assessment are evidenced, so the overlay does not alter the finding. The net position is that the report's 'Limited' characterisation is not supported by the questionnaire evidence, which records 'No understanding / knowledge', and there is no compensating narrative on file. This is a material discrepancy between the report's assertion and the assessed evidence.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report states (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section): the client has previously taken out cash savings products but has not taken out a stocks & shares investment by himself. He has held group/workplace pensions for some time and feels comfortable with how investments work, with a reasonable understanding of potential risks and rewards.

    File:     The risk profile report (category D) records the following investor-experience questionnaire responses: 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' — answer: 'No understanding / knowledge'. 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' — answer: 'No'. 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years?' — answer: 'Yes, by myself'. 'Have you invested in stocks and shares within the last 5 years?' — answer: 'No'. The Investor Experience section of the risk profile report records no additional notes. The recommended product is a pension (Aviva Pension Portfolio, a SIPP-style arrangement with self-directed fund selection).

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report asserts the client 'feel[s] comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' in the context of having held group pensions. The risk profile questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' to the question about pension planning and income in retirement risks — a direct contradiction.

      - The suitability report implies pension experience supports a level of understanding consistent with the recommendation. The risk profile questionnaire records 'No' to having invested in pension planning products in the last 5 years, notwithstanding the existence of legacy workplace pensions (which are passive/employer-arranged holdings, not self-directed investing).

      - The report states the client has not taken out a stocks & shares investment by himself — this is consistent with the file's 'No' responses to stocks and shares experience, so no discrepancy here.

      - The report states the client has held cash savings products — consistent with the file's 'Yes, by myself' for savings/deposit accounts. No discrepancy.

      - No additional adviser notes are recorded in the Investor Experience section of the risk profile to explain or qualify the 'No understanding / knowledge' answer regarding pensions, despite the report's assertion of reasonable understanding.

    The central discrepancy is material. The suitability report characterises the client as having a 'reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' derived from holding group pensions, and uses this to frame the recommendation of a pension consolidation into the Aviva Pension Portfolio (a self-directed platform pension involving fund selection). The risk profile questionnaire, completed on 18 September 2025 and forming part of the formal assessment, records the client's own answer as 'No understanding / knowledge' when asked whether he understands how pension planning and income in retirement works and the risks associated with them. That answer is the material one under the guard: 'A client recorded as having no understanding of pension planning is the material answer where the recommendation is a pension.' The guard also cautions that holding a workplace pension is passive experience and that plan ownership does not prove understanding — so the report's reliance on having 'held group pensions for some time' to infer comfort with how investments work is not corroborated by the file. The Investor Experience section of the risk profile contains no additional notes from the adviser that might explain why the questionnaire response was overridden or qualified. There is no record of client questions asked, explanations given, or comprehension checks performed in connection with the recommended product's features (multi-fund self-directed pension platform). The recommended product is therefore a product with meaningful complexity being recommended to a client whose own recorded answer is 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning, with no documented bridge between that recorded level and the report's assertion of reasonable understanding. This meets the severity guidance for a High finding: the report's description of the client's knowledge contradicts the fact find (here, the risk profile questionnaire which is the formal knowledge/experience record). No vulnerability indicators in the file appear to bear on this specific check beyond reinforcing the concern that comprehension was not adequately evidenced.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report recommends an Aviva Platform Personal Pension (Flexi-Access Drawdown) which the extraction classifies as Complex, citing: pension transfer from multiple existing arrangements; Flexi-Access Drawdown with flexible income withdrawal; investment into multi-asset funds with ongoing management; and IHT implications from April 2027. The client is stated to understand pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of FAD. The report explains death benefits (pre- and post-75), IHT treatment from April 2027, the Lump Sum Allowance, Annual Allowance, and the mechanics of FAD. The complexity classification is inferred by the extractor rather than explicitly stated in the report.

    File:     The file contains: (1) Aviva Key Features document describing the Pension Portfolio, including FAD options, drawdown risks, and retirement benefit choices [P1, P20, P22]; (2) Aviva Terms and Conditions covering FAD, investment options, charges, and adviser charge arrangements [P2, P3, P5, P7, P10, P11, P13, P14, P15, P18]; (3) KIIDs for CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (risk rating 4, ongoing charge 0.29%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio [P8, P9, P21, P23, P24]; (4) SIPP research report including risk level, investment selection, and product/platform shortlisting supporting the recommendation [P16, P19]; (5) Policy research document evidencing FAD availability, UFPLS, annuity and death benefit options across compared plans [P6, P12]; (6) The suitability report itself describing the product and explaining FAD, lifetime allowance changes, annual allowance, and IHT implications to the client [P4, P17]. The file does not contain an explicit fact-find or knowledge-and-experience questionnaire grading the client's investment knowledge level. There is no record of the client asking questions about FAD or the IHT changes, nor any documented comprehension check beyond the report's own narrative.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report's complexity classification is inferred by the extractor (assertionStatus: Inferred) rather than explicitly stated in the report — no explicit complexity label is present in the suitability report itself.

      - No explicit knowledge/experience grading of the client (e.g. basic, intermediate, sophisticated) is recorded in the file; the report asserts the client understands pension freedoms and FAD but no fact-find or questionnaire corroborates that grading.

      - No record of client questions, comprehension checks, or documented acknowledgement of the IHT 2027 change or FAD mechanics is present in the file beyond the report's own assertions.

      - The ATR questionnaire produced a score of 4 but was overridden to 5 by adviser judgement; no corroborating evidence of the client's knowledge level that might support or contextualise this override is recorded.

    The recommended product — an Aviva Platform Personal Pension in FAD invested in multi-asset funds — is a mainstream personal pension wrapper. Under the applicable guard, a mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not inherently complex by product volume or value alone. The complexity drivers cited (FAD flexibility, IHT implications from 2027, pension consolidation) are real features that warrant explanation, and the report does explain them in reasonable detail at [P4] and [P17]. The file contains the relevant product literature (Key Features [P20], KIIDs [P21, P23, P24], T&Cs [P2, P7, P10]) confirming that the product's features as described in the report are accurately characterised. The FAD mechanics described in the report are consistent with [P1] and [P4]. The IHT point is a genuine complexity element introduced from April 2027 and is flagged in the report. However, the file contains no fact-find or knowledge-and-experience record grading the client. The report asserts the client understands pension freedoms and prefers FAD flexibility, but this is an unsupported assertion — no questionnaire, meeting note, or fact-find passage corroborates it. The absence of a comprehension check or knowledge grading, where the product has the unusual feature of the IHT rule change and FAD flexibility, represents a moderate gap. This does not rise to the highest severity because the product is a mainstream pension and the complexity drivers do not push it into a genuinely exotic category, but the absence of any evidenced knowledge grading or comprehension record is a moderate concern. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved passages that would materially alter this assessment. The ATR override from 4 to 5 is noted but does not directly bear on knowledge and experience evidencing.

  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report records the client's knowledge and experience level as 'Limited'. The recommended product (Aviva Pension Portfolio) is classified as 'Complex'. The report states in the capacity for loss section: 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements'. The report also states in the attitude to risk section that the client 'may be suited to a Balanced approach to investing' if they 'have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. The fund recommendation section states that knowledge and experience were considered alongside ATR and capacity for loss. The report records the client wishes to keep the investment strategy 'fairly simple' and recommends two multi-asset balanced funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc and CT Universal MAP Balanced C).

    File:     The risk profile report (category D) records the client's questionnaire responses as: 'No understanding / knowledge' for stocks and shares; 'No understanding / knowledge' for Enterprise Investment Schemes and Venture Capital Trusts; 'No understanding / knowledge' for pension planning and income in retirement. For recent investment activity, the client has invested in current accounts, savings/deposit accounts, Cash ISA and National Savings by themselves in the last 5 years, but has NOT invested in unit trusts, general investment accounts, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension planning products in the last 5 years. The natural risk level section of the risk profile report describes a 'Cautious Balanced' investor as one with 'limited or moderate experience of investments'. No additional notes were recorded in the Investor Experience section. The agreed risk level was 5 (Balanced), overriding the questionnaire result of 4, with the rationale that the client is not reliant on pension funds and wishes for best growth potential. The suitability report itself acknowledges in the capacity for loss section that 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements'. The recommended product is the Aviva Pension Portfolio, which can hold insured funds, unit trusts, OEICs, investment trusts, exchange traded instruments and equities. The fact find (category B) and client agreement (category A) contain no additional recorded knowledge or experience detail beyond the risk profile questionnaire. No category C record evidencing client comprehension of the complex product features is present in the pack.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report classifies the recommended product as 'Complex' but records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited' — a complex product is being recommended to a client with limited knowledge without adequate evidenced justification of comprehension.

      - The risk profile questionnaire (P9) records 'No understanding / knowledge' for pension planning and income in retirement, yet the report states knowledge and experience were considered and found sufficient to support the recommendation — this overstates the client's knowledge relative to the file evidence.

      - The risk profile description for the agreed level 5 (Balanced) states the client 'may be suited to a Balanced approach' if they 'have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' (P1, P7, P19); however the questionnaire responses record no understanding or knowledge of pensions, stocks and shares, or EIS/VCTs, and no recent investment activity in any product beyond cash-based savings — the preconditions of the Balanced description are not met by the file evidence.

      - The Investor Experience section of the risk profile report (P10) records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning there is no documented narrative evidencing the client's investment experience to support the 'limited' characterisation used in the report.

      - No category C record (e.g. client questions, adviser explanations, confirmation of understanding) evidencing comprehension of the complex product's features is present in the pack, despite the product being classified as complex.

      - The suitability report states the client 'confirmed' they understand pension freedoms and prefer Flexi Access Drawdown flexibility (P16), but this is an adviser assertion with no corroborating category C evidence of comprehension in the file.

    The report records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited' and the recommended product complexity as 'Complex'. The file evidence from the risk profile questionnaire (P9) is materially more negative than 'limited': the client recorded 'No understanding / knowledge' across all three knowledge questions (stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, pension planning and income in retirement), and has had no investment activity in any pension or market-linked product in the last five years, only cash-based savings. The report's use of the word 'limited' in the capacity for loss section ('your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements') is consistent with the file in acknowledging the restricted nature of the client's experience, but the characterisation of knowledge as 'Limited' (rather than 'None' or 'Basic') in the canonical knowledge level field arguably overstates what the questionnaire recorded. The guard that 'You confirmed you understand' is an adviser assertion rather than evidence of understanding applies directly here: the report's statement that the client 'confirmed' understanding of pension freedoms and Flexi Access Drawdown is an adviser assertion, not a category C record, and no corroborating evidence of comprehension appears in the pack. The risk profile description for Balanced (risk 5) carries the precondition of 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' (P1, P7, P19). The questionnaire responses directly contradict this precondition, meaning the description is being used as evidence of knowledge it does not establish. The Investor Experience section (P10) records no additional notes, so there is no supplementary narrative to bridge the gap. The recommended product (Aviva Pension Portfolio) is classified as complex and can hold a wide range of instruments including exchange traded instruments and investment trusts, though the actual fund recommendation is two risk-rated multi-asset balanced funds (P5), which represents a simpler implementation than the full product capability would suggest. Nevertheless, the product itself is classified as complex by the report, and no category C comprehension evidence is present. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability drivers have been identified in the pack as materially affecting this check. The ATR override from 4 to 5 is noted in the ambiguities section and the report itself (P7) but this check excludes attitude to risk unless directly relied upon to evidence knowledge — it is not so relied upon here. The combination of: a complex product; a client with no recorded understanding of pension planning, stocks or shares; no documented investor experience notes; and no category C evidence of comprehension constitutes a material gap. This is at the high end of severity given the direct contradiction between the questionnaire responses and the report's assertion that knowledge and experience were considered and found consistent with the recommendation.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report asserts (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section) that the client 'feel[s] comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards.' It further states (page 4, Capacity for Loss section) that 'although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' The report thus characterises the client as having a reasonable investment understanding but explicitly limited experience — confined to workplace pension arrangements only.

    File:     The risk profile report (category D, [P14]) records no additional notes under 'Investor Experience', stating only 'No additional notes were recorded.' Similarly, [P16] records no additional notes under 'Agreed risk level' and 'Investment target.' The file notes (category C, [P2]) corroborate that the client's experience is limited to workplace pension arrangements and confirm the client understands returns are not guaranteed and that funds may go up and down. The file notes also record the client was taken through an ATR discussion. However, there is no recorded evidence in the file of specific questions asked by the client about the recommended product's features or complexity, no record of comprehension checks, and no documented explanation of the CIP (Centralised Investment Proposition) multi-fund structure's complexity to the client. The fact find ([P21]) does not contain a graded knowledge assessment. The recommended product is an Aviva Pension Portfolio using a CIP comprising multiple funds across different asset classes, regions and sectors ([P4], [P5], [P7]) — a multi-fund, actively governed structure that is more complex than a single workplace pension.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts the client has 'a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' but the risk profile report (category D) records no additional notes under Investor Experience, providing no independent corroboration of this level of understanding beyond the report's own assertion.

      - The report characterises the client's experience as limited to workplace pension arrangements, yet recommends a complex CIP multi-fund structure — there is no file evidence of comprehension checks or client questions recorded to evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of this more complex product.

      - No graded knowledge level appears in the fact find (category B) to corroborate the 'reasonable understanding' characterisation in the report.

      - The file notes record the ATR discussion and capacity for loss narrative but contain no record of the client asking questions about the CIP structure, its governance, diversification mechanics, or multi-fund complexity.

    The recommended product — an Aviva Pension Portfolio using a CIP with multiple funds across different asset classes, regions and sectors — is materially more complex than the client's prior experience (workplace pensions only). The report asserts the client has a reasonable understanding of investment risks and rewards. The file notes (category C, [P2]) do partially corroborate that the client understands returns are not guaranteed and that values can rise and fall, which is consistent with the report's narrative on non-guaranteed returns. However, the risk profile document (category D, [P14]) records no additional notes under Investor Experience, meaning there is no independent, graded or contemporaneous record of the client's level of understanding beyond the suitability report's own assertions. The fact find contains no knowledge grading. Crucially, there is no recorded evidence anywhere in the file of client questions about the CIP structure, comprehension checks regarding how the multi-fund approach works, or documented explanations of its complexity and how it differs from a simple workplace pension. The guard states that an absence of recorded questions is not proof the client did not understand, but where the product is complex this constitutes an evidence gap. The CIP is a complex product relative to the client's demonstrated experience (workplace pensions only), and the file contains no category C evidence of comprehension. No vulnerability factors have been identified in the evidence that would independently alter this conclusion, though the absence of a formal vulnerability assessment outcome (noted in the extraction metadata) means this cannot be ruled out. On balance, the report's assertion of 'reasonable understanding' is only weakly corroborated, and the absence of any comprehension evidence for a complex product recommended to a client with limited prior investment experience constitutes a moderate concern under the severity guidance.

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section) asserts: (1) the client has held cash savings products; (2) the client has NOT held a stocks & shares investment by themselves; (3) the client has held group/workplace pensions for some time and feels comfortable with how investments work, with a reasonable understanding of potential risks and rewards. Five existing pension arrangements are recorded: People's Pension (OccupationalDC, fund: Global Investments up to 85% shares, 100%, risk 9), Scottish Widows Master Trust (PersonalPension, funds: SW Pension Portfolio Four CS1 & SW Pension Portfolio Three CS1), Aviva (PersonalPension, fund: Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation, risk 2), Zurich (PersonalPension, fund: Managed AP, risk 7), and Standard Life (PersonalPension, fund: SL At Retirement Universal PP 10 Year, risk 4).

    File:     The case file contains policy documents for all five arrangements. Aviva [P2] confirms plan SP50163762 holding 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2' with value £3,744.36, with a Lifestage Investment Approach (auto-switching). Zurich [P8] confirms plan P10026-719-001/DL holding 'Managed AP (accumulation)' with fund value £103,439.24. Standard Life [P5,P14] confirms plan D2301334000 holding 'SL At Retirement Universal (PP10 Year) Pension Fd'. Scottish Widows [P11,P13] confirms plan ZU4225575 (SWMT) with value £211.32. People's Pension [P12] confirms a plan invested in 'Global Investments (up to 85% shares)'. The policy research report [P21] records plan types for the five arrangements. No fact-find document is visible in the retrieved passages recording the client's self-assessed knowledge level, cash savings history, or a direct client statement about comfort with investments beyond what the suitability report itself records. No client questionnaire or fact-find category A or C passages addressing knowledge and experience are present.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the client 'confirmed' cash savings experience and that they have 'not taken out a stocks & shares investment by yourself' — no supporting fact-find, client questionnaire or category A/C document in the retrieved passages corroborates these self-reported statements; the assertion rests solely on the report's own narrative.

      - The report describes the client as feeling 'comfortable with how investments work' and having 'a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' — no corroborating fact-find grading or knowledge assessment score is present in the file passages retrieved.

      - The Aviva plan (risk 2, auto-switching Lifestage approach) was set up in November 2017 and is shown as 'Paid Up' [P2] — as an arrangement likely set up by a previous adviser and running on autopilot via Lifestage, it provides limited evidence of active investment engagement by the client.

      - The Zurich plan [P8,P15] is described as a former Allied Dunbar plan closed to new investors, with no payments received in the last ten years [P8] — this is an inherited arrangement that says little about the client's own investment decision-making experience.

      - The People's Pension [P12] is an occupational/workplace pension — under the applicable guard, auto-enrolment ownership alone does not evidence investment experience without something more, and nothing more is present in the file.

      - Standard Life policy value on 30 September 2025 is shown as £2,994.30 in [P4] whereas the report records the Standard Life value as £3,002.00 — a minor numerical discrepancy between file and report (though within a short valuation-date window and likely immaterial to knowledge/experience).

    The report makes three distinct knowledge/experience assertions: (1) cash savings held; (2) no solo stocks & shares investment; (3) group pensions held for some time with self-reported comfort and reasonable understanding. The file does contain policy documents for all five existing pension arrangements, which corroborates the bare fact of pension ownership. However, the guards are important here: (a) the People's Pension is a workplace/auto-enrolment arrangement — ownership alone does not evidence investment experience; (b) the Aviva plan is a paid-up stakeholder on an auto-switching Lifestage approach set up in 2017, likely by a previous adviser, with no active client engagement evidenced; (c) the Zurich plan is a legacy Allied Dunbar policy closed to new investors with no payments in ten years — an inherited arrangement that says little about the client's own experience. Holdings corroborate experience but are not proof of knowledge. The report's positive characterisation — that the client is 'comfortable with how investments work' and has 'a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' — is a knowledge-level assertion for which no fact-find, questionnaire or graded assessment appears anywhere in the retrieved passages. The cash savings assertion and the 'no solo stocks & shares' statement similarly rest only on the report's own narrative with no corroborating document. The recommended product (Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice, a SIPP with access to a wide investment universe including ETIs, equities and structured products per [P16,P17]) is not a straightforward product. Given that the client's knowledge level is stated but not graded or corroborated in the file, and that the existing holdings are predominantly legacy, auto-managed or workplace arrangements that provide limited evidence of active investment understanding, there is a gap: the report asserts a positive knowledge level ('comfortable', 'reasonable understanding') to support suitability of a relatively sophisticated wrapper, but the file evidence does not independently corroborate that characterisation. No vulnerability information in the retrieved passages is identified that would alter this assessment. The Standard Life value discrepancy (£2,994.30 vs £3,002.00) is likely a valuation-date difference and is not material to this check. The outcome is a Potential Concern at Low-to-Moderate severity: the knowledge level is stated in the report but not graded or corroborated by a fact-find or assessment document in the file, and the existing holdings — while present — are predominantly legacy or auto-enrolled arrangements that provide weak independent evidence of active investment experience or understanding.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report classifies the recommended product as 'Complex', identifying four complexity drivers: (1) pension transfer from multiple existing arrangements; (2) Flexi-Access Drawdown with flexible income withdrawal; (3) investment into multi-asset funds with ongoing management; (4) IHT implications from April 2027. The report provides rationale across multiple recommendation sections: the existing Aviva plan is 0.06% more expensive and invested in lifestyling funds that will move outside the agreed risk range; the new scheme offers wider investment options, adviser management, and facilitates adviser charging; the Zurich plan is expensive at 0.93% and invested in a non-matching risk fund; the Standard Life plan is stated as 0.08% more expensive; Scottish Widows does not offer a suitable single multi-asset fund at risk rating 5 and does not allow adviser management; the People's Pension is retained as contributions continue; Scottish Widows encashment recommended under small pots rules for ~£170 net. The client is described as a balanced investor (ATR 5, overriding questionnaire result of 4), with experience limited to workplace pension arrangements. The file notes record that the client understands funds can go up and down and that underperformance would not detrimentally affect day-to-day living.

    File:     The risk profile report [P22] records a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) with the description referencing 'limited or moderate experience of investments'. The file notes [P8] record the client as having experience 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and confirm the client understands returns are not guaranteed and that potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living. The ATR was overridden to 5 at the adviser's instigation. The file notes [P8] record the agreed ATR wording for a Balanced investor as requiring 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. The policy research/file notes [P10] record rationale for discounting alternatives: Aviva alternative only offers risk-4 fund; Zurich has no risk-5 multi-asset funds and the blend includes an asset-specific fund of interest deposits; Standard Life funds in the same equity range are more expensive than Aviva. The Aviva Pension Portfolio Key Features [P2] confirms the product allows income drawdown (flexi-access), lump sum, and annuity options — features consistent with the complexity drivers stated. The SIPP research report [P3, P4] confirms platform research was conducted and Aviva Platform was selected. The risk profile report [P14] records no additional notes under 'Investor Experience'. The file notes [P8] contain ATR wording referencing 'some experience in investments' consistent with a balanced investor but the underlying questionnaire result of 4 carries the description of 'limited or moderate experience'. No record of specific client questions asked or explanations given about FAD mechanics, IHT implications from April 2027, or the multi-arrangement transfer complexity.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving by switching), but the charges comparison table (noted in the internal inconsistencies) shows '+0.26%' impact — i.e. the new plan is more expensive for the Standard Life tranche. This undermines part of the cost-based rationale for that transfer.

      - The client's underlying questionnaire result was 4 (Cautious Balanced — 'limited or moderate experience'), but the report proceeds on the basis of ATR 5 (Balanced — 'some experience'). The rationale for the complexity of the recommendation rests on the overridden ATR 5, not the questionnaire-evidenced ATR 4.

      - The risk profile report [P14] records no additional notes under 'Investor Experience', providing no documented corroboration of the client's investment knowledge or experience beyond the bare characterisation in the file notes.

      - No record in the evidence of specific explanations given to the client about the FAD mechanics, IHT implications from April 2027, or the complexity of consolidating multiple arrangements — features explicitly identified as complexity drivers.

      - The report identifies IHT implications from April 2027 as a complexity driver but provides no rationale in the retrieved passages explaining how this was explained to the client or why the client's knowledge/experience is sufficient to understand this feature.

    The recommended product is classified as complex with four stated drivers. The check requires that the rationale adequately justifies recommending a complex product to this client and that the client's knowledge and experience support the recommendation. Several points arise. First, the rationale for transferring the three pensions (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) is partially corroborated: the file notes [P10] record alternative-option research for each, and the suitability report provides cost and investment-alignment reasons. However, the Standard Life cost rationale is internally contradicted — the report says 0.08% saving but the charges table shows +0.26% increase (noted in the extraction's internal inconsistencies). This weakens one strand of the rationale but does not wholly undermine the transfer case, which also rests on fund alignment and consolidation objectives. Second, regarding knowledge and experience: the client's experience is recorded as limited to workplace pension arrangements [P8], and the questionnaire produced ATR 4 with a description of 'limited or moderate experience' [P22]. The ATR was overridden to 5 at the adviser's instigation, with the client agreeing. The Balanced investor description at ATR 5 requires 'some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' [P8]. The tension between the questionnaire result (limited/moderate experience) and the overridden level (some experience) means the knowledge base supporting the complex recommendation is not robustly evidenced. Third, and most materially for this check, there is no record in the evidence of specific explanations given to the client about the FAD mechanics, the IHT implications from April 2027, or the multi-arrangement transfer complexity — all of which are listed as complexity drivers in the report. The risk profile report records no notes under Investor Experience [P14]. The absence of documented comprehension evidence where the product is complex and the client's experience is limited to workplace pensions is a moderate concern. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability is flagged in the evidence that materially affects this check — the capacity for loss notes reference income sufficiency rather than cognitive or other vulnerability. The ATR override inconsistency (the file note [P8] contains a self-contradictory passage stating both 'happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6' and 'confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5') is noted but is primarily a CHK-005 matter and does not directly affect the knowledge/experience assessment here. On balance: the rationale for the transfers is present and partially corroborated, but (a) the Standard Life cost rationale is contradicted by the charges table, (b) there is no documented record of client comprehension of the complex features (FAD, IHT, multi-transfer), and (c) the knowledge base is limited and the experience record contains no notes. This constitutes a moderate concern — no record of client questions or explanations where the product has unusual features, with a client whose experience is limited to workplace pensions.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.8). The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying a cost saving from switching). The charges comparison table shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — an increase, not a saving. These directly contradict each other. Derived figures confirm the Standard Life (EA5) charges row is calculated using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), meaning the cost comparison for Standard Life is computed on the wrong base and is unreliable regardless of the direction stated. Multiple rationale bullets are repeated verbatim across the Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life recommendations: 'The new scheme offers access to a wider range of investment options, allowing a more tailored investment strategy that better suits the client's risk profile and retirement goals'; 'The new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly, ensuring ongoing financial planning support without separate payments'; 'The adviser will have the ability to actively monitor and adjust investments as required.' These are boilerplate shared across recommendations rather than plan-specific rationale. 6 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report provides rationale for five recommendations: (1) Retain People's Pension — employer contributions ongoing, transferring would lose employer contributions; (2) Encash Scottish Widows — no suitable single multi-asset fund at risk level 5, no adviser management permitted, net receipt ~£170 under small pots rules; (3) Switch Aviva into new Aviva pension — lifestyling drift outside risk range, 0.06% cost saving, wider investment options, adviser charging facility, active monitoring; (4) Switch Zurich — no risk-level-5 funds available, blended funds underperformed, consolidation objective, wider options, flexible drawdown, adviser charging, monitoring; (5) Switch Standard Life — lifestyling drift, 0.08% cost saving, wider options, flexible drawdown, adviser charging, monitoring. All 22 rationale items are flagged isClientSpecific=true. Recommendations are linked to objectives OBJ2 and OBJ3. Several rationale bullets are repeated verbatim across Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life recommendations (e.g. 'The new scheme offers access to a wider range of investment options...', 'The new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly...', 'The adviser will have the ability to actively monitor...'). The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving). No retirement income projection is present in the report. Client is identified as a sole client (C1); C2 fields absent from extraction.

    File:     Policy research (P1, P4, P8) confirms the five existing plans and the strategy of consolidation. P8 records the overall advice summary as consolidation due to the client not understanding multiple pensions, with alternative strategies noted as 'Current set up isn't working because it is too much information for him to understand.' P8 records per-plan recommendations (Switch for Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life; Retain for People's Pension; Encash/Switch for Scottish Widows) with brief notes: Aviva — 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; Zurich — 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; People's Pension — 'Active workplace pension'; Scottish Widows — encash/small pots. P1 confirms Scottish Widows does not allow adviser charging (Can adviser charging be applied: No for Scottish Widows column). SIPP research report (P5, P7) confirms the recommended Aviva platform solution with investment of £110,185, fund selection (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund), and MiFID II cost disclosures. Risk profile report (P11, P12) confirms natural ATR of 4, agreed risk level discussion took place, but no additional notes recorded for investment target or agreed risk level discussion. The charges comparison table (internal inconsistency noted in extraction) shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% (an increase, not a saving as stated in the rationale). Derived figures confirm EA5 charges row is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable. No retirement income projection is evidenced in the file. The cashflow modelling scenario (P3, P6) is present but the People's Pension is noted as potentially omitted from inputs.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying a cost saving from switching). The charges comparison table shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — an increase, not a saving. These directly contradict each other.

      - Derived figures confirm the Standard Life (EA5) charges row is calculated using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), meaning the cost comparison for Standard Life is computed on the wrong base and is unreliable regardless of the direction stated.

      - Multiple rationale bullets are repeated verbatim across the Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life recommendations: 'The new scheme offers access to a wider range of investment options, allowing a more tailored investment strategy that better suits the client's risk profile and retirement goals'; 'The new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly, ensuring ongoing financial planning support without separate payments'; 'The adviser will have the ability to actively monitor and adjust investments as required.' These are boilerplate shared across recommendations rather than plan-specific rationale.

      - The policy research file (P8) records the objective field for each plan recommendation as blank (no objective listed), and the rationale notes are brief ('Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing') compared to the fuller narrative in the suitability report. The research document does not corroborate the specific linked objectives OBJ2/OBJ3 by name.

      - No retirement income projection is present in the report or the file (flagged as NotPresentInDocument in extraction), leaving the assertion that the recommendations position the client 'well-positioned for retirement' unsupported by projected outcomes.

      - The Scottish Widows recommendation presents two alternative outcomes (encash now for ~£170 net vs retain to retirement for potential full tax-free receipt) without unambiguously stating which is the single recommended course of action, though the extraction notes the £170 net receipt appears to be the recommended course.

      - The risk profile report (P11) records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both the investment target discussion and the agreed risk level discussion, meaning the override of ATR from 4 to 5 and its link to the recommended solution is not documented in the risk profile evidence beyond the ambiguity noted in the extraction.

    The report provides rationale for all five recommendations and links them to objectives, satisfying the basic requirement. Several elements are genuinely client-specific: the People's Pension retention rationale (active employer contributions) is plan-specific and corroborated by P8; the Scottish Widows rationale (no suitable single-fund at risk 5, no adviser charging) is corroborated by P1 which confirms adviser charging is not available; the Zurich rationale (no risk-5 funds, blended funds underperformed) is specific to that provider. However, three material concerns arise. First, the most serious: the Standard Life cost rationale states a 0.08% saving, but the charges table shows +0.26% increase, and the derived figures demonstrate the EA5 row is computed on the wrong fund value entirely. This is a direct internal contradiction within the report itself on a factual matter central to the switch justification — the rationale claims cost saving where the report's own charges table and arithmetic show a cost increase. This is not explained by any guard and constitutes a genuine finding. Second, three rationale bullets are repeated verbatim across the Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life recommendations. Under the guard, identical verbatim repetition is boilerplate. While some generic benefits (adviser charging access, investment range) legitimately apply to all three switches, their word-for-word duplication without plan-specific adaptation reduces the client-specificity of the rationale for the Aviva and Standard Life recommendations in particular. Third, the absence of a retirement income projection (flagged as NotPresentInDocument) means the assertion that the client will be 'well-positioned for retirement' is unsupported by projected outcomes. The Scottish Widows dual-outcome presentation is a lower-level concern as the recommended course appears identifiable. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability drivers are identified for C1 in the evidence (C2 fields are absent/not applicable as this appears a sole client case), so no material impact on this check. The Standard Life cost contradiction is the dominant finding — a rationale asserting cost saving where the report's own table and derived arithmetic show a cost increase is a High-severity issue under the severity guidance ('rationale that contradicts the case facts').

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts Aviva was selected as provider on five grounds: (1) financial strength – S&P A (Stable) rating, £407bn AUM; (2) fund choice – over 6,900 funds; (3) value – transparent, competitive charges; (4) convenience – online tools; (5) governance – strong regulatory oversight. The wrapper is a Personal Pension (SIPP – Aviva Pension Portfolio). The investment solution is two CIP multi-asset funds: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk 5, 0.18% pa) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk 5, 0.29% pa). Rationale for the funds includes: alignment with risk profile (ATR 5), diversification across asset classes, active/passive blend to keep costs low, client preference for simplicity, competitive charges, peer-relative performance, CIP governance via quarterly investment committee, and suitability as a risk-profiled multi-asset solution linked to OBJ3. Investment pathways were considered and rejected because the client requires a more tailored solution reflecting individual circumstances. The report does not provide a client-specific narrative linking the provider selection to any individual client characteristic; all five provider rationale points are generic.

    File:     The SIPP research report (Defaqto) shows a shortlist of four products: Aviva Platform, Elevate (Aberdeen), Aegon ARC, and Wealthtime Platform. No DNA filters were applied and no additional justification text was entered for the recommended product. The product filter shortlist resulted in 1 from 118 products after filters were applied for the pension product itself (Pension Portfolio – Choice, Aviva). The research report provides performance data for both recommended funds (HSBC and CT Universal MAP) showing positive returns across 1YR, 3YR and 5YR periods with peer-group context. MiFID II ex-ante costs are shown: HSBC 0.18% ongoing, CT Universal MAP 0.29% ongoing, total 0.24% ongoing aggregated. The pension switch report confirms the three plans recommended for consolidation (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) and the receiving plan (Aviva Pension Portfolio – Choice SIPP on Aviva Platform). The policy research notes confirm ATR 5 CIP funds as the investment choice goal and AUM of £116,998.49. The Zurich policy info confirms drawdown is not available on the existing Zurich plan, supporting the transfer rationale. No document records why Elevate, Aegon ARC or Wealthtime were rejected in favour of Aviva Platform; the research report records no DNA analysis and no comparative scoring between the four shortlisted platforms.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts provider selection on five grounds but all are generic marketing claims with no client-specific linkage; the research report corroborates the shortlist of four platforms but records no comparative analysis, no DNA scoring, and no stated reason why the three alternatives (Elevate, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) were rejected in favour of Aviva.

      - The report states 'No additional justification text has been entered' in the research report for the recommended product, meaning the research tool itself carries no provider-specific rationale beyond the shortlist.

      - The report claims 'Value – Transparent, competitive charges with potential tiered pricing' as a provider justification, but no charge comparison between the four shortlisted platforms is present in the evidence to corroborate this claim.

      - The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8 narrative) whereas the charges comparison table (page 11) shows +0.26% impact for Standard Life — these are contradictory and affect the cost-saving rationale for that recommendation.

      - The EA5 charge row in the derived figures is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002.00), rendering the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable.

      - The report asserts 'The funds have performed well in comparison with their peers' — the research report provides cumulative and discrete performance data for both funds showing positive returns, which partially supports this, but no explicit peer-group ranking or benchmark comparison is shown beyond raw returns.

      - The investment pathway rejection rationale states the client 'requires a more tailored solution' and the pathway 'does not reflect individual circumstances, withdrawal requirements and risk profile' — this is partially supported by the client's stated ATR 5 and simplicity preference but the language is largely generic template text.

      - The report records no client-specific reason why the Aviva wrapper (SIPP) was selected over, for example, a standard personal pension or a platform from one of the three other shortlisted providers.

    The evidence supports some elements of the justification but leaves material gaps. On provider selection: the Defaqto research confirms a shortlist of four platforms and that Aviva was selected, but records no comparative DNA analysis, no scoring, and no written reason for rejecting the three alternatives. The report's five provider rationale points are all generic and none is linked to a specific client need or circumstance. The guard acknowledges that firm panel/CIP membership is a legitimate justification if the report says so — the report does reference a CIP and quarterly investment committee for the fund selection, which partially addresses the investment rationale. However, this guard applies to the fund/CIP selection, not to the platform provider selection, where no panel membership is cited. On fund selection: the research report provides performance data and cost disclosures that corroborate the claims of competitive charges and relative performance, and the ATR 5 alignment is confirmed by the policy research notes. The client preference for simplicity and the multi-asset rationale linked to OBJ3 are client-specific touches, though expressed in template language. On the Standard Life cost-saving rationale: the report's page 8 claim of a 0.08% saving is directly contradicted by the page 11 charges table showing +0.26% (an increase), and the derived figures confirm the EA5 charge row uses Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's £3,002, making the comparison arithmetically unreliable. This is a material internal contradiction in a cost-based rationale. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence that would materially alter the assessment of this requirement. Overall: provider justification is asserted without comparative evidence for the three rejected alternatives; the Standard Life cost rationale is internally contradictory; fund rationale is partially supported but largely generic template language. These are Moderate-to-High concerns: the Standard Life cost contradiction is the most serious individual issue.

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The canonical model asserts that all 22 recommendation rationale elements carry isClientSpecific = true, meaning the suitability report claims every rationale is individually tailored to the client's circumstances, objectives and needs.

    File:     The policy research file (P14, P15) records the overall file strategy as consolidation because the client 'has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together'. However, the per-plan research rows for Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension and Scottish Widows leave 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' blank for every plan. The suitability report itself (P13) opens with standard advisory framing and references ATR, needs and objectives generically. The fact find (P5) records the client's goal as organising pensions, consolidating for simplicity, retiring in approximately 2 years, and needing £1,700 net per month household income. There is also an identified ATR override from questionnaire result 4 to agreed rating 5, and a negative monthly cash flow of -£288 (P4). No retrieved passage from the suitability report itself evidences per-recommendation personalised rationale linking each discrete recommendation to a named objective; the research document's objective column is blank for every plan.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts isClientSpecific = true for all 22 rationale elements, but the policy research file leaves the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field blank for every existing arrangement (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Standard Life).

      - The report's claim of personalised rationale is not corroborated by any retrieved passage showing client-specific language linking each recommendation to the client's stated £1,700/month retirement income goal or 2-year retirement horizon.

      - The ATR override from 4 to 5 is flagged as an ambiguity in the extraction; no retrieved passage from the suitability report demonstrates that the rationale for any specific recommendation acknowledges or addresses this override in a personalised way.

      - The client's negative monthly cash flow (-£288, P4) is a material personal circumstance; no retrieved passage evidences that any recommendation rationale addresses this in a personalised manner.

      - The Standard Life charge comparison contains a direct internal contradiction (0.08% saving stated in narrative vs +0.26% increase in the charges table), meaning the cost-saving rationale for that recommendation, if present, would rest on a figure contradicted by the report's own table — undermining the personalised justification for that switch.

      - The cashflow modelling input ambiguity (People's Pension potentially omitted) means any rationale referencing cashflow projections as personalised justification may be built on incomplete client-specific data.

    The report asserts that all 22 rationale elements are client-specific. The evidence pack partially supports the existence of personalised client circumstances (retirement goal, income need of £1,700/month, 2-year horizon, negative cash flow, ATR 5 override) recorded in the fact find. However, the critical weakness is that the policy research document — which is the closest available evidence of how recommendations were constructed — leaves the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field blank for every plan (P15). This is not a formatting gap; it is the field specifically designed to link each recommendation to a client objective, and it is empty across the board. The suitability report narrative (P13) contains a brief opening statement but the retrieved passage does not demonstrate per-recommendation personalised language beyond standard framing. The Standard Life rationale is further undermined by the internal inconsistency between the page 8 narrative (0.08% saving) and the page 11 charges table (+0.26% increase), meaning the cost justification for that specific switch contradicts the report's own data — this is not explained by any guard and constitutes a factual error in the rationale. The guard on standard regulatory wording does not apply here because the concern is absence of personalisation and presence of contradictory figures, not the presence of boilerplate caveats. The vulnerability overlay: the client's negative cash flow (-£288/month) and short retirement horizon (2 years) are characteristics that heighten the importance of personalised, accurate rationale — particularly around costs and charges — and the identified contradictions in the charge comparison are therefore more material in this context. Taken together: the report's assertion of client-specific rationale across all 22 elements is not corroborated by the evidence, the research document's objective linkage fields are uniformly blank, and at least one recommendation (Standard Life) contains a rationale built on a figure the report's own charges table contradicts. This is a Potential Concern of at least Moderate severity, elevated toward High given the contradictory cost rationale for Standard Life.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report presents multiple alternatives considered and rejected: (1) Consolidate into People's Pension — rejected because funds do not meet risk profile, plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution, £4.50 annual admin charge, no multi-asset funds, limited fund range. (2) Leave Scottish Widows pot to grow until retirement — rejected in favour of encashment under small pots rules (estimated £170 net) aligned with consolidation objective. (3) Option 1 — Leave existing pension scheme untouched — rejected because lifestyling funds would move portfolio outside agreed risk range, lower fund choice, no adviser management. (4) Option 2 — Leave existing scheme and switch investment funds internally (Aviva) — rejected because existing Aviva plan does not allow adviser management and limited fund range does not meet risk profile. (5) Option 4 — Switch into People's Pension — same rejection as (1). (6) Stakeholder pension — rejected because recommended plan is more cost-effective, stakeholder pensions typically do not allow flexible access and have limited fund ranges. The report also refers to Scottish Widows as rejected for consolidation (no single multi-asset fund at risk rating 5, would require two funds leaving 20–30% in money market/cash, no adviser management, limited flexible withdrawal options). For Zurich, internal fund switch rejected as no risk level 5 multi-asset funds, blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options, and consolidation is the main priority. For Standard Life, the report states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' as justification for switching. The research options list (OPT1–OPT5) covers: do nothing, internal fund switch, consolidate into existing (Scottish Widows), consolidate into People's Pension, and the recommended new Aviva platform solution.

    File:     The pension switch report (G) confirms research was conducted comparing existing plans to the new Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice on the Aviva Platform. Alternative comparisons were run for each individual plan (Aviva only, Standard Life only, Zurich only). The SIPP research report (G) shows a shortlist of 4 platforms (Aviva Platform, Elevate/Aberdeen, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) with no filters applied and no DNA analysis performed; no additional justification text was entered for the platform selection. The file notes (C) record: Scottish Widows discounted (no servicing, reduced flexibility); Aviva alternative option — only mixed asset Aviva fund is risk rated 4, does not meet ATR, discounted; Zurich alternative — no risk level 5 funds, blend of 7 and 2 used, no multi-asset funds in risk range, blend has not performed as well as Aviva offering, main priority is consolidation; Standard Life alternative — all funds within same range (20–60% equities) are more expensive than Aviva offering. The suitability report itself (I) confirms the stakeholder pension alternative section states the recommended plan is more cost-effective and stakeholder pensions do not allow flexible access or have broad fund ranges. The charges comparison table (internal inconsistency noted in extraction) shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% (an increase), contradicting the report narrative of '0.08% saving'. The switch report (G) shows the Zurich-only comparison mid-growth rate as 2.95% while the report narrative references 2.94% in one place. For the overall comparison (all three plans switching), the new solution projects lower maturity values at all growth rates than the existing pensions. Platform shortlist included 4 providers but no DNA analysis was carried out and no additional justification text was entered for why Aviva Platform was selected over Elevate, Aegon ARC, or Wealthtime.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving from switching), but the charges comparison table in the suitability report shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase in cost, not a saving). These are directly contradictory.

      - The SIPP research report platform shortlist includes 4 platforms (Aviva Platform, Elevate/Aberdeen, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) but no DNA analysis was carried out and no additional justification text was entered for the platform selection. The report does not explain why Aviva Platform was chosen over the other three shortlisted platforms.

      - The pension switch report shows that at all three growth rates the new solution produces a lower projected maturity value than the existing pensions (e.g. mid-growth: existing £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 for the combined comparison including Zurich; Zurich-only mid-growth: existing £127,000 vs new £125,000). The report does not explicitly acknowledge this ranking or explain why switching remains suitable despite the new solution projecting lower returns.

      - The mid-growth rate used in the pension switch report is stated as 2.95% in the Zurich-only and combined comparison tables, but the suitability report narrative states 2.94% in one place. This is a minor but present inconsistency.

      - Option 1 (leave existing scheme untouched) is listed in the alternatives with a rejection reason referencing lifestyling funds and no adviser management, but no rejection reasons appear in the OPT1 entry in the research options list (rejectionReasons array is empty), indicating the rejection rationale is asserted in the report but not corroborated in the structured research record.

      - The report presents two possible outcomes for the Scottish Widows encashment (£170 net now under small pots rules, or full amount tax-free at retirement) without a fully explicit statement of which is definitively recommended, though the £170 net appears to be the recommended course. The ambiguity means the client-specific rationale for rejecting the 'leave to grow' alternative is inferred rather than clearly stated.

      - The Standard Life alternative rejection in the file notes (C) states 'all funds within the same range of 20–60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva', which corroborates switching on cost grounds, but is contradicted by the charges table showing +0.26% (a cost increase) for Standard Life under the recommended solution.

    The report presents a broad set of alternatives with client-specific rejection reasons for most options, and the file notes and pension switch research broadly corroborate the approach. However, several material concerns arise. First, and most seriously, the Standard Life cost rationale is internally contradicted: the report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying switching saves cost), but the charges comparison table in the same report shows +0.26% (a cost increase from switching). The derived figures confirm this: EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438), not Standard Life's (£3,002), meaning the charge row is applied to the wrong plan and the comparison is unreliable. This is a direct contradiction within the report itself and the guard on 'lower costs where charge comparison shows an increase' applies — this is a highest-severity finding. Second, the platform selection: the SIPP research report shortlists four platforms but records no DNA analysis and no additional justification for choosing Aviva Platform over Elevate, Aegon ARC, or Wealthtime. The report does not provide reasons for rejecting these three shortlisted alternatives, which is a gap in the alternatives rationale for the wrapper/platform choice. Third, the pension switch report shows the new solution projects lower maturity values than the existing pensions at all growth rates for the overall comparison. While the report acknowledges costs and switching rationale (consolidation, adviser management, fund range, flexibility), it does not explicitly address this adverse projection ranking or reconcile it with the suitability conclusion. The guard on ranked comparisons placing the recommendation below the existing arrangement applies — this must be reported. Fourth, the Scottish Widows encashment alternative rationale is inferred rather than stated, creating a minor ambiguity. The Aviva and Zurich internal fund switch rejections are well-supported by file notes (C) and the report text. The People's Pension and stakeholder pension rejections are clearly stated and corroborated. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. On balance, the Standard Life cost contradiction, the absent platform comparison rationale, and the unaddressed adverse projection ranking each represent material concerns that prevent a No Issue outcome.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Five existing arrangements are identified: (1) People's Pension – Retain, rationale: current workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions that would be lost on transfer; (2) Scottish Widows – Encash under small pots rules, rationale: funds do not meet risk profile (no single multi-asset fund at risk rating 5), limited retirement options (UFPLS only, no FAD), no adviser management, limited withdrawal flexibility, and small pots encashment recommended with estimated net receipt of £170; (3) Aviva – Switch to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs (0.06% saving), fund switch to align with risk rating 5 suggested, lifestyling concern, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging facilitation; (4) Zurich – Switch to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs, funds do not meet risk profile, lifestyling concern, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging facilitation; (5) Standard Life – Switch to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs (stated as 0.08% saving), lifestyling funds, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility, adviser charging facilitation. All rationales are marked isClientSpecific=true and linked to OBJ3 (and OBJ2 for Scottish Widows). The report also notes an alternative of leaving Scottish Widows to grow to retirement and taking within personal allowance tax-free, but the recommended course is encashment now.

    File:     Policy research (P3, P5, P12) confirms five arrangements exist: Aviva SP50163762, Zurich P10026-719-001/DL, Standard Life D2301334000, People's Pension 6197175/PP, Scottish Widows ZU4225575. The suitability report itself (P21, P23) reproduces the recommendation table and rationale text. P21 shows a provider comparison table listing retirement options: People's Pension (FAD, Full and partial UFPLS), Scottish Widows (Full and partial UFPLS only – no FAD), Aviva (FAD, Full and partial UFPLS), Zurich (Full and partial UFPLS only – no FAD), Standard Life (FAD only). Allows Servicing Adviser: People's Pension No, Scottish Widows No, Aviva No, Zurich No, Standard Life Yes. However, the canonical model's internalInconsistencies flag records that the charges table on page 11 shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% (an increase, not a saving). The derived figures confirm EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable.     Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life cost rationale states '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges table (page 11, per internalInconsistencies) shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% – an increase in cost, not a saving. These are contradictory.

      - Derived figures confirm the EA5 charge rows are calculated on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), meaning the Standard Life cost comparison is arithmetically unreliable regardless of the directional claim.

      - The report's narrative rationale for Aviva switch (page 7) states '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving), but the check notes this is directionally consistent with the charges table showing -0.06% for Aviva – no contradition for Aviva specifically; the discrepancy is confined to Standard Life.

      - The Scottish Widows rationale presents two possible outcomes (encash now for ~£170 net; leave to grow and take tax-free at retirement) without definitively ruling out the second option in a single unambiguous sentence, though the recommended course appears to be encashment now.

      - People's Pension is invested in Global Investments (up to 85% shares) per P13, which is above risk rating 5; the retain rationale does not address the fund mismatch within the retained plan, only the employer contribution benefit.

      - Standard Life policy value per P6 is £2,994.30 (30 Sep 2025) versus £3,002 stated in the report – minor valuation date difference.

    All five existing arrangements carry a stated recommended outcome (Retain, Encash, Switch x3) and each has a rationale linked to at least one client objective. The rationales are client-specific rather than wholly generic: they reference the client's workplace scheme and employer contributions (People's Pension), risk rating 5 and absence of a suitable fund (Scottish Widows), lifestyling concern and risk alignment (Aviva and Zurich), and cost, lifestyling and adviser management (Standard Life). The People's Pension retain rationale is coherent and client-specific; the employer contribution point is a legitimate standalone reason to retain even where the fund may not align with the agreed risk rating – the fund mismatch within the retained plan is noted but does not undermine the outcome recommendation for this check's purposes. The Scottish Widows dual-outcome presentation (P21 ambiguity) is a mild concern but the recommended course (encash now) is identifiable from the rationale text. The most material issue is the Standard Life cost rationale: the report states switching saves 0.08%, but the charges table (page 11) shows a +0.26% cost increase for that arrangement, and the derived figures confirm the EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's value not Standard Life's, making the cost comparison unreliable.
