The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially alter these conclusions. The ATR override (questionnaire 4, agreed 5) is noted in the research (P19) and the rationale references risk rating 5 throughout, which is consistent. Overall: four of five arrangements have sound, supported rationales; the Standard Life recommendation carries a cost-saving rationale that is directly contradicted by the charges table and by arithmetic error in the underlying cost comparison rows.

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Five recommendations are made, all marked as 'Recommended': (1) Retain People's Pension (£6,601.55) — continue employer contributions until retirement; (2) Encash Scottish Widows (£211.32) under small pots rules, proceeds to savings account; (3) Transfer Aviva (£3,744.36) to new Aviva platform — lower costs, investment flexibility, adviser charging, professional oversight; (4) Transfer Zurich (£103,439.24) to new Aviva platform — better investment choice, retirement flexibility, adviser charging, professional oversight aligned to risk profile; (5) Transfer Standard Life (£3,002) to new Aviva platform — lower costs, exit lifestyling, investment choice, retirement flexibility, adviser charging, professional oversight. Implementation: expected timescale 10–15 working days; cancellation rights stated (30 days); client directed to complete nomination of beneficiaries form after transfer process begins. The canonical model records no value for /recommendations[]/implementation/willAdviserImplement or /recommendations[]/implementation/responsibleParty.

    File:     The retrieved passages are predominantly Aviva platform terms and conditions, a risk profile report, and a SIPP research report. None of these passages address who will implement the recommended transfers or encashment. The SIPP research report (P12) confirms agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) and the two recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced, 50/50), supporting the investment strategy element. No passage in the file states that the adviser — as opposed to the client — will implement the changes, nor does any passage confirm implementation responsibility.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report does not state whether the adviser or the client will implement each recommended change (/recommendations[]/implementation/willAdviserImplement and /recommendations[]/implementation/responsibleParty are absent from the canonical model and were not found in the document per the extraction self-report).

      - For REC2 (Scottish Widows encashment), the extraction ambiguity notes two alternative outcomes are presented (£170 net now under small pots rules vs. full amount tax-free at retirement) and states 'only one is recommended' with the £170 net appearing to be the recommended course, but the report presents both without unambiguously selecting one as the single recommended action.

      - The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (cost saving justification for REC5) but the charges table shows +0.26% impact (an increase), making the cost-saving rationale for REC5 internally contradictory — the report claims lower costs as an intended outcome where the evidence within the report itself shows a cost increase.

      - No retirement income projection is present (extraction confirms /needsAnalysis/retirementIncomeProjection absent), limiting client-specific linkage of the recommendations to quantified retirement objectives.

      - C2's assets, liabilities, ATR and capacity for loss are absent from the extracted model, meaning the recommendation rationale cannot be verified as applying to both clients where applicable.

    The check method is PresenceOnly. The question is whether the report states: (a) that changes are recommended; (b) who will implement them; and (c) the intended outcome of each change. On (a): all five recommendations are explicitly marked 'Recommended' — no concern. On (c): each recommendation carries a client-specific intended outcome — no concern. On (b): the extraction self-report confirms /recommendations[]/implementation/willAdviserImplement and /recommendations[]/implementation/responsibleParty are absent from the document. No retrieved passage fills this gap. The guard notes that an implementation step handled by the provider is still a stated step — but the issue here is not who physically acts; it is that the report does not state at all whether the adviser will arrange or implement the changes on the client's behalf or whether the client must act independently. This is a material omission under the requirement. Separately, the Standard Life cost rationale is internally contradictory: the report asserts a saving of 0.08% as justification for REC5, but the charges table within the same report shows +0.26% (a cost increase). This is not a comparison-methodology difference; the derived figures confirm the EA5 charges row is computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's £3,002, rendering the Standard Life comparison unreliable and making the 'lower costs' intended outcome for REC5 unsupported and potentially misleading. The Scottish Widows ambiguity (two outcomes presented without a clear single recommendation) is a lesser but real concern. No vulnerability factors are identified in the retrieved passages that would materially alter this assessment. Taken together, the absence of implementation responsibility and the contradictory cost rationale for REC5 are material concerns warranting a Potential Concern finding at Moderate severity: implementation responsibility is a required disclosure element and the cost rationale contradiction directly undermines the stated intended outcome for one recommendation.

No passage addresses the encashment process for the Scottish Widows small pot or who arranges it.

    Discrepancies:

      - The canonical model records no per-recommendation responsible party (/recommendations[]/implementation/responsibleParty is absent), so it is not possible to confirm from the report who will arrange each transfer or the encashment.

      - The canonical model records no per-recommendation timescale (/recommendations[]/implementation/expectedTimescale is absent); only a single global timescale of 10-15 working days is stated, not mapped to individual recommendations.

      - The encashment of the Scottish Widows plan is noted as having two possible outcomes in the ambiguities (encash now under small pots rules vs leave to grow), and the extraction flags this as an ambiguity; the report does not unambiguously state a single recommended course and who will action it.

      - No passage in the evidence file independently corroborates the 10-15 working day timescale as a provider-stated service standard for transfers into the Aviva Pension Portfolio specifically.

    The report states five clear actions (Retain, Encash, Transfer x3) and a global implementation timescale of 10-15 working days, which is a stated timescale satisfying the guard that a timescale quoted as a provider service standard is a stated timescale. The Aviva T&Cs at [P1] reference a 10 working day standard for income payment changes, which is consistent in tenor but does not directly confirm the transfer timescale. The 'by whom' dimension is not explicitly resolved in the report: the extraction found no responsible party per recommendation, and the canonical model path is absent. However, the check's guard states that naming the adviser firm rather than an individual satisfies 'by whom', and the report is an advised suitability report where it is implicit that the adviser firm will arrange the transfers. The absence of an explicit responsible party per recommendation is a low-level gap rather than a material suitability concern given the context. The Scottish Widows encashment ambiguity (two possible outcomes) is a more meaningful gap: the report presents two alternatives without unambiguously stating which is recommended and who will take action, though the extraction notes the net £170 encashment now appears to be the recommended course. The absence of per-recommendation timescales is noted but the global 10-15 working day statement covers the transfer recommendations. No vulnerability factors in the file appear to make the implementation timing or responsible party materially more significant for this client. Overall, the implementation section is reasonably complete but has a low-level gap on responsible party and a moderate ambiguity on the Scottish Widows encashment outcome and action.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). 1. Investment management charge: report states 0.22% / £253.43, but the Aviva illustration [P8] shows a weighted average OCF/TER of 0.23% across the two funds (CT Universal MAP 0.29%, HSBC 0.18%, weighted average 0.23%). The 0.22% in the report does not match the 0.23% weighted average in the illustration. 2. Standard Life charge comparison contradiction: page 8 of the report states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving), but the charges table on page 11 shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase/more expensive). These are directly contradictory — one implies switching saves cost, the other implies it increases cost. 3. EA5 (Standard Life) charge row in the comparison table is computed on a fund value implying ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's own value of £3,002. The derived figures confirm this: EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a fund value of ~£103,439, not £3,002. This makes the monetary comparison for Standard Life arithmetically unreliable. 33 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report discloses: Platform charge 0.21% / £231.39 pa; Investment Management charge 0.22% / £253.43 pa; Total ongoing charge 0.44% / £484.81 pa (all for the recommended Aviva Platform Personal Pension). Transaction costs are explicitly excluded with a stated reason (variable, already reflected in net performance figures). Initial advice fee of 3% / £3,305.55 deducted from transfer proceeds. Ongoing adviser fee referenced via Client Agreement. Aggregated total of 0.44% is given. The report also presents a charges comparison table (page 11) showing existing plan charges vs recommended plan charges for each switching plan, including: Aviva EA -0.06% saving; Zurich EA impact stated; Standard Life EA described as '0.08% more expensive than recommended solution' on page 8, but shown as +0.26% in the charges table on page 11. The report states the new plan is 0.24% less expensive like-for-like, but 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees, requiring £4,672 outperformance. DFM/discretionary fee is not applicable (not disclosed as false). Cumulative effect is stated as disclosed.

    File:     [P3] The Aviva illustration and suitability report charges table confirms Platform 0.21% / £231.39, Investment Management 0.22% / £253.43, Total 0.44% / £484.81. Transaction cost exclusion language is verbatim in [P3]. [P8] The Aviva illustration confirms the Aviva platform charge at 0.21% for investments above £0, and investment charges based on fund selection: CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc 0.29% OCF, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 0.18% OCF, weighted average 0.23%. [P10] The SIPP research report shows total aggregated fund costs including transaction costs: CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund ongoing 0.29%, transaction costs 0.26%; HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio ongoing 0.18%, transaction costs not shown separately; total aggregated £406.74 (0.37%) including transaction costs of £147.80 (0.13%). [P17] Confirms initial advice fee of 3% / £3,305.55 deducted from transfer proceeds per Client Agreement. [P5] Pension switch report for Standard Life shows existing charge used as 0.00% (charges data N/A), and [P23] shows Aviva existing plan investment charge used as 0.50%. [P20] Standard Life illustration shows fund charge 1.0%, discount -0.5%, net 0.5%. [P7] Zurich policy info shows bid-to-offer spread of 5% and AMC of 0.75% pa. [P1] KIID shows ongoing charge 0.18% (consistent with HSBC fund). [P21] Zurich policy info confirms no transfer value/early retirement charge deduction. The illustration [P8] shows weighted average OCF of 0.23%, which differs from the report's stated investment management charge of 0.22%.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. Investment management charge: report states 0.22% / £253.43, but the Aviva illustration [P8] shows a weighted average OCF/TER of 0.23% across the two funds (CT Universal MAP 0.29%, HSBC 0.18%, weighted average 0.23%). The 0.22% in the report does not match the 0.23% weighted average in the illustration.

      - 2. Standard Life charge comparison contradiction: page 8 of the report states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving), but the charges table on page 11 shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase/more expensive). These are directly contradictory — one implies switching saves cost, the other implies it increases cost.

      - 3. EA5 (Standard Life) charge row in the comparison table is computed on a fund value implying ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's own value of £3,002. The derived figures confirm this: EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a fund value of ~£103,439, not £3,002. This makes the monetary comparison for Standard Life arithmetically unreliable.

      - 4. Transaction costs disclosed in the SIPP research report [P10] include £147.80 (0.13%) for CT Universal MAP, but the report excludes transaction costs with a stated reason. The exclusion is noted, but the research report quantifies them — the report does not reference or estimate this figure even as context.

      - 5. Aviva platform charge monetary figures: £231.39 at 0.21% implies a fund value of £110,185.71; Aviva investment management charge £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195.45 — neither matches the stated transfer value of £110,185. The 0.22% / £253.43 pairing is arithmetically inconsistent (0.22% of £110,185 = £242.41, not £253.43).

      - 6. Total ongoing charge: £484.81 at 0.44% implies a fund value of £110,184.09, broadly consistent with £110,185 transfer value. However, adding £231.39 + £253.43 = £484.82, which rounds to £484.82 not £484.81 (minor rounding).

      - 7. Mid growth rate referred to as 2.94% on page 11 of the report, but critical yield table on page 18 labels it as 2.95% — a marginal inconsistency noted in the extraction.

    The comparison can be made from the available evidence. Several issues arise:

   

    1. INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT CHARGE MISMATCH: The report states 0.22% for investment management, but the Aviva illustration [P8] shows a weighted average OCF of 0.23%. The difference (0.22% vs 0.23%) is small but the illustration is the authoritative source for fund charges and the two do not agree. This is a low-severity discrepancy but is a genuine inconsistency.

   

    2. STANDARD LIFE COST COMPARISON CONTRADICTION (material): The report's page 8 narrative states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', implying switching saves money. The charges comparison table on page 11 shows the impact as +0.26% — meaning the recommended solution is 0.26% MORE expensive for Standard Life. These are directly contradictory and the +0.26% figure is also computed on the wrong fund value (Zurich's ~£103,439 rather than Standard Life's £3,002, per derived figures). This means: (a) the narrative and the table contradict each other on direction of cost saving; (b) the table's monetary figures for Standard Life are arithmetically incorrect because the wrong fund value was used as the basis. The report's statement that switching Standard Life is cost-beneficial cannot be verified and is internally contradicted.

   

    3. AVIVA INVESTMENT MANAGEMENT MONETARY ARITHMETIC: £253.43 at 0.22% implies £115,195, not the stated transfer value of ~£110,185. 0.22% of £110,185 = £242.41. The figure of £253.43 is inconsistent with both the percentage and the fund value stated in the report. This is a moderate-severity arithmetic error.

   

    4. TRANSACTION COSTS: The exclusion of transaction costs is stated with reasons and is a disclosure, not a silent omission — the guard applies. However, the SIPP research report [P10] quantifies transaction costs at 0.13% / £147.80, which the suitability report does not reference even as an estimate. The stated reason (already reflected in net performance) is the conventional exclusion justification, so this is a low concern but the research tool itself has the figure.

   

    5. EA5 BASE VALUE ERROR: The derived figures confirm Standard Life's (EA5) comparison rows are computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own £3,002. Any monetary cost comparison for Standard Life is therefore unreliable.

   

    6. OVERALL COST PICTURE: Platform (0.21%) and total (0.44%) are corroborated by [P3] and [P8]. Initial advice fee (3% / £3,305.55) is corroborated by [P17]. The like-for-like vs including-adviser-fee dual presentation (0.24% saving vs 4.24% more expensive) is present in the report; both figures address different bases and are disclosed, though the presentation could confuse.

   

    VULNERABILITY OVERLAY: No vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially change this assessment. The ATR override (questionnaire 4, agreed 5) does not affect the cost disclosure analysis.

   

    Conclusion: The Standard Life cost comparison contradiction (page 8 narrative vs page 11 table, compounded by the wrong base value being used) is a material internal inconsistency that undermines the stated justification for switching Standard Life. The investment management charge arithmetic (0.22% producing £253.43 rather than ~£242) is a further arithmetic inconsistency. These are genuine findings not explained by any guard.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement

  Potential Concern — severity Low

    Report:   The suitability report asserts an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested, equating to £3,305.55 with current transfer values, to be paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer. An ongoing adviser charge of 1% per annum is stated, equating to circa £1,101.85 for the first year (based on current values and expected contributions, minus the initial fee), to be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme. Services for the ongoing fee include: reviewing ATR and capacity for loss; reviewing ongoing suitability; market updates and legislative changes; access to advisers for ad-hoc advice; and access to full holistic financial planning.

    File:     The client agreement (IMPORT_2.md / TFAS Wealth Client Agreement) confirms an initial advice charge of 3% of the value of the funds advised on (minimum £1,350), consistent with a 3% initial fee. The ongoing annual review management charge is stated as 1% per annum (minimum £100 per annum). The services listed in the client agreement for the ongoing charge include: review meeting at least once a year; statements and performance reports twice per year; cash flow analysis when relevant; family benefit; regular market updates; quarterly e-magazine; online access for portfolio valuations; and access to the adviser as needed. The client agreement states fees are set out in the suitability report and confirmed by subsequent charges confirmation. The Aviva illustration (Illustration - new.md) confirms adviser charges are facilitated through the platform. The value £3,305.55 is corroborated in [P12] and £1,101.85 is corroborated in [P5].

    Discrepancies:

      - The monetary initial fee of £3,305.55 represents 3% of £110,185 (the total transfer value of the three switching plans). However, derived figures show that 3% of the stated transfer value of £110,185 is £3,305.55, which is arithmetically consistent. No discrepancy on quantum.

      - The ongoing fee of £1,101.85 is described as 'minus the initial fee' from current values. 1% of £110,185 would be £1,101.85, which is consistent. No discrepancy on quantum.

      - The matchesClientAgreement field is null in the extracted model — the report does not explicitly state that the fees match the client agreement, it only cross-references it ('as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement'). This is a self-assertion by the report that is not independently verified in the extraction.

      - The suitability report does not disclose the minimum fee threshold (£1,350 initial, £100 ongoing per annum) stated in the client agreement, although these are not breached given the amounts involved.

      - The ongoing fee monetary amount (£1,101.85) is described as based on 'current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee' — the basis for deducting the initial fee from the fund value before computing 1% is not explicitly explained to the client in the report, though arithmetically it is consistent with 1% of (£110,185 − £3,305.55) = £106,879.45 × 1% ≈ £1,068.79, which does not equal £1,101.85. Alternatively, 1% of £110,185 = £1,101.85 exactly, suggesting the 'minus the initial fee' wording may refer to something else or be loosely worded, but the figure itself matches 1% of the gross transfer value.

    The report asserts a 3% initial fee (£3,305.55) and a 1% per annum ongoing fee (£1,101.85). Both percentage rates are corroborated by the client agreement documents (IMPORT_2.md at [P13] and [P3], and TFAS Wealth Client Agreement at [P12]). The monetary amounts are arithmetically consistent: 3% × £110,185 = £3,305.55 exactly, and 1% × £110,185 = £1,101.85 exactly. The figure £3,305.55 is corroborated verbatim in [P12] and £1,101.85 in [P5]. The guard notes that monetary fees move with the transfer value — the report itself acknowledges this ('this figure may fluctuate depending on transfer values'), which is appropriate. The 'minus the initial fee' phrase in the ongoing fee description is loosely worded: if the initial fee is deducted first, 1% of £106,879.45 would be ~£1,068.79, not £1,101.85. In practice the figure of £1,101.85 matches 1% of the gross transfer value (£110,185), suggesting the 'minus the initial fee' language is either inaccurate or refers to the initial fee being taken from the plan before the ongoing fee basis is established in some other way. This is a minor presentation inconsistency rather than a material overcharge. The matchesClientAgreement field being null means the extraction did not find an explicit confirmation in the report that fees match the agreement — the report references the client agreement but does not confirm explicit alignment. The client agreement at [P12] states fees are set out in the suitability report, creating a circular cross-reference rather than an independent corroboration. However, since both rate figures are directly corroborated in the client agreement documents and the monetary amounts are arithmetically consistent with those rates applied to the stated fund value, there is no material concern on quantum. No vulnerability indicators in the file are identified as materially affecting the fee disclosure assessment. The minor loose wording around 'minus the initial fee' in the ongoing fee calculation basis is a low-level concern but does not constitute a material misstatement.

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report (page 18, Critical Yield section) states the following RIY and effect-of-switching figures at age 75: Low growth (0.00%): Existing RIY 0.3%, Recommended RIY 2.1%, Critical yield 1.9%, Effect of switching £10,400 / 9.6% decrease. Mid growth (2.95%): Existing RIY 0.3%, Recommended RIY 2.2%, Critical yield 4.8%, Effect of switching £12,000 / 9.4% decrease. High growth (5.88%): Existing RIY 0.3%, Recommended RIY 2.3%, Critical yield 7.8%, Effect of switching £15,000 / 10.0% decrease. The report narrative (page 11) also states a mid-growth rate of 2.94% and confirms the critical yield at mid growth is 4.8%. The report discloses that an initial adviser charge of £3,305 and an ongoing charge of 1.00% p.a. have been factored into the new-solution projections. Page 11 states that including adviser fees the new plan would need to outperform by £4,672 (4.24% more expensive). Page 11 also states the new plan is 0.24% less expensive on a like-for-like basis (underperformance tolerance £264).

    File:     [P1] (suitability report appendix table, the canonical source itself) reproduces all RIY, critical yield and effect-of-switching figures and is fully consistent with the canonical model. [P10] (Aviva illustration) shows the reduction in growth rate after all charges at the medium growth scenario as 2.1% (from 2.9% to 0.8%), which corresponds to the recommended-plan RIY of 2.1% at low growth in the suitability report — though the illustration uses a medium rate of 5% / real 2.9%, not the 2.95% label used in the critical yield table. [P3] (Aviva illustration fund values) shows at medium growth the fund could be worth £115,000 at age 75, consistent with the report's £115,000 projected value at mid growth. At low growth the illustration shows £97,500, consistent with the report's £97,600. At high growth the illustration shows £135,000, consistent with the report's £135,000. [P21]/[P22]/[P23] (Defaqto pension switch report) provide an independent comparison. At low growth the Defaqto table shows: Existing RIY 0.3%, New solution RIY 0.6%, Critical yield 0.3%, Effect of switching £2,000 / 1.9% decrease. At mid growth (2.95%): Existing RIY 0.3%, New solution RIY 0.6%, Critical yield 3.2%, Effect of switching £2,000 / 1.6% decrease. At high growth (5.88%): Existing RIY 0.3%, New solution RIY 0.6%, Critical yield 6.2%, Effect of switching £3,000 / 2.0% decrease. These Defaqto figures are materially different from the suitability report figures. [P11] (narrative page 11 of suitability report) states 2.94% mid-growth rate; the critical yield table in [P1] labels it 2.95%. The derived figures note that the EA5 (Standard Life) row in the charges comparison table is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438), not Standard Life's own value of £3,002, making the Standard Life charge-impact row unreliable. The internal inconsistency note flags that the Standard Life recommendation rationale states '0.08% saving' while the charges table shows '+0.26%' — a direct contradiction within the report on cost-saving claims underpinning the cumulative impact analysis.

    Discrepancies:

      - Recommended-plan RIY at low growth: report states 2.1%; Defaqto switch report [P22]/[P23] states 0.6%.

      - Recommended-plan RIY at mid growth: report states 2.2%; Defaqto switch report states 0.6%.

      - Recommended-plan RIY at high growth: report states 2.3%; Defaqto switch report states 0.6%.

      - Critical yield at low growth: report states 1.9%; Defaqto switch report states 0.3%.

      - Critical yield at mid growth: report states 4.8%; Defaqto switch report states 3.2%.

      - Critical yield at high growth: report states 7.8%; Defaqto switch report states 6.2%.

      - Effect of switching at low growth: report states £10,400 / 9.6% decrease; Defaqto switch report states £2,000 / 1.9% decrease.

      - Effect of switching at mid growth: report states £12,000 / 9.4% decrease; Defaqto switch report states £2,000 / 1.6% decrease.

      - Effect of switching at high growth: report states £15,000 / 10.0% decrease; Defaqto switch report states £3,000 / 2.0% decrease.

      - Mid-growth rate label: narrative page 11 states 2.94%; critical yield table [P1] states 2.95%.

      - Standard Life (EA5) charge-impact row: report narrative page 8 states new plan is 0.08% cheaper; charges table page 11 shows +0.26% (new plan more expensive). These are contradictory within the report itself.

      - EA5 charge arithmetic: derived figures confirm that the EA5 row monetary charges (£186.19 at 0.18% and £268.94 at 0.26%) imply Zurich's fund value (~£103,439), not Standard Life's own value of £3,002, meaning the Standard Life comparison row is computed on the wrong plan value.

      - Aviva illustration RIY of 2.1% (reduction in growth from 2.9% to 0.8%, [P10]) is labelled against a medium growth scenario using a 5% / real 2.9% rate, whereas the suitability report uses a low-growth 0.00% scenario for its 2.1% RIY figure — the labels are not directly aligned, though the numerical match is coincidental.

      - Required outperformance £4,672 at 4.24%: derived figures show this implies a fund value of £110,188.68, which does not match any stated arrangement value or total precisely (closest is the transfer value total of £110,185.60 — a minor rounding difference of ~£3).

    The primary corroborating documents for cumulative cost impact are [P1] (the suitability report's own appendix table, which is the canonical source) and [P21]/[P22]/[P23] (the Defaqto pension switch report, the independent research tool used to generate the switching analysis). The suitability report's RIY figures for the recommended solution range from 2.1% to 2.3% across growth scenarios; the Defaqto report shows only 0.6% across all scenarios for the recommended solution. The effect-of-switching figures differ by an order of magnitude: the suitability report shows £10,400–£15,000 decreases, while the Defaqto report shows only £2,000–£3,000 decreases. The critical yield figures also differ materially (e.g. 4.8% vs 3.2% at mid growth). These are not rounding differences or basis differences — they represent fundamentally different conclusions about the cumulative cost impact of the recommendation. The most likely explanation is that the suitability report's critical yield figures incorporate the 1.00% ongoing adviser charge (as disclosed in [P17] and [P23]), while the Defaqto comparison summary was run before the effect of advice charges. The Defaqto report does separately model advice charges in its 'effect of advice charges' section ([P23]), but the base comparison table figures without advice charges would account for much of the gap: the recommended plan RIY at 0.6% plus 1.00% adviser charge = 1.6%, still below the report's 2.1%–2.3%. The residual difference could reflect different fund charge assumptions or the fact that the Defaqto report uses a weighted average 0.52% investment charge ([P21]) versus the suitability report's 0.22% ([P19]). However, the report does not reconcile these figures or explain why they differ from the Defaqto research tool output. This creates a material concern: a client reading both the suitability report and the Defaqto switch report would see contradictory pictures of cumulative cost impact. The Standard Life EA5 issue is a separate but compounding concern: the derived figures confirm beyond doubt that the EA5 charge row is computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own £3,002, making the per-plan cost comparison for Standard Life arithmetically impossible on its own stated values. This is independently flagged as a highest-severity issue under the decision logic. Additionally, the internal contradiction between the page 8 narrative (Standard Life is 0.08% cheaper after switch) and the charges table (Standard Life switch results in +0.26% cost increase) means the report simultaneously claims a cost saving and a cost increase for the same plan — a direct contradiction within the report that cannot be attributed to a legitimate guard. The mid-growth rate discrepancy (2.94% narrative vs 2.95% table) is a minor rounding issue. The £4,672 / £110,188.68 fund-value implied by required outperformance is only ~£3 away from the transfer total of £110,185.60, a rounding difference that is not material. The Aviva illustration [P3] projected values (£97,500 low, £115,000 mid, £135,000 high) are consistent with the suitability report's projected values (£97,600 / £115,000 / £135,000) to within £100 — a rounding difference only. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the passed evidence that would materially alter this cost-disclosure finding beyond noting that a vulnerable client would be less able to reconcile contradictory cost figures across documents. Overall: the cumulative impact section is present and the report discloses initial and ongoing adviser charges, RIY, critical yield and effect-of-switching at three growth rates. However, (1) the report's RIY and effect-of-switching figures are materially inconsistent with the Defaqto switch report without explanation; (2) the EA5 Standard Life charge row is computed on the wrong fund value, making it arithmetically impossible; (3) the report simultaneously asserts a cost saving and a cost increase for Standard Life. These are Potential Concerns at High/Highest severity.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report (page 11, 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment') presents a per-arrangement comparison table: EA3 (Aviva existing) 0.50% / £18.72 pa, impact -0.06% / -£2.25; EA4 (Zurich existing) 0.93% / £961.98 pa, impact -0.49% / -£506.85; EA5 (Standard Life existing) 0.18% / £186.19 pa, impact +0.26% / +£268.94. On a like-for-like basis the Aviva new plan is stated to be 0.24% less expensive, with a permitted underperformance tolerance of £264 pa. Including adviser fees the new plan is 4.24% more expensive, requiring £4,672 outperformance. The page 8 narrative states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving on switching). The new plan charges are: platform 0.21% / £231.39, investment management 0.22% / £253.43, total 0.44% / £484.81. Transaction costs are excluded with an explanation. Critical yield at mid-growth (described as 2.94% on page 11) is 4.8%.

    File:     [P3] (suitability report pages 11 charges tables) corroborates the per-arrangement figures: EA3 0.50% / £18.72 / -0.06% / -£2.25; EA4 0.93% / £961.98 / -0.49% / -£506.85; EA5 0.18% / £186.19 / +0.26% / +£268.94; new plan 0.21% / £231.39 / 0.22% / £253.43 / 0.44% / £484.81; £264 underperformance tolerance. [P1] confirms EA3 (Aviva) existing charge 0.50%, EA5 (Standard Life) 0.18% / 0.52% blended. [P8] (Standard Life illustration) shows fund charge 1.0% less discount 0.5% = net 0.5%, not 0.18% as stated in the report's comparison table. [P2] and [P16] (Zurich policy info) show charges including a 5% bid-offer spread, 0.75% AMC and other charges; the report uses 0.93% for Zurich. [P6] (pension switch report) shows an investment charge of 0.18% used in the switch report calculation. [P21] (critical yield table) labels the mid growth rate as 2.95%, while [P19] (narrative) states 2.94%. Derived figures confirm: EA5 annual charge £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of £103,438.89 — which is Zurich's value (£103,439.24), not Standard Life's value of £3,002; and EA5 impact £268.94 at 0.26% similarly implies £103,438.46 — again Zurich's value. The Standard Life row in the comparison table is therefore computed on the wrong plan value. Additionally the page 8 narrative says Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving) while the table on page 11 shows the impact as +0.26% (an increase in cost on switching).

    Discrepancies:

      - EA5 (Standard Life) existing annual charge £186.19 at 0.18%: derived figures show this implies a fund value of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's fund value (EA4), not Standard Life's £3,002. The Standard Life row appears to have been computed on the wrong plan value, making the monetary comparison for that row unreliable.

      - EA5 (Standard Life) impact amount £268.94 at 0.26%: derived figures show this also implies ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), confirming the row is based on the wrong fund value.

      - Page 8 narrative states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying a saving on switch), but the charges table on page 11 shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase in cost). These are contradictory.

      - Standard Life illustration [P8] shows a net fund charge of 0.5% (1.0% gross less 0.5% discount), whereas the report's comparison table uses 0.18% for Standard Life's existing charge. These figures do not reconcile.

      - Mid-growth rate label: page 11 narrative states 2.94%, but the critical yield table on page 21/page 18 labels it 2.95%. Minor inconsistency.

      - New plan monetary charges (platform £231.39 at 0.21%; investment management £253.43 at 0.22%; total £484.81 at 0.44%) imply a fund value of ~£110,185–£115,195 depending on the line, not a single consistent base — the platform and IMC lines imply different fund values, indicating the charges are not computed on the same base.

      - The like-for-like saving of 0.24% / £264 and the including-adviser-fees cost of 4.24% / £4,672 are both presented without explicit reconciliation to clarify which figure a client should rely on for value assessment; however, each is labelled on its own basis so this is a presentation matter rather than an arithmetic error.

    The most material finding is that the EA5 (Standard Life) row in the charges comparison table has been computed on the wrong fund value. The derived figures establish unambiguously that £186.19 at 0.18% and £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a fund of ~£103,439 — which is Zurich's current value (EA4), not Standard Life's £3,002. This means the monetary impact figures for Standard Life in the switch charges table are arithmetically impossible against Standard Life's own fund value and are instead Zurich's numbers transposed into the wrong row. This makes the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable as presented. This is compounded by a direct narrative contradiction: page 8 asserts the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving justifying the switch), while the table on page 11 shows +0.26% (an additional cost). The guard on internal consistency applies: where the report states the same plan's charge in two places and they contradict each other, the whole comparison for that arrangement is unreliable. The Standard Life illustration [P8] adds a further dimension: it shows a net charge of 0.5% (1.0% fund charge less 0.5% discount), which does not match the 0.18% used in the report's table. The 0.18% figure appears in the pension switch research report [P6] but is not corroborated by the Standard Life policy documentation as the existing plan charge. For EA3 (Aviva) and EA4 (Zurich), the percentage and monetary figures are internally consistent (derived figures confirm 0.50%/£18.72 on £3,744 and 0.93%/£961.98 on £103,439), and the like-for-like saving claims for those two arrangements are directionally coherent. The minor 2.94%/2.95% growth rate discrepancy is noted but is low severity. The new plan charge lines (platform vs IMC) implying slightly different base values is a rounding/methodology issue acknowledged in the report's own footnote. The vulnerability overlay: there is no indication in the available passages of vulnerability characteristics that would specifically amplify the impact of these cost disclosure issues beyond their general suitability significance. Overall, the EA5 row computed on the wrong fund value, combined with the contradictory narrative on whether switching Standard Life saves or costs money, constitutes a material concern: the cost comparison supporting the Standard Life switch recommendation is arithmetically incorrect and internally contradictory.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts six benefits: (1) Lower Costs — 'the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension'; (2) Greater Investment Choice; (3) Adviser Charging Facilitation; (4) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments; (5) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility; (6) Aviva rated A (Stable) by S&P with £407bn AUM. For cost justification the report states: on a like-for-like basis the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive (£264 underperformance tolerance); including adviser fees it is 4.24% more expensive (£4,672 outperformance required in year one). It states the critical yield including adviser fees requires 4.8% p.a. growth to age 75 against a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, and asserts this is achievable based on past performance of recommended funds. For Standard Life specifically (page 8), the report states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'. The charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact for Standard Life as +0.26% — i.e. the new plan is more expensive for Standard Life, directly contradicting the page 8 narrative.

    File:     The SIPP research report (P3) confirms the product is Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice. The suitability report performance table (P1) shows the recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio (B) has 5-year cumulative performance of 42.40% (research report P3 shows CT Universal MAP 44.73% and HSBC Global Strategy 40.06% over 5 years), against existing Aviva My Future Focus (A) at 15.58% and Zurich Managed AP (D) at 44.32% (P7). The file notes (P19) confirm Standard Life funds in the 20-60% equities range are 'more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva', which supports a cost saving for Standard Life — but the charges table extracted from the report itself shows +0.26% for Standard Life (an increase), directly contradicting both the page 8 narrative and the file note. The derived figures confirm: EA5 annual charge is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) not Standard Life's (£3,002), rendering the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically unreliable. The derived figures also confirm that the critical yield of 4.8% must be achieved against a stated mid-growth rate of 2.94%/2.95% — a hurdle of approximately 185 basis points above the mid scenario. The performance evidence shows the recommended funds (B Aviva Risk 5 / CT Universal MAP / HSBC Global Strategy) have not outperformed Zurich Managed AP (44.32% vs 42.40% / 44.73% / 40.06% over 5 years). The cashflow modeller (P23) excludes the Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life pensions from the proposed scenario (marked EXCLUDED FROM SCENARIO), so no cashflow-based projected outcome supports the benefit assertions. No provider illustration with projected values or RIY figures is present in the retrieved passages.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report page 8 states Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%); the charges comparison table on page 11 shows the impact for Standard Life as +0.26% (the new plan is more expensive) — these are directly contradictory.

      - Derived figures confirm EA5 existing annual charge and EA5 impact charge are both computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) not Standard Life's fund value (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically wrong and unreliable.

      - Report asserts benefit (1) 'Lower Costs — the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure' as a general benefit for all transfers; however, including adviser fees the plan is 4.24% more expensive and the charges table shows Standard Life transfer results in a +0.26% cost increase — the lower-cost claim is inconsistent with the plan-level data.

      - Report states critical yield of 4.8% is achievable 'based on past performance of recommended funds'; the 5-year performance of the recommended Aviva Risk 5 fund (42.40%) has not outperformed the Zurich Managed AP (44.32%) which is one of the funds being transferred away — the evidence does not straightforwardly support achievability.

      - Mid-growth rate cited as 2.94% on page 11 but 2.95% in the critical yield table on page 18 — minor inconsistency in the same document.

      - No provider illustration with projected values, RIY or cumulative cost impact is present in the retrieved passages — the report references an enclosed illustration but no such document is in evidence.

      - Transaction costs are noted as absent from the canonical model (expectedButAbsent path /costsAndCharges/transactionCostsMonetary) with no estimate or explanation provided in the retrieved passages.

      - Cashflow modeller (P23) excludes Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life pensions from the proposed scenario, so no cashflow projection corroborates the benefit and value assertions for those plans.

    The central benefit claim — 'Lower Costs' — is directly contradicted by the report's own charges table for Standard Life, which shows +0.26% rather than the -0.08% stated in the recommendation rationale. The derived figures demonstrate this is not a rounding issue: the EA5 rows in the charges comparison were computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), producing a materially wrong basis for the Standard Life cost comparison. This is an internal arithmetic error that undermines the stated justification for transferring Standard Life. For the overall recommendation the report does acknowledge that including adviser fees the plan is 4.24% more expensive and requires £4,672 outperformance in year one, which is transparent. However, the achievability assertion rests on past performance: the recommended funds (Aviva Risk 5, 42.40% over 5 years) have not outperformed the Zurich Managed AP being transferred away (44.32% over 5 years). While the report explains this is due to Zurich holding higher-risk funds outside the client's agreed ATR 5, the achievability claim is therefore not straightforwardly supported by performance data — consistent with the guard that requires supporting performance data before accepting such a claim. The file note (P19) does confirm Standard Life funds are more expensive than the Aviva offering, which directionally supports the switch, but this is contradicted by the charges table in the report itself. No provider illustration is in the evidence pack to verify projected values or RIY. Transaction costs are silently omitted with no estimate or stated reason for exclusion. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved passages that materially affect this cost/benefit assessment beyond noting the client's limited disposable income (negative monthly cashflow of -£288 per month per P14), which makes the cost burden of the 4.24% higher all-in charge more material — this is not addressed in the benefit/cost assessment. Taken together, the contradiction between the Standard Life cost narrative and the charges table, the arithmetic error in the EA5 rows, the absence of a provider illustration, and the silent omission of transaction costs constitute material concerns.

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   On a like-for-like basis (excluding adviser fees) the recommended Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than the current plans. Including the adviser fee, the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than the current plans, implying a required outperformance of £4,672 per annum. The report presents both figures on page 11. The Standard Life plan is described in the recommendation rationale (page 8) as '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', but the charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact of switching Standard Life as +0.26% — a cost increase, not a saving. EA5 (Standard Life) annual charge figures in the table are computed on a fund value consistent with Zurich's current value (£103,438.xx) rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002, rendering that row arithmetically unreliable. The mid growth rate used is stated as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative and 2.95% in the page 18 critical yield table.

    File:     This requirement is assessed from the report alone. No supporting passages were retrieved. The derived figures confirm: (a) the required outperformance of £4,672 at 4.24% implies a fund value of £110,188.68, broadly consistent with the £110,185 new plan transfer value; (b) EA5 annual charge £186.19 at 0.18% and EA5 impact £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a fund value of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's value not Standard Life's £3,002 — the EA5 row is computed on the wrong plan; (c) the Standard Life narrative on page 8 claims a 0.08% saving while the table on page 11 shows +0.26% cost increase for that plan — a direct contradiction.

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life (EA5) recommendation rationale (page 8) states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — a cost increase. These figures are contradictory.

      - EA5 annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's current value, not Standard Life's current value of £3,002. The EA5 row in the charges table appears to have been computed on the wrong plan's fund value.

      - EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% also implies a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich), not £3,002 (Standard Life), confirming the EA5 comparison row is unreliable.

      - Mid growth rate is stated as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative but 2.95% in the page 18 critical yield table — a marginal inconsistency.

      - The report presents both a 0.24% cost saving (like-for-like) and a 4.24% cost increase (including adviser fees) without clearly reconciling these for the client, and the monetary difference jumps from £264 to £4,672.

    The primary concern is the contradiction between the Standard Life recommendation rationale and the charges comparison table. Page 8 asserts switching Standard Life saves 0.08% (i.e. the existing plan is more expensive), while page 11 shows +0.26% — meaning the recommended solution is more expensive for Standard Life. The derived figures independently confirm the EA5 row is computed on a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value) rather than Standard Life's £3,002, meaning every percentage and monetary figure in that row is wrong by approximately 34-fold in fund-value terms. This is not a rounding difference or formatting artefact — it is an arithmetic impossibility against Standard Life's stated plan value of £3,002. The 4.24% total cost increase including adviser fees is arithmetically consistent with the new plan transfer value of ~£110,185 (implied fund £110,188.68 vs stated £110,185 — rounding only). The like-for-like saving of 0.24%/£264 contrasted with the 4.24%/£4,672 adviser-fee-inclusive increase is presented in the report, but the report does not reconcile the two figures in a way that would help a client understand the net position. The 2.94% vs 2.95% mid-growth-rate discrepancy is minor. The high-severity finding is the EA5 charges row computed on the wrong fund value, producing a directly contradictory cost narrative for Standard Life. No vulnerability indicators have been identified in the evidence pack that would separately affect this assessment, though the complexity of the cost disclosure is relevant for any client with lower financial sophistication.

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The canonical model holds no value at /recommendations[]/alternativesConsidered[]/comparativeCost. However, the suitability report (passages P7, P10, P11) makes the following assertions per plan: (1) Aviva existing plan — 'Lower Costs' cited, the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure, 'The plan itself is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution'; (2) Standard Life — 'Lower Costs' cited, 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'; (3) Zurich — no cost saving claimed; benefits cited are investment choice, multi-asset fund limitations, retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation and ongoing monitoring. The charges table in the suitability report (P8) shows new plan total: 0.44% / £484.81. The charges comparison table (P8) shows: Aviva existing 0.50% / £18.72, impact -0.06% / -£6.56; Standard Life existing 0.18% / £186.19, impact +0.26% / £268.94; Zurich existing 0.93% / £961.98, impact -0.49% / -£477.17. On a like-for-like basis the new plan is 0.24% less expensive / £264 underperformance tolerance. Including adviser fees it is 4.24% more expensive / £4,672 outperformance required. For Standard Life specifically, page 8 of the report states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving), but the charges table shows +0.26% impact (i.e. the new plan is more expensive for Standard Life).

    File:     P8 (suitability report charges table): Aviva existing 0.50% / £18.72; Standard Life existing 0.18% / £186.19, impact +0.26% / £268.94; Zurich existing 0.93% / £961.98, impact -0.49% / -£477.17. P10 (suitability report, Standard Life section): 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution.' P7 (suitability report, Aviva section): lower costs cited. P11 (suitability report, Zurich section): no cost saving claimed; non-cost benefits cited. P18 (file notes): 'Standard Life – Alternative option: Discounted as all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva.' P2/P5 (pension switch report): projected maturity value comparisons provided per plan. Derived figures: EA5 (Standard Life) existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of £103,438.89, which is Zurich's current value — not Standard Life's (£3,002.00); EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% similarly implies Zurich's value. The Standard Life row in the charges comparison table appears to have been computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002.00.

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life narrative (P10) states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving for the client), but the charges comparison table (P8) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase, meaning the new plan is more expensive). These are directly contradictory.

      - The derived figures confirm that the Standard Life (EA5) existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and the EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a fund value of approximately £103,439 — Zurich's fund value — not Standard Life's own value of £3,002. The Standard Life row in the charges table therefore appears to have been computed on the wrong plan's fund value, rendering the monetary comparison for Standard Life unreliable.

      - The like-for-like cost comparison (0.24% less expensive / £264 underperformance tolerance) and the all-in comparison including adviser fees (4.24% more expensive / £4,672 outperformance required) are both presented in the report without reconciliation, creating a material risk that a client reading only one figure would be misled about the true cost impact.

      - Transaction costs are excluded from the charges disclosure. The report acknowledges this but states they are 'accounted for in the fund's performance figures' — no monetary estimate is provided.

      - The canonical model path /recommendations[]/alternativesConsidered[]/comparativeCost is absent, meaning no structured comparative cost for alternatives considered (and rejected) is recorded in the extracted model.

    The check can be substantially performed on the available evidence. The report does present cost comparisons and non-cost benefit justifications, so this is not a case of complete silence. However, several material concerns arise. First, and most seriously, the Standard Life narrative (P10) states the new plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' — meaning the existing Standard Life plan is more expensive, implying a cost saving — while the charges comparison table (P8) shows +0.26% impact, meaning the new plan is more expensive than Standard Life. These are directly contradictory statements within the same document. This is not a rounding difference; the direction of the cost comparison is reversed. The derived figures confirm why: the Standard Life row has been computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002). This means the monetary figures in the Standard Life row of the charges table are arithmetically wrong, and the narrative figure of 0.08% cannot be verified as correct either. Any cost justification for the Standard Life switch therefore rests on unreliable arithmetic. Second, the all-in cost including adviser fees (4.24% more expensive / £4,672 outperformance required) is a material cost increase. The report does present non-cost benefits to justify this — investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, retirement flexibility, ongoing monitoring — and the pension switch research report (P2, P5) provides projected maturity value comparisons. The file notes (P18) support the Standard Life rejection on cost grounds ('all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive'). However, given the arithmetic error in the Standard Life charges row, the cost justification for that specific switch is not robustly supported. Third, transaction costs are excluded with a stated reason (variable, reflected in net performance figures) but no monetary estimate is given — this is a Moderate-level gap per the severity guidance. The guard 'A cheaper option rejected for evidenced non-cost reasons' applies to Zurich (no cost saving claimed; non-cost benefits well-evidenced) and partially to the overall recommendation given the adviser fee overlay. However, the direct contradiction between the Standard Life narrative and the table, compounded by the wrong-fund arithmetic, is not explained by any guard and stands as a genuine finding. No vulnerability indicators have been identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment beyond the general need for clear cost disclosure.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report (page 18) states the following critical yield / RIY table for the combined switch (all three plans into the new Aviva Pension Portfolio): Low growth (0.00%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%, effect of switching £10,400 / 9.6% decrease. Mid growth (2.95%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%, effect of switching £12,000 / 9.4% decrease. High growth (5.88%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%, effect of switching £15,000 / 10.0% decrease. The report also states (page 11 narrative) a mid growth rate of 2.94% and that the plan must grow by 4.8% to match the existing value.

    File:     Passage [P2] (pension switch report) contains a table headed with the same three plans switching to the new Aviva solution and shows: Low growth (0.00%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%, effect of switching £10,400 / 9.6% decrease. Mid growth (2.95%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%, effect of switching £12,000 / 9.4% decrease. High growth (5.88%): existing RIY 0.3%, recommended RIY 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%, effect of switching £15,000 / 10.0% decrease. Passage [P17] reproduces the same table from the suitability report itself. Passage [P16] quotes '2.94%' as the mid growth rate in the narrative and '4.8%' as the critical yield. Passage [P13] (new Aviva illustration) shows an all-charges RIY of 2.1% at the 2.9% (real) growth rate, consistent with the recommended RIY figure. Passage [P1] (switch report) contains a separate table — apparently for a different combination of plans — showing lower RIY figures for the recommended solution (0.6% at all growth rates) and much lower effects of switching (e.g. £2,000 / 1.9% at low growth), with critical yields of 0.3% / 3.2% / 6.0%. The mid growth rate label in [P2] reads 2.95% while [P16] narrative reads 2.94%.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report narrative (page 11, [P16]) states the mid growth rate as 2.94%, while the critical yield table in the report (page 18, [P17]) and in the switch report ([P2]) label it as 2.95% — a marginal inconsistency noted in the internal inconsistencies log.

      - Passage [P1] of the switch report contains a table for what appears to be a different combination of plans (possibly Aviva-only or a subset) that shows a recommended RIY of only 0.6% at all growth rates, critical yields of 0.3% (low), 3.2% (mid) and 6.0% (high), and effects of switching of only £2,000 at low and mid growth — materially different from the figures the suitability report relies upon. The suitability report does not reference or reconcile this alternative table.

      - The switch report ([P1]) projects the existing pension value at low growth as £108,000 and the new solution as £106,000 (£2,000 gap), whereas the suitability report ([P17]) projects the existing as £108,000 and the new solution as £97,600 (£10,400 gap) — the projected new-solution values differ materially between the two documents at the same growth rate.

      - The switch report ([P1]) projects the existing pension value at mid growth as £127,000 and the new solution as £125,000, whereas the suitability report ([P17]) shows £127,000 existing and £115,000 new solution.

      - The switch report ([P1]) projects the existing pension value at high growth as £150,000 and the new solution as £147,000, whereas the suitability report ([P17]) shows £150,000 existing and £135,000 new solution.

    The suitability report's critical yield / RIY table is internally consistent and is corroborated exactly by passages [P2] and [P17], which appear to be the 'including adviser fees' version of the switch analysis. The 2.94% vs 2.95% discrepancy between the narrative and the table label is a rounding artefact and does not affect the critical yield figures themselves; it is already recorded as an internal inconsistency and represents only a minor labelling difference.

   

    However, passage [P1] of the same pension switch report contains a different table — showing the same three plans switching to the same Aviva solution — but with the recommended RIY at only 0.6% (versus 2.1%–2.3% in the suitability report), critical yields of 0.3% / 3.2% / 6.0% (versus 1.9% / 4.8% / 7.8%), and effects of switching of only £2,000 / 1.9% (low), £2,000 / 1.6% (mid) and £3,000 / 2.0% (high). The earlier internal inconsistency note confirms that the suitability report separately shows a 'like-for-like' position of 0.24% cheaper and an 'including adviser fees' position of 4.24% more expensive. This strongly suggests that [P1] is the like-for-like (product/platform/investment charges only) version while [P2] is the including-adviser-fee version. The suitability report relies on and reproduces the [P2] figures. The existence of the [P1] table is material because a client reading the switch report appendix would encounter both tables without a clear signpost distinguishing them; the report itself does not explain why two different sets of critical yield / RIY / effect-of-switching figures appear in the same supporting document. The suitability report relies on the higher-cost (including adviser fee) scenario as its primary disclosure, which is correct, but the presence of the lower-cost table without reconciliation creates a material risk of client confusion and means the disclosure is not unambiguous.

   

    The derived figures note that several charge percentages imply fund values referencing Zurich's current value rather than the plan actually labelled (EA5 / Standard Life), indicating that the underlying switch report calculation may have been run against the wrong base value for that plan. This affects the reliability of the RIY and critical yield comparison for Standard Life specifically.

   

    The new Aviva illustration ([P13]) shows an all-charges RIY of 2.1% (real, at a 2.9% real growth rate), which is consistent with the recommended RIY at low growth in the report.

   

    Vulnerability overlay: No specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence that would materially change this assessment.

   

    In summary: the report's core critical yield figures match the supporting switch report table in [P2]. However, the switch report also contains a second, materially different table ([P1]) that the suitability report does not acknowledge, reconcile or explain to the client. This is a Potential Concern of Moderate severity.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge

  Potential Concern — severity Low

    Report:   The suitability report (page 17) states that the ongoing adviser charge is 1% per annum, estimated at circa £1,101.85 for the first year based on current values, paid via the Aviva Platform. The services included are: (1) Reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss; (2) Reviewing the ongoing suitability of the investment portfolio; (3) Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation; (4) Access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers at any time for ad-hoc advice; (5) Access to full holistic financial planning. No review frequency is specified in the report (recorded as 'Unspecified' in the extracted model).

    File:     The client agreement (TFAS Wealth Client Agreement Privacy.md) confirms an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (subject to a minimum fee of £100), automatically taken from funds invested. The 'Important Information' document (IMPORT_2.md) describes the on-going advice and review service as including: a review meeting at least once a year (with possible bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and ATR); statements and performance reports twice per year; cash flow analysis when relevant; family benefit (adviser fee-free advice for direct family members for ISA or pension set up, plus up to three hours advice for children); regular market updates; quarterly e-magazine; online access for portfolio valuations; access to adviser as needed. The charge is confirmed at 1% per annum with a minimum of £100. The client agreement states fees are set out in the suitability report.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report does not specify a review frequency (recorded as 'Unspecified'), whereas the client agreement/service document specifies 'at least annually' with a review meeting at least once a year.

      - The suitability report omits several service elements that the client agreement document lists: statements and performance reports twice per year; cash flow analysis when relevant; family benefit (fee-free advice for direct family members); quarterly e-magazine; online access for portfolio valuations. None of these are mentioned in the report's service description.

      - The suitability report's service description is generic and not client-specific (all five items flagged isClientSpecific: false), whereas the client agreement describes a more detailed and structured service proposition.

      - The report states the ongoing fee will be 'circa £1,101.85 for the first year' based on 1% of the recommended fund value (£110,185), which is arithmetically consistent with 1% of £110,185 = £1,101.85. No discrepancy here, but the report does not state the minimum fee of £100 that applies under the client agreement.

      - The suitability report does not reference the minimum annual fee of £100 stated in the client agreement.

    The 1% ongoing adviser charge rate is consistent between the suitability report and the client agreement/service document — both state 1% per annum with a minimum of £100 (though the report omits the minimum). The monetary estimate of £1,101.85 is arithmetically consistent with 1% of £110,185. The core service items in the report (risk profile review, portfolio suitability review, market updates, ad-hoc adviser access, holistic financial planning) are all present in or at least consistent with the broader service proposition described in the client agreement documents. However, the suitability report's service description is materially less detailed than the client agreement: it omits the explicit annual review meeting commitment, the twice-yearly statements and performance reports, the cash flow analysis commitment, the family benefit, the quarterly e-magazine, and online portfolio access. Most significantly, the report records review frequency as 'Unspecified', whereas the client agreement commits to 'at least annually'. The absence of a stated review frequency in the report means the client is not clearly informed of how often they will receive the service they are paying 1% per annum for. The guard notes that the client agreement and report may name services differently — the core services are recognisable across both documents. However, the omission of the annual review meeting commitment (the most material service element underpinning the ongoing charge) and the absence of review frequency in the report are genuine gaps, not mere labelling differences. The omission of the minimum fee is a minor point. There is no vulnerability information in the evidence that materially affects this assessment. The overall picture is that the charge rate is supported, but the service proposition as described in the report is thinner than what the client agreement commits to, and the lack of a stated review frequency is a specific gap for a client paying 1% pa on a pension fund of circa £110,000.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P15)

        "Reviewing your attitude to risk and capacity for loss to ensure your investment portfolio remains suitable for you.

         Reviewing the ongoing suitability of your investment portfolio.

         Market updates and updates with any changes in financial legislation.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts six categories of benefit to justify the higher-cost recommendation: (1) Lower Costs — 'the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension'; (2) Greater Investment Choice — wider range of investment options for a more tailored strategy; (3) Adviser Charging Facilitation — adviser charges deducted directly from the new scheme; (4) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments — ability to actively monitor and adjust investments; (5) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility — more flexible retirement options including tax-efficient withdrawal strategies; (6) Provider Financial Strength — Aviva rated A (Stable) by S&P, £407bn AUM. The report simultaneously discloses that on a like-for-like basis the new plan is 0.24% less expensive (£264 underperformance tolerance), but including adviser fees it is 4.24% more expensive, requiring the plan to outperform by £4,672 in year one. A critical yield of 4.8% per year to age 75 (mid growth rate 2.94%) is stated as achievable based on past fund performance. For the Standard Life plan specifically, the recommendation rationale on page 8 states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving from switching), while the charges comparison table on page 11 shows the impact as +0.26% (a cost increase).

    File:     The evidence pack supports several but not all of the benefit claims. On lower costs: the charges table (P19/P21) shows a like-for-like saving of 0.24% but the derived figures and internal inconsistency note confirm that the EA5 (Standard Life) row in the charges table was computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable. The computed figures also flag that the +0.26% impact figure for Standard Life implies a fund value of £103,439, not £3,002 — meaning the 'lower costs' claim for Standard Life is not supported and is directly contradicted within the report's own table. On investment choice: the policy research document (P7) confirms the Aviva Pension Portfolio offers 80+ funds versus 60+ for Zurich and 300 for Standard Life, so the claim of 'wider range' is not unambiguously supported for all ceding plans (Standard Life has 300 funds, Aviva has 80+). On retirement flexibility: the evidence (P4, P8, P19) confirms Aviva offers FAD, full and partial UFPLS and annuity. The existing Zurich plan (P19) only supports 'Full and partial UFPLS', not FAD, so the flexibility gain is real for that plan. Standard Life (P19) already offers FAD. The People's Pension (P19) already offers FAD. On adviser charging facilitation: the Aviva Key Features (P4) confirm the product supports adviser charges deduction. The Zurich policy info (P9) and the research report (P5) confirm Zurich and Standard Life do not support a servicing adviser, corroborating the facilitation benefit. On ongoing monitoring: no independent evidence in the pack contradicts this claim, but it is a generic service description rather than a plan-specific feature. On provider financial strength: no independent evidence in the pack corroborates the S&P A (Stable) rating or £407bn AUM figure. The MiFID II cost disclosure table (P10) shows transaction costs for the recommended funds (HSBC 0.01%, CT Universal MAP 0.26%, total 0.13%) which the report's costs section does not appear to include in its headline charge comparison. Transaction costs are noted as absent from the canonical model (/costsAndCharges/transactionCostsMonetary marked NotPresentInDocument).

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. The report claims 'lower costs' as a benefit for all switched plans (page 7), but the charges table on page 11 shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% (cost increase), directly contradicting the page 8 narrative that Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (which would mean switching saves 0.08%). The derived figures confirm the EA5 row was computed on Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's, rendering the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically unreliable.

      - 2. The 'lower costs' benefit claim is qualified only at the aggregate level (0.24% like-for-like saving) but the per-plan Standard Life figure is contradictory and the EA5 computation base is wrong.

      - 3. The mid growth rate used in the cost justification is stated as 2.94% on page 11 but 2.95% in the critical yield table on page 18 — a minor inconsistency but present.

      - 4. The report states the critical yield is 4.8% per year to age 75, justified by past fund performance. No quantified past performance data for the recommended funds against this specific threshold is provided in the evidence pack to corroborate the achievability assertion.

      - 5. The 'Greater Investment Choice' benefit is claimed for all switched plans, but Standard Life's existing plan offers 300 funds versus Aviva's 80+, meaning the benefit does not hold for the Standard Life switch on a raw fund count basis.

      - 6. Transaction costs (HSBC 0.01%, CT Universal MAP 0.26%, aggregate 0.13%) disclosed in the MiFID II table (P10) are not reflected in the report's headline charge comparison figures, meaning the true all-in cost comparison understates the new plan's costs.

      - 7. The Aviva financial strength claim (A Stable, £407bn AUM) is stated in the report but is not corroborated by any independent document in the evidence pack.

      - 8. 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' is cited as a benefit for all switches including Standard Life, which already offers FAD — the ceding plan already provides this feature for that plan.

    The central finding is the internal contradiction on costs for the Standard Life switch. The report uses 'lower costs' as a headline benefit justifying all three switches. For the Standard Life plan, page 8 states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive (switch saves 0.08%), but page 11's charges table shows +0.26% impact (switch costs more). The derived figures confirm the EA5 row was computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own £3,002, making the entire Standard Life cost comparison wrong. This is not a rounding difference or a false-positive guard situation — it is an arithmetically incorrect row that produces a figure contradicting the narrative in the same report. This directly undermines the 'Lower Costs' benefit claim as applied to the Standard Life switch. The 'Greater Investment Choice' benefit is also not straightforwardly supportable for the Standard Life switch: Standard Life's existing plan offers 300 funds versus Aviva's 80+, so the claim that Aviva offers a 'wider range' does not hold for this specific ceding plan, though the research report confirms Aviva was selected for other qualitative reasons including risk-rated multi-asset funds. The 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' benefit claim is partially undermined for Standard Life, which already offers FAD (per P19). The guard that 'a benefit the existing plan already provides cannot justify the extra cost' applies here. For Zurich, by contrast, the flexibility gain (FAD not available) is genuine and evidenced. The 4.24% total cost increase including adviser fees requires an outperformance of £4,672 in year one. The report asserts the 4.8% critical yield is achievable 'based on past performance' but no quantified performance data in the pack demonstrates this. Transaction costs of 0.13% aggregate (P10) are disclosed in the research report but absent from the headline charge comparison, meaning the true cost differential is understated. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment, though the absence of a formal vulnerability assessment outcome (/vulnerability/C1/assessmentOutcome NotPresentInDocument) is noted. Overall, the cost-justification analysis contains a material arithmetic error on the Standard Life comparison, a contradicted benefit claim (lower costs for Standard Life), and an unsupported benefit claim (investment choice breadth for Standard Life). These are not explained by any false-positive guard. The outcome is Potential Concern.
