  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report (page 11) states the recommended Aviva plan is 0.24% LESS expensive than current plans on a like-for-like basis (excluding adviser fees), but 4.24% MORE expensive including adviser fees, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one. The critical yield table (page 18) shows: Low (0.00%): projected value £97,600, RIY 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%; Mid (2.95%): projected value £115,000, RIY 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%; High (5.88%): projected value £135,000, RIY 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%. Page 16 narrative states mid critical yield requires 4.8% growth to match current plan, using a mid-growth rate of 2.94%. The report also asserts (page 8) that the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08% for that plan). The report presents the recommendation as justified by the expected investment performance of the recommended funds relative to the critical yield.

    File:     The Defaqto pension switch report [P10] confirms the overall comparison figures: Low (0.00%): existing £108,000 vs new £97,600, RIY existing 0.3% / new 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%, effect £10,400 / 9.6% decrease. Mid (2.95%): existing £127,000 vs new £115,000, RIY existing 0.3% / new 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%, effect £12,000 / 9.4% decrease. High (5.88%): existing £150,000 vs new £135,000, RIY existing 0.3% / new 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%, effect £15,000 / 10.0% decrease. At all three growth rates the new solution produces a lower projected value than the existing pensions (before allowing for the benefits of consolidation). [P16] confirms the 4.8% critical yield narrative and £4,672 outperformance requirement. The charges comparison table (page 11 of the suitability report, referenced in the canonical model's internal inconsistency notes) shows the Standard Life impact as +0.26% (i.e. the new plan is MORE expensive for Standard Life), contradicting the page 8 narrative of a 0.08% saving. The derived figures confirm the EA5 Standard Life row in the charges table was computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making that comparison arithmetically unreliable. The switch report [P3] covers a different scenario (retaining more plans) and shows different figures (e.g. mid projected value £125,000 vs £115,000 in [P10]) — these are alternative comparison scenarios within the switch report, not contradictions of [P10]. No passage corroborates the 0.24% like-for-like saving figure or the 4.24% including-adviser-fee figure directly from the case file (the arithmetic check confirms neither matched). Transaction costs are noted as absent from the canonical model (/costsAndCharges/transactionCostsMonetary NotPresentInDocument).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report (page 8) states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving from switching), but the charges comparison table (page 11, per the internal inconsistency log) shows the impact for Standard Life as +0.26% (the new plan is more expensive). These are contradictory.

      - The derived figures confirm the EA5 Standard Life row in the charges table was computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,438) rather than Standard Life's value (£3,002), making the +0.26% figure itself unreliable and the 0.08% saving assertion unsupported.

      - The report's 0.24% like-for-like saving is not corroborated by any passage in the evidence pack (arithmetic check: nearest match 0.29% in [P5], 20.8% away).

      - The report's 4.24% more expensive including adviser fee figure is not directly corroborated by any passage (arithmetic check: nearest 4% in [P10], 5.7% away).

      - The mid-growth rate is stated as 2.94% in the page 16 narrative but as 2.95% in the page 18 critical yield table — a marginal inconsistency within the report itself.

      - At all three growth rates the new solution projects a lower value than the existing arrangements (Low: -£10,400 / -9.6%; Mid: -£12,000 / -9.4%; High: -£15,000 / -10.0%), meaning higher charges (including adviser fees) produce materially lower projected outcomes. The report discloses this but the justification relies on the achievability of 4.8% growth, which is above the mid-growth assumption of 2.95%.

      - Transaction costs are absent from the report and not estimated, per the extraction log (/costsAndCharges/transactionCostsMonetary NotPresentInDocument).

    The primary concern under G7.11 is that the recommended solution produces materially lower projected outcomes at every growth rate (a £10,400–£15,000 reduction), driven by a RIY that more than doubles from 0.3% (existing) to 2.1–2.3% (new solution including adviser charges). The report discloses this in the critical yield table and in the page 11 cost comparison, and provides a narrative justification (page 16) that the 4.8% critical yield is achievable based on past fund performance. That disclosure is present and the key figures in [P10] and [P16] corroborate the projection and RIY numbers. However, several specific concerns remain. First, the Standard Life charge comparison is internally contradictory: the page 8 narrative claims a 0.08% saving from switching Standard Life, while the page 11 charges table shows +0.26% (a cost increase). The derived figures further show the EA5 row was computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's £3,002 value, meaning the +0.26% figure is itself arithmetically wrong. This means neither the 0.08% saving nor the +0.26% increase is reliable for Standard Life, and the cost justification for switching that specific plan is unsupported. Second, the 0.24% like-for-like saving and the 4.24% including-adviser-fee figure are not directly corroborated by any passage in the evidence pack — the switch report provides the underlying projection data but not those summary percentages. Third, the mid-growth rate inconsistency (2.94% vs 2.95%) is minor and falls within rounding; it does not affect the conclusion materially. Fourth, transaction costs are absent without estimation, which is a moderate omission on a pension switch. The guard on required outperformance does not apply here: a 4.24% annual outperformance requirement (£4,672 in year one) is far above the 0.5% noise threshold. The higher-cost guard (a higher charge buying a materially better fund range may be justified) is partially satisfied by the report's narrative on fund performance achievability, but the Standard Life charge arithmetic error undermines the cost justification for at least one of the three switched plans. The vulnerability overlay: the extraction notes absence of vulnerability assessment outcomes for both clients (/vulnerability/C1/assessmentOutcome and C2 NotPresentInDocument). No vulnerability characteristics are evidenced in the retrieved passages that would materially alter this cost finding. Overall: the critical yield and projection disclosure is present and substantially corroborated, but the Standard Life charge contradiction and arithmetic error, the unverified summary percentages, and the missing transaction costs together constitute material concerns that require clarification.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' on a like-for-like basis (page 8). The Defaqto charges table shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase in cost to the client, not a saving). These are contradictory: the narrative says switching saves 0.08% for Standard Life; the table says it costs 0.26% more. The EA5 derived figures confirm the Standard Life charge row was computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), meaning any cost comparison for Standard Life resting on this row is arithmetically unreliable. The mid-growth rate is cited as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative (and canonical model) but as 2.95% in the critical yield table on page 18. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report discloses the following material disadvantages and limitations: (1) Including adviser fee, the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one. (2) Critical yield of 4.8% p.a. required to age 75 at mid-growth rate of 2.94%. (3) At mid-growth rate, switching results in a projected value decrease of £12,000 or 9.4% at age 75. (4) People's Pension limits flexible withdrawal access. (5) Transferring away from People's Pension loses employer contributions. (6) Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution on a like-for-like basis. (7) Scottish Widows does not allow adviser management. (8) Scottish Widows lacks a single multi-asset fund at risk rating 5, requiring two funds with 20–30% in money market holdings. (9) Zurich lacks risk-5 funds, requiring a blend of risk-7 and risk-2 funds with underperformance versus Aviva options. (10) Performance data for Standard Life funds is unavailable in the adviser's system. (11) Cashflow model assumes 0% of surplus is saved. (12) Risk rating of 5 is above the questionnaire result of 4. (13) Aviva existing plan is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution. (14) Market movements during transfer could disadvantage or advantage client. (15) No guarantee the new investment will outperform the existing arrangement. (16) Access to funds typically takes 10–15 working days. (17) Recommended funds have not outperformed current Zurich funds over the five-year period. (18) Scottish Widows encashment: 25% tax-free cash, 75% taxed at marginal rate, estimated net receipt £170 under small pots rules; alternatively full amount could potentially be taken tax-free within personal allowance at retirement. (19) Like-for-like, the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive / £264 underperformance tolerance, but 4.24% more expensive / £4,672 outperformance required including adviser fee. (20) Loss at low growth: £10,400 or 9.6% decrease; at mid-growth: £12,000 or 9.4% decrease; at high growth: £15,000 or 10% decrease (from critical yield table, page 18).

    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto) provides switching effect figures for the three transferred plans individually and in combination. For the Aviva plan alone, the effect of switching at mid-growth is a £10 or 0.3% decrease, and at high growth a £10 or 0.2% decrease. The combined switching effect at mid-growth is stated as £12,000 or 9.4% decrease and at high-growth £15,000 or 10% decrease, consistent with what the report cites. The product literature (Aviva Key Features and Terms & Conditions) confirms: value may go up or down; client could get back less than invested; income drawdown risk that income may deplete the fund; investment growth needed to compensate for income withdrawals; tax treatment subject to change; market movement risk during switching; no additional Aviva charges for switching but possible transactional costs; 10–15 working day liquidity delay is corroborated by product terms. The policy research document confirms People's Pension does not offer flexi-access drawdown. The Defaqto charges table (page 11 of the switch report) shows the Standard Life impact as +0.26%, contradicting the report's narrative claim of a 0.08% saving for Standard Life on a like-for-like basis. The EA5 charge row in the canonical derived figures is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), confirming the Standard Life charges comparison is unreliable. No featuresOrGuaranteesLost data was extractable for any of the five existing arrangements (all five paths returned NotPresentInDocument). No vulnerability assessment outcome was recorded for either client (both paths returned NotPresentInDocument).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' on a like-for-like basis (page 8). The Defaqto charges table shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase in cost to the client, not a saving). These are contradictory: the narrative says switching saves 0.08% for Standard Life; the table says it costs 0.26% more.

      - The EA5 derived figures confirm the Standard Life charge row was computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), meaning any cost comparison for Standard Life resting on this row is arithmetically unreliable.

      - The mid-growth rate is cited as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative (and canonical model) but as 2.95% in the critical yield table on page 18.

      - The combined pension value is described as 'approximately £110,000' on page 3, 'approximately £106,000' on page 4, and the arithmetic sum of the five individual values is £116,998.47 — none of the three agree.

      - The report presents 'loss: £10,400 at 9.6%' (low growth), '£12,000 at 9.4%' (mid growth) and '£15,000 at 10%' (high growth) from the critical yield table. The derived figures show these percentages imply fund values of approximately £108,333, £127,660 and £150,000 respectively, none of which match any stated arrangement value or total — the base on which these percentages are computed is not made explicit in the report.

      - The report states 'the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive / £264 underperformance tolerance' on a like-for-like basis, then immediately states '4.24% more expensive / £4,672 outperformance required' including adviser fee. Both figures appear without reconciliation of the material difference between them for a reader who reads only one.

      - Features or guarantees lost on transfer are flagged as NotPresentInDocument for all five existing arrangements; the report contains no disclosure of any features or guarantees given up on transfer from any of the five plans.

      - No vulnerability assessment outcome is recorded for either client; it is therefore not possible to confirm whether any vulnerability-related communication adjustments affected how risks and disadvantages were presented.

    The report contains a substantial and client-specific risk and disadvantages disclosure covering cost impact, critical yield, projected value decrease at multiple growth rates, platform and fund limitations, tax consequences of encashment, market movement during transfer, no-guarantee warning and liquidity delay. These are corroborated by the Defaqto switch report and the Aviva product literature. Several concerns arise however. First, the Standard Life cost comparison is internally contradictory: the page 8 narrative asserts a 0.08% saving while the charges table shows +0.26% additional cost, and the derived figures confirm the Standard Life charge row was applied to Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's own £3,002, making the entire Standard Life cost comparison unreliable. This is a material discrepancy that a client reading the narrative rationale would receive a misleading impression from. Second, the report discloses no features or guarantees lost on transfer from any of the five existing arrangements. The extraction confirmed all five featuresOrGuaranteesLost paths returned NotPresentInDocument, meaning this is silence in the report rather than an extraction gap. For a pension switch recommendation, any guaranteed annuity rates, protected retirement ages, or scheme-specific protections that may be lost are material and their absence from the disclosure is a gap. Third, the mid-growth rate discrepancy (2.94% narrative vs 2.95% table) is minor but present. Fourth, the inconsistent total pension values across the report (£110,000 / £106,000 / £116,998.47) create confusion about the base on which projections and percentage losses are computed, undermining the reliability of the disclosed loss figures. Fifth, the two cost figures (0.24% saving vs 4.24% more expensive) are both disclosed but without explicit reconciliation, which could mislead a client focusing only on the like-for-like figure. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability assessment outcome is recorded for either client, so it cannot be confirmed that communication of risks and disadvantages was adjusted for any vulnerability needs. This is noted but assessed under CHK-010 in its own right. On balance, the Standard Life cost comparison error (narrative contradicts table, and the table itself is computed on the wrong fund value) is a material disadvantage that is misstated rather than omitted, and the absence of any features-or-guarantees-lost disclosure across all five plans is a gap in a pension switch context. These together constitute a Potential Concern at Moderate severity: risk content is present and substantial, but specific identified discrepancies mean the disclosure is not entirely accurate and a material category (features/guarantees lost) is absent.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report discloses the following product-specific and investment-specific risks: (1) Liquidity/access: funds will usually take 10–15 working days to access (LiquidityRisk, p.8). (2) Minimum access age: benefits can be drawn from age 55, rising to 57 from 6 April 2028 (p.9). (3) IHT legislative change: from 6 April 2027 unused DC pensions will be included in the taxable estate (p.9). (4) Cancellation value risk: if investment falls before cancellation notice received, the fall is deducted (p.16). (5) Cancellation provider risk: previous provider not obliged to take plan back (p.16). (6) Relative performance risk: recommended Aviva funds outperformed existing Aviva holdings but not existing Zurich funds (p.13). (7) Zurich higher-risk volatility: Zurich investments in higher-risk funds, more susceptible to market volatility (p.13). (8) Long-term care cost omission: cashflow does not consider long-term care costs (p.15). (9) Projection assumption risk: small variance in assumptions can markedly affect outcome over time (p.15). (10) Transaction costs excluded: charges quoted exclude transaction costs of underlying funds (p.10). (11) Market risk (capital at risk): unit prices and pension fund value can fall as well as rise; no guarantee of return of original capital, especially in early years or if income taken (p.12). No mention is made of: counterparty risk, credit/default risk on bonds, derivatives risk, currency risk, leverage risk, emerging markets risk, smaller companies risk, interest rate risk on fixed-interest securities, or dilution levy / SDRT charges — all of which appear in the product literature and KIIDs on file for the recommended funds.

    File:     The Aviva Key Features document (P4, P7, P14) discloses: capital/pension pot value can go down as well as up; currency risk (sterling strengthening reduces value); property liquidity delay risk; fixed-term product access restriction; cancellation period risk; income drawdown specific risks (impact on means-tested benefits; fund depletion if income too high; less for dependants or annuity; tax position uncertainty; risk of overpaying tax). The Aviva Terms and Conditions (P10, P13) disclose: dilution levy risk; SDRT on surrender of units; fund manager charges including those taken from capital rather than income. The HSBC KIID (P1, P3, P6, P8, P9) discloses: counterparty risk; credit risk; default risk; derivatives risk; currency risk; liquidity risk; risk and reward category 4 (medium fluctuations); minimum recommended holding period of 5 years; income reinvested. The Columbia Threadneedle KIID (P11, P12) discloses: derivative risk (losses can exceed initial investment); fixed interest securities risk (interest rate changes); credit risk; smaller companies risk; emerging markets risk; liquidity risk; currency risk; leverage risk. The Zurich Policy Info (P16, P17) discloses: property fund valuation and delay risk (up to 12 months); high yield bond fund risk; smaller companies/concentrated portfolio/less mature market risk; political/economic risk in less developed markets; fund managers taking yearly management charge from capital; derivative counterparty risk; OEIC cross-liability risk; fixed-interest inflation/interest rate risk. The SIPP research report (P15) records the agreed risk level as 5 and lists CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio as the recommended funds, with MiFID II pre-sale cost disclosures including transaction costs noted.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report does not mention counterparty risk, which is identified in both the HSBC KIID (P3) and the Columbia Threadneedle KIID (P11) as a material risk not fully captured by the risk and reward indicator.

      - The report does not mention credit risk or default risk on bonds, disclosed in the HSBC KIID (P3) and Columbia Threadneedle KIID (P11) as material risks for the recommended funds.

      - The report does not mention derivatives risk (including that derivatives losses can exceed the initial investment), disclosed in the Columbia Threadneedle KIID (P11) and HSBC KIID (P3).

      - The report does not mention currency risk, disclosed in both KIIDs (P3, P11) and the Aviva Key Features (P4) as applicable to the recommended funds.

      - The report does not mention leverage risk, disclosed in the Columbia Threadneedle KIID (P11).

      - The report does not mention emerging markets risk, disclosed in the Columbia Threadneedle KIID (P11).

      - The report does not mention smaller companies risk, disclosed in the Columbia Threadneedle KIID (P11).

      - The report does not mention interest rate / fixed interest securities risk, disclosed in the Columbia Threadneedle KIID (P11) and Zurich Policy Info (P17).

      - The report does not mention the dilution levy / SDRT risk disclosed in the Aviva Terms and Conditions (P10).

      - The report does not mention income drawdown-specific risks (impact on means-tested benefits; risk of fund depletion from high income; reduced funds for dependants/annuity; tax position uncertainty) disclosed in the Aviva Key Features (P4), though drawdown is a stated benefit option.

      - The report does not mention the minimum recommended holding period of 5 years for the HSBC fund (P9), relevant given the client's pension access timeline.

      - The report's transaction cost exclusion caveat (p.10) is present, but the MiFID II pre-sale cost disclosures in the SIPP research report (P15) include transaction costs; there is no reconciliation in the report of how these were communicated to the client.

      - The report acknowledges the recommended Aviva funds have not outperformed the Zurich funds (p.13) but does not address whether this underperformance, combined with the higher charges on the Standard Life plan (the charges table shows +0.26% impact, contradicting the page 8 narrative of a 0.08% saving), constitutes a material disadvantage that has been fully explained.

    The report contains a reasonable base layer of risk disclosure: capital-at-risk wording, market risk, liquidity (access timing), minimum access age, IHT legislative change, cancellation risks, relative performance acknowledgement, projection assumption caveats, and transaction cost exclusion notice. These satisfy the general capital-at-risk warning but the guard requires product-specific risks to be tested separately. When tested against the KIIDs and product literature on file, several material product-specific risks present in those documents are absent from the report: counterparty risk, credit/default risk, derivatives risk (including losses exceeding initial investment from the CT fund KIID), currency risk, leverage risk, emerging markets risk, smaller companies risk, and interest rate / fixed interest securities risk. These are not generic boilerplate omissions — they are explicitly identified as material risks in the KIIDs for the two specific recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced). The guard against deferring risk disclosure to product literature applies here: the report does not cross-reference these KIIDs or state that the client has been directed to read them for these specific risks, nor does it reproduce them. The income drawdown risks in the Aviva Key Features (fund depletion from high income withdrawals; impact on means-tested benefits) are also absent, which is material given the product's flexibility to take drawdown. The minimum 5-year recommended holding period for the HSBC fund (P9) is not mentioned, which has relevance to the client's access timeline. The Standard Life charge discrepancy (report page 8 says 0.08% saving; table page 11 shows +0.26% increase) is an internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction and means the cost disadvantage of that switch may not have been accurately disclosed to the client — this is a material risk/disadvantage concern in its own right. No vulnerability flags are raised in the available evidence that would materially alter this assessment. The overall picture is that general investment risk is disclosed but product-specific and fund-specific risks identified in the on-file literature are substantially absent from the report, and risk disclosure is in effect partially delegated to product literature without explicit direction to it. Severity is assessed as Moderate-to-High: risks are stated (so not wholly absent) but are generic and incomplete relative to what the product literature identifies as material.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report states the following tax consequences: (1) pension tax relief on contributions (£80 net becomes £100 gross); (2) death benefits before age 75 paid free of tax to nominated beneficiaries; (3) death benefits after age 75 taxed at beneficiaries' marginal rate; (4) Scottish Widows encashment under small pots rules: 25% tax-free, 75% taxed at marginal rate, estimated net receipt £170 from a £200 fund; (5) alternative if Scottish Widows pot left to grow until retirement, could be taken within personal allowance with no tax due; (6) flexi-access drawdown: up to 25% tax-free lump sum (subject to Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275), remainder taxed at marginal rate; (7) annual allowance excess triggers tax charge up to 45%, tax relief limited to £3,600 p.a. or 100% of salary; (8) pensions currently outside estate for IHT, but from 6 April 2027 most unused DC pensions and death benefits will be included in taxable estate; (9) tax law caveat — based on current understanding, subject to change; (10) benefits can be drawn from age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028), always take advice for tax-efficient withdrawal.

    File:     Key Features document [P2] confirms: up to 25% of pension fund can normally be withdrawn as tax-free lump sum; income tax payable on income and lump sum payments taken; HMRC lump sum allowance and lump sum & death benefit allowance limits apply; tax information based on current UK legislation subject to change. Terms and Conditions [P4] confirms: death before 75 — benefits normally paid tax free to nominated beneficiaries (with caveat on lump sum & death benefit allowance excess); death after 75 — benefits taxed at beneficiaries' marginal rate; lump sum paid to trust or personal representatives taxed at 45%. Suitability Report [P10] records the Scottish Widows small pots calculation verbatim. Suitability Report [P6] sets out flexi-access drawdown tax treatment including the £268,275 Lump Sum Allowance, annual allowance (£60,000 maximum), and the new allowances replacing the lifetime allowance. Suitability Report [P5] records the IHT position and the 6 April 2027 change. The pension switch research report [P23] records the day-one death benefit shortfall of £3,305 (existing £110,185.60 vs new solution £106,880.60) — this is a material tax-adjacent consequence (immediate reduction in death benefit value) disclosed in research but the suitability report does not explicitly flag the day-one death benefit shortfall figure in monetary terms in the main body. Loss scenario table [P9] shows effect of switching at low/mid/high growth rates (£10,400/9.6%, £12,000/9.4%, £15,000/10% decrease).

    Discrepancies:

      - The pension switch research report [P23] discloses a day-one death benefit shortfall of £3,305 (existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day-one £106,880.60 due to adviser charge deduction). The suitability report does not state this monetary shortfall figure explicitly in the main body when discussing death benefits or tax consequences, though it discloses the adviser fee of £3,305.55 and the charges impact table on page 11.

      - The report's mid-growth rate narrative references '2.94%' (per extraction internal inconsistency) while the critical yield table [P9] labels it '2.95%' — a marginal discrepancy noted but immaterial to tax consequence assessment.

      - The report's small pots calculation states a £200 fund withdrawal; the actual Scottish Widows value is £211.32 per the existing arrangements table [P5]. The tax estimate of £170 net is therefore based on a slightly rounded/understated figure, though the difference is minimal.

      - The report states death benefits before age 75 are paid 'free of tax' without qualifying this with the lump sum & death benefit allowance caveat that appears in the Key Features [P4] (i.e. if the value of tax-free benefits taken during lifetime and on death exceeds the lump sum & death benefit allowance, the beneficiary may pay income tax on the excess). The Key Features document explicitly notes this qualification.

      - The report's flexi-access drawdown section [P6] states the remainder 'remains invested within a tax advantaged environment' but the canonical model quotes it as income withdrawals being taxed at marginal rate — these are consistent with each other and with [P2] and [P6].

    The report covers the principal tax consequences applicable to this recommendation set with reasonable breadth: pension tax relief, drawdown crystallisation (25% TFLS, marginal rate on remainder), death benefit tax treatment pre- and post-75, the IHT legislative change from April 2027, the small pots tax treatment with a client-specific estimate, the annual allowance charge, and the tax law caveat. These are corroborated by [P2], [P4], [P6], [P7], [P9], and [P10]. The client-specific small pots tax consequence is stated and verified against [P10]. Loss scenarios are present in [P9] and referenced in the report. The vulnerability overlay is noted — the client (aged 69, married, in good health, financially independent children) has no recorded vulnerability characteristics in the evidence that would alter the tax consequence assessment. However, two concerns arise: (1) The death benefit tax-free treatment stated in the report omits the qualification present in [P4] that where the cumulative tax-free benefits from pension schemes exceed the lump sum & death benefit allowance, income tax applies on the excess. While this is a standard product literature caveat and the client's total fund (c.£110,000) is far below the £1,073,100 allowance making it practically immaterial here, the report's assertion that death benefits are paid 'free of tax' without this qualification is technically incomplete relative to what the product literature says. This is a low-level concern given the client's fund size. (2) More materially, the pension switch research [P23] discloses a specific day-one death benefit shortfall of £3,305 attributable to the adviser charge being deducted on transfer. The report discloses the adviser charge amount and the charges comparison table but does not explicitly state the monetary day-one death benefit reduction in the section discussing death benefits and their tax treatment — a client reading only the death benefit section would not see the immediate £3,305 value reduction. This is a material consequence of the recommendation that is present in the research file but not prominently flagged in the relevant section of the report. The small pots figure discrepancy (£200 used vs £211.32 actual) is immaterial. The 2.94%/2.95% mid-growth rate discrepancy is immaterial to tax consequences. The generic legislative explanations (annual allowance, drawdown, IHT change) are not client-specific but are relevant to the recommendation and their presence is appropriate. The guard noting that a tax consequence stated without the client's tax band is unverifiable rather than wrong applies to the small pots marginal rate calculation: the client's tax band is not explicitly stated in the suitability report's tax section, though the estimated net of £170 implies a basic rate taxpayer assumption. This is unverified but not necessarily wrong. Overall, the tax consequence coverage is substantively adequate with one moderate gap (day-one death benefit shortfall not monetarily quantified in the death benefit/tax section) and one low-level gap (lump sum & death benefit allowance qualification on pre-75 death benefits).

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report has a dedicated section headed 'Other Considerations of Switching' appearing in the main body (pages 8, 11, 12, 15, 16, 18). Risk warnings include: stock-market movements during pending transactions; no guarantee that the new provider's funds will outperform existing funds; investment values can fall, especially in early years or when taking income; past performance is no guarantee of future returns. Cost disadvantage is disclosed: the new plan requires outperformance of £4,672 in the first year (including advice fees), and based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94% the new plan must grow at 4.8% p.a. to age 75 to match the existing plan. Critical yield analysis appears in the Appendix. Loss scenarios appear in a critical yield table showing: low growth — £10,400 or 9.6% decrease; mid growth — £12,000 or 9.4% decrease; high growth — £15,000 or 10.0% decrease. An immediate fund shortfall of £3,305 following the switch (reduction in death benefit) is also disclosed. The report also discloses that the Standard Life plan is stated to be '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' as a benefit/lower-cost rationale. Risk warnings appear both in the main body and appendix.

    File:     The pension switch report (G category) confirms the combined critical yield table figures exactly matching the suitability report: low growth £10,400/9.6%, mid growth £12,000/9.4%, high growth £15,000/10.0% (P11). The switch report confirms the immediate fund value shortfall: existing £110,185.60, new solution day one £106,880.60, shortfall £3,305.00 (P15). Per-plan critical yield tables also appear for Zurich-only and Aviva-only and Standard Life-only scenarios in the pension switch report (P9, P10, P12, P13). The suitability report's main body (P5, P6) contains explicit risk warnings about investment values falling and no guarantee of outperformance. The charges table (P4) shows the new plan total charge of 0.44%/£484.81, and the switch-only charges table (P4) shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% — contradicting the main body's claim of a 0.08% saving on Standard Life. The mid-growth rate is stated as 2.94% in the main body narrative (P2) but 2.95% in the critical yield table (P1/P11). The Standard Life recommendation rationale (P6) states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' as a reason to switch, while the charges table shows +0.26% impact — meaning the new plan is actually more expensive for Standard Life, not cheaper. No explicit tax consequence explanation for the pension switches appears in the evidenced passages (the Scottish Widows small-pot encashment tax consequence is noted in the ambiguities but the main pension switches have no tax discussion in the retrieved passages). No features or guarantees lost are documented (extraction confirms these paths are NotPresentInDocument).

    Discrepancies:

      - The Standard Life recommendation rationale states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying a saving on switching) but the charges comparison table shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase in charges, meaning the new plan is more expensive for this segment) — a direct contradiction within the report itself, confirmed by derived figures showing EA5 charge arithmetic is computed on the wrong plan value (Zurich's value rather than Standard Life's £3,002).

      - The mid-growth rate is stated as '2.94%' in the main body narrative (P2) but the critical yield table labels it as '2.95%' (P1/P11) — a minor internal inconsistency.

      - No tax consequences of the pension switches are explained in the report's risk/disadvantage disclosures retrieved in the evidence — the passages confirm benefits of 'tax-efficient withdrawal strategies' are mentioned as a positive but no adverse tax consequence of switching is addressed.

      - Features or guarantees lost on transfer are entirely absent from the report — the extraction confirms these paths are NotPresentInDocument for all five existing arrangements (EA1–EA5), meaning any safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates or other plan-specific protections going to the new provider are undisclosed.

      - The loss scenarios (£10,400/£12,000/£15,000) and critical yield figures appear in the Appendix (pages 18 onward) while the main body leads prominently with the benefits of switching — cost savings, greater investment choice, enhanced flexibility — with only a brief reference in the body to the £4,672 outperformance figure and a direction to 'the Appendix of this report' for the full critical yield detail.

      - The pension switch report contains per-plan critical yield tables (Zurich-only, Aviva-only, Standard Life-only and combined) but the suitability report presents only the combined all-plans critical yield table, potentially obscuring the individual plan switching impact.

    The report does contain a dedicated 'Other Considerations of Switching' section in the main body and explicit risk warnings (investment values can fall, no guarantee of outperformance, past performance caveat). Loss scenarios with monetary and percentage figures are provided in the critical yield table, consistent with the pension switch report evidence. The immediate death benefit reduction of £3,305 is disclosed. These are genuine positives.

   

    However, several material concerns remain. First, the most serious is the Standard Life charge contradiction: the main body presents switching the Standard Life plan as a cost saving ('0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'), while the charges table shows +0.26% — meaning the new plan is actually more expensive for that plan segment. The derived figures confirm this is not a rounding artefact: the EA5 charge arithmetic is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438), not Standard Life's (£3,002), so the +0.26%/£268.94 figure is on the wrong base entirely. The client therefore receives a misleading cost-benefit message for the Standard Life switch.

   

    Second, features or guarantees lost on transfer are wholly absent. The extraction explicitly flags these as NotPresentInDocument for all five arrangements. While for workplace-type plans these may be nil, there is no documented check and no disclosure — a material omission under COBS 9.4.7R.

   

    Third, there is no discussion of tax consequences of the pension-to-pension switches. The report mentions 'tax-efficient withdrawal strategies' as a benefit but does not address any adverse tax consequence of switching (e.g. loss of any protected tax-free cash, or tax treatment of the Scottish Widows encashment net receipt of approximately £170). The ambiguity note on the Scottish Widows encashment acknowledges two different tax outcomes were identified but the evidence passages do not show this being explained in the disadvantages/risks section.

   

    Fourth, regarding prominence: the main body leads with six bullet-pointed benefits and directs the reader to the Appendix for critical yield detail. The £4,672 outperformance requirement is mentioned once in the body (P2) but the full loss scenarios (£10,400–£15,000) appear only in the Appendix table (P1/P8). Under the severity guidance, a material disadvantage quantified only in an appendix while benefits are prominent in the body is a prominence finding, though here the body does reference the critical yield/outperformance requirement, partially mitigating this.

   

    The Standard Life charge misrepresentation is the highest-severity item: a material disadvantage (higher cost for that plan) is not only absent from the report but is affirmatively contradicted by a benefit claim. The missing features/guarantees disclosure and absent tax consequence discussion are also material. The vulnerability overlay does not materially alter this finding as no vulnerability characteristics specific to risk comprehension are flagged in the evidence beyond the general capacity for loss disclosure.

   

    Overall outcome: Potential Concern, severity High, primarily driven by the Standard Life cost misrepresentation and the absence of features/guarantees lost disclosure.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts, for each of the four existing arrangements being switched (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, and one further plan), that 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits.' The existingArrangements model records hasSafeguardedBenefits=false and isBeingGivenUp=false for all five arrangements. No other benefits, features or protections lost are recorded in the replacementAnalysis paths — the extraction confirms featuresOrGuaranteesLost is NotPresentInDocument for all five EA entries. The report does note that the Standard Life plan benefits from a Differential Fund Discount (leaver discount of 0.4800% from the TER) but no explicit statement that this discount is lost on transfer appears in the canonical model. The retirement options available on each plan are recorded: EA1 (People's Pension, retained): FAD/UFPLS/PartialUFPLS; EA2 (Scottish Widows, encashed): UFPLS/PartialUFPLS; EA3 (Aviva, switched): FAD/UFPLS/PartialUFPLS; EA4 (Zurich, switched): UFPLS/PartialUFPLS; EA5 (Standard Life, switched): FAD only (unless fund value increased to £10k).

    File:     Policy research and provider literature confirm: (1) No GAR, GMP, guaranteed growth rates, guaranteed minimum fund, reference scheme test, waiver of premium or life cover on any of the five plans (People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life). (2) Standard Life policy letter explicitly states 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' in relation to the Differential Fund Discount / leaver discount of 0.4800% applied to the TER on the SL At Retirement Universal fund — this is a material feature that will be lost on transfer. (3) Policy research table confirms FAD is not available on Zurich or Scottish Widows plans (Scottish Widows: 'no'; Zurich: 'no'); FAD is available on Aviva and Standard Life (Standard Life only if fund value reaches £10k). Full and partial UFPLS are available on People's Pension, Scottish Widows and Zurich but not on Aviva or Standard Life per the policy research table. (4) The Aviva policy letter notes the plan does not allow adviser charging and that in-specie transfers are not possible — cash only. (5) The People's Pension policy literature confirms no annuity purchase option directly from that provider. (6) Scottish Widows policy information confirms no GMP, no guaranteed minimum fund, no reference scheme test, no waiver of premium, no life cover, no fund rebates. (7) No with-profits or guaranteed growth features identified in any plan.

    Discrepancies:

      - The Standard Life policy letter explicitly warns that the Differential Fund Discount (leaver discount 0.4800% off TER) 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' — this is a material feature being given up on switch. The suitability report's replacement analysis contains no disclosure of this lost discount, and the featuresOrGuaranteesLost path is confirmed absent from the document.

      - The policy research table shows that Full and Partial UFPLS are NOT available on Aviva (existing plan EA3) — yet the canonical model records retirementOptionsAvailable for EA3 as [FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS, PartialUFPLS]. This is a discrepancy between the report and the policy research evidence.

      - The policy research table shows FAD is NOT available on Zurich (EA4) — the canonical model records retirementOptionsAvailable for EA4 as [UFPLS, PartialUFPLS] which is consistent, but the report makes no disclosure that FAD is unavailable on Zurich and being given up by consolidating into a new plan that does offer FAD. No explicit disclosure of this retirement option difference appears in the report.

      - The Standard Life plan (EA5) only offers FlexiAccessDrawdown per the report, and only if fund value reaches £10k per the policy research — yet no disclosure appears in the report that UFPLS is not available on this plan (contrasting with the new plan which offers broader options), nor is the £10k threshold for FAD mentioned.

      - The charge arithmetic confirms that the EA5 (Standard Life) charges comparison row in the report was computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), making the cost comparison for Standard Life unreliable — the report presents a saving of 0.08% on page 8 but the charges table shows +0.26%, a direct contradiction. This affects whether any cost disadvantage of switching Standard Life is accurately presented.

    The primary finding is that the Standard Life policy letter in the case file (category E) contains an explicit warning that the Differential Fund Discount of 0.4800% applied to the TER 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a named, plan-specific financial feature that the client is giving up by switching. The guard at [G8.6] states that a guarantee or benefit recorded in provider literature but not mentioned in the report is a finding. The report's replacement analysis is confirmed absent (NotPresentInDocument) for all featuresOrGuaranteesLost paths. This is therefore a material omission under the highest severity band: a material disadvantage present in product literature and omitted entirely from the report. The secondary discrepancy — the Standard Life charges comparison being computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's (per the derived figures), producing a stated 0.08% saving that is contradicted by the charges table showing +0.26% — means the report not only fails to disclose the lost discount but positively misstates the cost position of the Standard Life switch. These compound rather than cancel. The retirement options discrepancy for Aviva (UFPLS shown as available in the report but not supported by the policy research table) is a further inconsistency, though less material given the small fund value (£3,744). The absence of any explicit narrative in the report about features or protections being given up on any of the switched plans (beyond the boilerplate 'no safeguarded benefits' statement) is itself a concern: the report identifies no retirement flexibility differences, no provider-specific features lost, and no mention of the Standard Life discount. No vulnerability flags have been identified in the canonical model that would further affect this check. The outcome is Potential Concern at High severity.

      · Standard_Life_Policy_Info.md [E] (P10)

        "Please note this member benefits from a Differential Fund Discount from the Total Expense Ratio (TER) being applied to their plan as a creation of units into the plan on a monthly basis. which we have therefore listed to you below. These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer."

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report acknowledges that including adviser fees the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring the new plan to outperform by £4,672 in the first year. It justifies this by asserting that based on past performance of the recommended funds, a required growth rate of 4.8% per year to age 75 (against a mid-growth rate of 2.94%) is achievable. The report also states that on a like-for-like basis (excluding adviser fee) the new plan is 0.24% cheaper, and the charges table shows per-plan impact figures. The critical yield table shows projections at low (0%), mid (2.95%) and high (5.88%) growth rates with values of £97,600, £115,000 and £135,000 respectively at age 75.

    File:     The pension switch research report (passages P1, P4, P11, P15) shows that at mid growth rate (2.94%) the existing solution projects to £127,000 and the new solution to £125,000 — a decrease of £2,000 or 1.6%. At low growth the existing projects to £102,000 vs £100,000 new; at high growth £150,000 vs £147,000. The new solution ranks 5th out of 9 compared products by maturity value. The research report projections for the new solution (£125,000 mid) differ from the critical yield table in the suitability report (£115,000 mid). The mid growth rate is labelled 2.94% in the research report but 2.95% in the critical yield table. The research report confirms an initial adviser charge of £3,305 reducing day-one value from £110,185.60 to £106,880.60 (P17). The SIPP research report (P12, P13) shows the recommended funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced) have 5-year cumulative performance of 42.40% combined, but no explicit comparison to existing Zurich fund performance is made in the retrieved passages to validate the claim that 4.8% growth is achievable based on past performance. The charges comparison table (P19) shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% but the report narrative states a 0.08% saving for Standard Life — a contradiction noted in the internal inconsistencies. EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's, making the Standard Life comparison unreliable per the derived figures. The research report (P4) shows existing solution mid-growth maturity value of £120,000 for the Zurich-only plan and £127,000 for the combined existing solution.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report's critical yield table states a mid-growth projected value at age 75 of £115,000 for the new solution; the pension switch research report states £125,000 for the new solution at the same mid growth rate — a £10,000 difference.

      - The mid growth rate is stated as 2.94% in the research report (P4) but 2.95% in the suitability report's critical yield table — a marginal inconsistency noted in the canonical model's internalInconsistencies.

      - The report states the Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution (saving on switch); the charges table (P19) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — these are contradictory and the derived figures confirm the +0.26% row is computed on Zurich's fund value not Standard Life's, making the Standard Life cost justification unreliable.

      - The report asserts that 4.8% growth per year is achievable based on past performance of the recommended funds, but no passage in the file explicitly benchmarks the recommended funds' past returns against this 4.8% threshold or demonstrates it is achievable; the 5-year cumulative of 42.40% (approx 7.3% annualised) for the combined portfolio is in the research report but is not linked to this claim in the report itself.

      - The new solution ranks 5th (out of 9) by projected maturity value in the research report, behind the existing solution (2nd) and three alternative products, yet the report does not acknowledge that cheaper or better-projecting alternatives existed in the market research — a potential gap in justification for why increased cost versus the existing arrangement is acceptable.

      - The report states £4,672 outperformance required; derived figures show 4.24% on a base of £110,188.68 which matches no arrangement value, though this is close to the transfer total of £110,185 and the discrepancy is immaterial in isolation.

    The report does provide a cost justification: it discloses that including adviser fees the plan is 4.24% more expensive (£4,672 first-year outperformance required) and asserts this is achievable based on past fund performance. This is a genuine justification rather than an absent one, so the guards direct a grading exercise rather than a binary finding. However, several issues reduce the quality of that justification materially. First, the most significant discrepancy is between the suitability report's critical yield table (mid-growth projected value £115,000) and the research report's projected value for the new solution (£125,000 at the same mid growth rate). This £10,000 gap is not explained and means the client-facing projection figures differ from the research tool figures. Where a client reads the suitability report they see a materially lower projection than the underlying research supports, which if anything understates the new plan — but the inconsistency itself undermines confidence in the figures. Second, the Standard Life charge comparison is computed on the wrong fund value (Zurich's £103,439 rather than Standard Life's £3,002), confirmed by the derived figures. The report narrative claims a 0.08% saving on Standard Life but the table shows +0.26%, making that element of the cost comparison unreliable. Third, the claim that 4.8% annual growth is achievable is based on past performance of the recommended funds, but the report does not explicitly cite which performance data supports this or connect it to the 4.8% threshold. The research report shows combined 5-year cumulative of 42.40%, which annualises to approximately 7.3%, which would exceed 4.8%, but this connection is not made explicitly in the report and the 42.40% figure covers only the period to October 2025 — the report's assertion therefore rests on an implicit rather than demonstrated link. Fourth, the market comparison shows three alternative products (Zurich standalone, Aviva Life standalone, Standard Life standalone) would project higher maturity values than the recommended new solution, yet the report does not address why the recommended solution is preferred over these cheaper/higher-projecting alternatives when justifying the increased overall cost to the client. This is a moderate gap in the justification narrative. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved evidence that would specifically heighten the harm from these cost justification gaps beyond the standard concern. The primary concerns are: (a) the £10,000 projection discrepancy between suitability report and research tool; (b) the unreliable Standard Life charge comparison; (c) the implicit rather than explicit basis for the achievability claim. These are moderate rather than high severity because a cost justification is present and the overall direction of the narrative is transparent — the report does tell the client the plan costs more and that it needs to outperform — but material elements of that justification are internally inconsistent or poorly supported.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report discloses the following material disadvantages and risks: (1) Higher cost including adviser fee: the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one; (2) Critical yield required: 4.8% per year to age 75 at mid-growth rate of 2.94%; (3) Switching cost at mid-growth: £12,000 or 9.4% decrease at age 75; (4) People's Pension limited flexibility: transferring into it would prevent flexible access; (5) Loss of employer contributions if transferring away from People's Pension; (6) Standard Life cost comparison: 0.08% more expensive than recommended solution on like-for-like basis; (7) Standard Life performance data unavailable in adviser's system; (8) Scottish Widows no adviser management; (9) Scottish Widows fund range limitation requiring two funds with 20-30% in money market; (10) Zurich fund range limitation requiring blend of risk level 7 and 2; (11) Cashflow surplus assumption of 0%; (12) Risk rating of 5 above questionnaire result of 4; (13) Liquidity risk of 10-15 working days; (14) Minimum access age 55 rising to 57 from April 2028; (15) IHT legislative change from 6 April 2027; (16) Cancellation value risk and previous provider not obliged to take plan back.

    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto) shows: at mid-growth rate (2.95%), the effect of switching across all consolidated pensions is £12,000 or 9.4% decrease at age 75, with a critical yield of 4.8% — consistent with the report. At low growth rate (0.00%), the effect of switching is £10,400 or 9.6% decrease. At high growth rate (5.88%), the effect is £15,000 or 10.0% decrease. The Defaqto switch report states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' in the benefits section. The Standard Life policy info confirms a Differential Fund Discount (leaver discount of 0.48%) on the Standard Life plan, noting 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' The Zurich policy info documents reference life cover and waiver benefits no longer available on new pension plans, and references guaranteed annuity rates as a consideration. The SIPP research report and KIIDs disclose investment risks (counterparty, credit, default, derivatives risk). The Key Features document discloses income drawdown risks including means-tested benefits impact. The growth rate used in the switch report is 2.95% (mid), while the suitability report states 2.94%. The switch report shows the overall position table uses 2.95% consistently.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report states the mid-growth rate as 2.94% in the critical yield narrative, but the pension switch report (Defaqto) uses 2.95% throughout the overall position comparison table. This is a marginal inconsistency already noted as an internal inconsistency.

      - The Standard Life policy info explicitly states that the Differential Fund Discount (leaver discount 0.48%) 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' — this is a material benefit being given up on transfer from Standard Life that the suitability report does not disclose as a specific feature or guarantee lost. The report mentions Standard Life charges are 0.08% more expensive than the new solution but does not mention the discounted charge that will be lost.

      - The Standard Life charges comparison in the report states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' but the Defaqto switch report charges table shows a +0.26% impact for Standard Life — a direct numerical contradiction already flagged as an internal inconsistency, meaning the report's stated cost saving for Standard Life may be materially incorrect and clients may be misled about the relative cost.

      - The Zurich policy info references life cover and waiver benefits no longer available on new pension plans, and raises guaranteed annuity rates as a question. The switch report's benefits section states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products.' The suitability report does not explicitly confirm whether guaranteed annuity rates or life cover/waiver benefits were checked and confirmed absent for Zurich — the Zurich policy info raises these as considerations without confirming their absence.

      - The report's loss scenario of £12,000 / 9.4% (mid growth) is corroborated, but the report does not present the high-growth scenario loss of £15,000 / 10.0% or the low-growth scenario loss of £10,400 / 9.6% as named loss scenarios in the risk warnings section, though these appear in the critical yield section of the switch report.

      - The report's stated Standard Life cost comparison (0.08% saving) conflicts with the switch report table showing +0.26% — this means the justification for switching Standard Life rests on a figure that is contradicted by the supporting evidence.

    Overall the report's risk disclosure is substantive and covers most material areas: cost disadvantage (monetary and percentage), critical yield, switching loss scenarios at mid-growth, product limitations for People's Pension/Scottish Widows/Zurich, loss of employer contributions, liquidity risk, access age change, IHT legislative change, and cancellation risks. The KIIDs and Key Features document contain generic market-risk language (counterparty, credit, derivatives risk) which the guard notes does not need repeating if it is client-generic product literature. These are not plan-specific omissions.

   

    However, two material concerns arise:

   

    1. The Standard Life Differential Fund Discount: The Standard Life policy info at [P15] explicitly states the plan benefits from a Differential Fund Discount (leaver discount 0.48%) and warns 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a specific financial benefit the client is giving up on transfer — a lower ongoing charge that is lost on exit. The report fails to disclose this as a feature or protection being given up. This is a material omission, particularly because the report's own cost comparison for Standard Life (0.08% saving) is contradicted by the switch report table (+0.26% increase at [P10]), meaning the picture presented to the client about Standard Life costs is both incomplete and potentially misleading. The guard noting that 'a risk disclosed in a separate document given to the client is disclosed' does not help here because the report does not reference this specific discount loss at all.

   

    2. The Standard Life cost figure contradiction: The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8), but the Defaqto switch report charges table shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — i.e. the new solution is more expensive, not the existing plan. The derived figures confirm EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's, making the Standard Life comparison unreliable. This directly affects the adequacy of risk disclosure: the report presents a cost saving where the switch report shows a cost increase, and the justification for switching Standard Life is therefore not adequately supported and potentially inaccurate in a way that harms consumer understanding.

   

    On the Zurich guaranteed annuity rate / life cover question: the switch report states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' at [P3], and the Zurich policy info at [P8] lists these as checkbox questions without indicating they are present. The absence of ticks and the switch report's statement provide sufficient basis to treat these as confirmed absent; this is not a finding.

   

    The high and low growth loss scenarios (£15,000/10.0% and £10,400/9.6%) appear in the critical yield section of the switch report and are likely present in the suitability report's critical yield table (page 18) even if not explicitly named in the risk warnings section — given the disclosure of the mid-growth scenario and the table at page 18, this is at most a low-level presentation concern rather than a material omission.

   

    The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would alter this assessment.

   

    Severity: The Standard Life discount omission combined with the contradictory cost figure is a material disadvantage present in the research/policy info that is either omitted (the discount loss) or misstated (the cost direction) in the report. This meets the highest severity tier. However, it is partially mitigated by the fact that the overall cost comparison (4.24% more expensive including adviser fee) is clearly disclosed, and Standard Life is a smaller plan (£3,002). Nevertheless, the omission and contradiction are genuine findings.

These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer."

      [G8.4] No Issue

        - The report states the mid growth rate as 2.95% in the Critical Yield table (page 18 per canonical model) but the internal inconsistency note records 2.94% on page 11 narrative vs 2.95% on page 18 — a minor label discrepancy within the report itself, not a contradiction with the evidence passages.

        - The stress test result (no shortfall) is stated in the cashflow section (page 15) rather than a dedicated risk/disadvantages section; however, the method guard explicitly permits this.

CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). Standard Life (EA5) cost comparison contradiction: report narrative (page 8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. the switch saves 0.08%), but the charges table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (meaning the new plan is more expensive). These are directionally opposite. Computed figures confirm the EA5 charges row is calculated on a fund value implying £103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's own value of £3,002, making the EA5 comparison arithmetically unreliable regardless of which figure is used. The Defaqto pension switch report ranks the new solution 5th out of 9 on projected maturity value at mid growth (£125k), below the existing combined solution (2nd, £127k) and all three individual plans being switched (Zurich 1st £128k, Aviva standalone 3rd £126k, Standard Life standalone 4th £126k). The suitability report does not address or acknowledge that the new solution ranks below the existing arrangements and below each of the three constituent plans on a charges-only basis before adviser fees. 26 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report compares existing arrangements against the recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio across multiple dimensions: (1) Scottish Widows (EA2): encashment recommended because SW lacks a single multi-asset fund at risk level 5, does not allow adviser management, limits retirement options to UFPLS only (no FAD), and funds do not meet the client's risk profile. (2) Aviva (EA3): switch recommended because the existing plan is in a lifestyling fund at risk level 2 moving outside agreed risk range, fewer investment options (80+) vs 6,900+, does not allow servicing adviser, and the new plan is 0.06% cheaper (saving £2.25 pa); charges table shows -0.06% impact. (3) Zurich (EA4): switch recommended because funds at risk level 4 do not align to agreed risk level 5, does not allow servicing adviser; charges comparison not stated in narrative but table shown. (4) Standard Life (EA5): switch recommended because the existing plan is stated to be '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8 narrative), but the charges table on page 11 shows impact of +0.26% (an increase, meaning the new plan is more expensive for EA5). (5) Performance comparison shows recommended Aviva Risk 5 portfolio outperforms existing Aviva My Future Focus Consolidation across all periods (3m: 4.74% vs 2.77%; 5y: 40.02% vs 15.58%), but does not outperform Zurich funds. The report states no safeguarded benefits exist for any of the five plans. The report states benefits lost on switching: 'No benefits were listed for the existing products'. Alternatives considered include retaining each plan individually and a generic stakeholder pension. A critical yield / switching cost analysis is included, showing the adviser charge results in a 9.4–10% decrease at mid/high growth rates. The report states the new plan is 0.24% cheaper like-for-like but 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees.

    File:     Policy research [P1, P8, P13, P23]: Scottish Widows — retirement options confirmed as Full and partial UFPLS only, no FAD; 29 funds; no adviser charging. Aviva (existing, EA3) — FAD and UFPLS available; 80+ funds; no adviser charging; lifestyling approach confirmed [P18]. Aviva policy info [P18] confirms no guaranteed rates, no GMP, no protected tax-free lump sum, fund is Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2, auto-switching (lifestage) in place. Zurich — UFPLS only, no FAD, 60+ funds, no adviser charging [P1, P8, P23]. Standard Life — FAD only, 300 funds, allows servicing adviser [P23]. Key Features document [P7] confirms Aviva Pension Portfolio supports FAD, UFPLS and annuity. Pension switch report [P3, P4, P9, P10, P15, P17, P20] provides Defaqto switching analysis: alternatives ranked (Zurich standalone ranked 1st at £128k, existing solution 2nd at £127k, new solution 5th at £125k at mid growth); stakeholder pension comparison included; per-plan alternative comparisons for Aviva-only, Standard Life-only and Zurich-only scenarios included. Pension switch report confirms 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' [P17]. Charges comparison table [P3, P17]: adviser charge of £3,305 taken from new solution at outset; ongoing adviser charge 1.00% included in projections. Critical yield at mid growth for combined switch: 4.8% [P19]. Performance data [P6, P14]: Aviva Risk 5 outperforms Aviva My Future Focus; Zurich outperforms recommended Aviva Risk 5 (46.14% vs 42.40% over 5 years per volatility chart [P6]). Computed figures flag that EA5 (Standard Life, value £3,002) charges row appears computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439), making the EA5 cost comparison unreliable. Pension switch report new solution investment details [P12]: CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc 50% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio 50%. Fund factsheets [P21] provided for CT Universal MAP. Pension switch report ranking [P9] shows new solution ranked 5th out of 9, below all three individual existing pensions being switched (Zurich 1st, existing combined 2nd, Aviva 3rd, Standard Life 4th) on a charges-only basis before adviser fees.

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life (EA5) cost comparison contradiction: report narrative (page 8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. the switch saves 0.08%), but the charges table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (meaning the new plan is more expensive). These are directionally opposite.

      - Computed figures confirm the EA5 charges row is calculated on a fund value implying £103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's own value of £3,002, making the EA5 comparison arithmetically unreliable regardless of which figure is used.

      - The Defaqto pension switch report ranks the new solution 5th out of 9 on projected maturity value at mid growth (£125k), below the existing combined solution (2nd, £127k) and all three individual plans being switched (Zurich 1st £128k, Aviva standalone 3rd £126k, Standard Life standalone 4th £126k). The suitability report does not address or acknowledge that the new solution ranks below the existing arrangements and below each of the three constituent plans on a charges-only basis before adviser fees.

      - The pension switch report shows that at all three growth rates the combined switch results in a decrease in maturity value vs the existing solution (e.g. mid growth: -£2,000 or -1.6%; high growth: -£3,000 or -2.0%) [P17]. The suitability report presents a cost saving of 0.06% on Aviva and asserts a 0.24% like-for-like saving overall, but does not address why projected outcomes are lower.

      - The critical yield analysis (included in the suitability report [P19]) shows that at mid growth the new solution requires 4.8% growth to match the existing pension and produces a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease in value. The report acknowledges this but frames the justification around non-cost factors without explicitly reconciling the materially negative switching outcome for the client.

      - The report states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' for the plans being switched. The extraction flags that featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent (NotPresentInDocument) for all five arrangements. While Aviva policy info [P18] confirms no guarantees for EA3, no provider documentation is in the pack confirming no safeguarded benefits for Zurich (EA4) or Standard Life (EA5), the two largest plans by value being switched.

      - The report states Scottish Widows does not allow adviser management. Policy research [P8] confirms 'Can adviser charging be applied: No' for Scottish Widows, which corroborates this assertion. No discrepancy on this point, but flagged for completeness.

      - Standard Life allows a servicing adviser per [P23] ('Yes' under Allows Servicing Adviser), whereas the three plans being switched (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) are each stated to not allow adviser management. However, [P23] shows Standard Life does allow a servicing adviser, partially contradicting the report's implicit grouping of Standard Life with plans that do not support adviser charging as a rationale for switching — though the report's primary Standard Life rationale rests on charges, not adviser servicing.

      - The Defaqto pension switch report [P9] includes a ranking table noting that alternative providers (Aegon, Aberdeen) and even individual plans (Zurich standalone) produce higher projected values than the chosen new solution. The suitability report's alternatives analysis does not specifically address why the Zurich plan in isolation (ranked 1st) was not retained instead of switched.

    The suitability report contains a structured comparison across fund range, risk alignment, retirement options, adviser servicing, charges and performance for each ceding plan, and this is substantially corroborated by policy research, the Defaqto pension switch report and the Aviva policy information. However, several material concerns arise after applying the evidence.

   

    First, the Standard Life (EA5) cost comparison is directly contradictory: the report narrative states the existing plan is 0.08% more expensive (i.e. switching saves money), but the charges table in the same report shows +0.26% impact (i.e. switching costs more). The derived figures independently confirm the EA5 charges row is calculated on the wrong fund value (£103,439 — Zurich's value — rather than Standard Life's £3,002), meaning neither the narrative nor the table figure is arithmetically grounded in Standard Life's actual fund value. This is a high-severity finding under the decision logic (a switch justified on cost savings that the report's own comparison table contradicts).

   

    Second, the Defaqto pension switch report [P9, P17] shows that the new solution ranks 5th out of 9 alternatives, below the existing combined solution and below each of the three individual constituent plans on projected maturity value at mid growth. The suitability report does not acknowledge or address this. The critical yield section does disclose the negative switching impact (9.4% decrease at mid growth) but frames the overall recommendation on non-financial benefits (risk alignment, fund choice, adviser servicing) without explicitly explaining why the materially worse projected outcome is accepted. This is a legitimate concern but is partially mitigated by the genuine non-cost benefits identified (risk alignment, FAD availability, adviser servicing) which are corroborated by evidence.

   

    Third, the report states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' and the safeguarded features column in [P16] confirms no safeguarded benefits for all five plans. Aviva policy info [P18] explicitly confirms no guaranteed rates and no GMP for EA3. However, no equivalent provider documentation is in the pack for Zurich (EA4, £103,439 — the largest plan) or Standard Life (EA5) positively confirming the absence of safeguarded benefits. The report's own table makes this assertion for both plans, and the Defaqto switch report confirms 'No benefits were listed', but neither constitutes provider-level confirmation. This is a moderate concern given the severity guidance that the highest severity applies where safeguarded benefits are given up with no provider documentation confirming none exist.

   

    The alternatives analysis is substantive: the Defaqto report [P9] includes a market ranking of alternative products and individual-plan alternative comparisons [P4, P5, P20], a stakeholder pension comparison [P9], and the overall position retaining vs switching is shown. The People's Pension is retained (active workplace scheme with employer contributions [P16]), and the Scottish Widows encashment is addressed. These elements are corroborated.

   

    The Standard Life adviser-servicing point is a minor discrepancy: [P23] shows Standard Life does allow a servicing adviser, which is inconsistent with the general framing that the three plans being switched do not support adviser charging. However, the primary rationale for switching Standard Life rests on charges (which is itself contradicted), not adviser servicing.

   

    On vulnerability: the extraction notes vulnerability assessment outcomes are absent (NotPresentInDocument) for both clients. No vulnerability indicators emerge from the retrieved passages that would materially alter this assessment.

   

    Overall, the EA5 cost comparison contradiction (narrative vs table vs derived arithmetic) and the absence of provider-level confirmation of no safeguarded benefits for Zurich and Standard Life are material concerns that leave the replacement justification insufficiently evidenced for those two plans.
