  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts: (1) No exit penalties on any ceding plan. (2) Aviva (EA3): existing plan 0.50% / £18.72 pa; switch saves 0.06% / £2.25 pa. (3) Zurich (EA4): existing plan 0.93% / £961.98 pa; switch saves 0.49% / £506.85 pa. (4) Standard Life (EA5): existing plan 0.18% / £186.19 pa; switch costs an additional 0.26% / £268.94 pa — yet the recommendation rationale on p8 states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving). (5) Like-for-like the new plan is 0.24% cheaper / £264 pa underperformance tolerance; including adviser fee the new plan requires £4,672 outperformance pa (4.24% more expensive). (6) Critical yield at mid-growth rate (described as 2.94% in p11 narrative, 2.95% in the switch report table) is 4.8% to age 75. (7) Benefits claimed: lower costs, elimination of lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, adviser-charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, multi-asset fund alignment. (8) Scottish Widows (EA2): encashed under small pots rules, estimated net receipt £170 (25% tax-free, 75% at marginal rate on £200 fund). (9) No safeguarded benefits or features lost are mentioned anywhere in the report for any ceding plan.

    File:     [P6] (Pension switch report) shows the combined three-plan comparison: existing pension projected £127,000 at mid-growth (2.95%), new solution £125,000; RIY existing 0.3%, new solution 0.6%; critical yield 3.2%; effect of switching £2,000 / 1.6% decrease at mid. [P3] shows Zurich-only comparison: existing £120,000 mid, new solution £118,000 mid; RIY existing 0.3%, new 0.6%; critical yield 3.6%; effect -£2,000 / 1.6%. [P2] shows Standard Life-only comparison: new solution £3,430 mid vs existing £3,440; effect -£10 / 0.3% decrease; critical yield 6.0% (very high relative to fund size). [P12] shows the full comparison including adviser charges: new solution projects £115,000 vs existing £127,000 at mid-growth; RIY new 2.2%; critical yield 4.8%; effect -£12,000 / 9.4% at mid. [P7] confirms existing pension total £110,185.60, new solution day-one value £106,880.60, death-benefit shortfall £3,305 immediately post-switch. [P9] (Zurich policy info) confirms: transfer value/early retirement charge — no deductions taken from current value; fund switch charge £0.00. [P13] (policy research) confirms no MVR on any plan. [P14] (Scottish Widows) confirms no protected tax-free cash, lifestyle arrangement may be affected by retirement age changes. [P16] (Standard Life) shows employer contribution payments history, meaning Standard Life was a former workplace scheme with employer contributions. [P11] (KIID) confirms 0.18% ongoing charge for one of the recommended funds. The switch report at [P6] uses 2.95% as the mid-growth rate, while the suitability report narrative at [P1] states 2.94%. The switch report at [P2] shows Standard Life new-solution critical yield of 6.0% at low growth, and the effect of switching Standard Life is only -£10 at mid-growth, a trivially small difference — yet the charges table in the report shows Standard Life switching adds 0.26% pa cost. No passage in the file addresses whether any safeguarded benefits, guaranteed annuity rates, or other protected features exist for any of the five plans.

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life (EA5) cost direction contradiction: the report's recommendation rationale (p8) states the existing Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving), but the charges comparison table (p11) shows the impact as +0.26% (the recommended plan is MORE expensive). These directly contradict each other.

      - EA5 charge arithmetic computed on wrong fund value: derived figures show the EA5 charges row (£186.19 at 0.18% and £268.94 at 0.26%) imply a fund value of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's value, not Standard Life's value of £3,002. The EA5 rows in the charges table are therefore computed on the wrong plan's fund value, making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable.

      - Mid-growth rate inconsistency: the suitability report narrative (p11/p1) states 2.94%, but the pension switch report table [P6] uses 2.95%. These are not identical.

      - Critical yield figure: the suitability report states critical yield at mid-growth is 4.8% (including adviser charges). [P12] also shows 4.8% at mid-growth including charges, which is consistent. However [P6] (charges-only basis) shows 3.2% — the report does not clearly distinguish between these two figures when citing 4.8%.

      - Standard Life-only critical yield at low growth is 6.0% per [P2], a very high hurdle for a small pot, but the report's narrative does not address this per-plan critical yield for Standard Life specifically.

      - No safeguarded benefits analysis: the report contains no mention of features or guarantees that may be lost on transfer for any of the five ceding plans (extraction confirms these paths are NotPresentInDocument). No provider documentation is cited confirming absence of such features for Zurich, Aviva, Standard Life or People's Pension transfers.

      - Standard Life was a former workplace scheme (employer contributions confirmed in [P16]). The report does not address whether any employer contributions would be lost or whether the scheme is still active.

      - Death benefit shortfall of £3,305 immediately post-switch ([P7]) is evidenced in the switch report but the suitability report does not appear to address this immediate reduction in death benefit coverage.

      - The report states a like-for-like saving of 0.24%/£264 pa and separately a requirement to outperform by 4.24%/£4,672 pa including adviser fee. These are both present but not reconciled for the client in a way that clearly shows the net cost position per plan.

      - Scottish Widows £170 net receipt and 25%/75% tax split: no supporting evidence in the file corroborates the specific £170 figure, the £200 fund value, or the tax calculation — these figures are unmatched in the retrieved passages.

    Several material concerns arise. First and most serious: the Standard Life (EA5) cost comparison is internally contradictory and arithmetically unreliable. The recommendation narrative says switching saves 0.08% on Standard Life, but the charges table shows switching adds 0.26% — a direct contradiction flagged in the extraction's own internal inconsistencies. Compounding this, the derived figures establish that the EA5 charges rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's £3,002, meaning every monetary figure in the Standard Life row of the charges table is wrong. This is a high-severity finding: the largest cost justification for switching Standard Life is built on a miscalculated base and contradicted by the report's own narrative. Second: no safeguarded benefits analysis is present for any ceding plan. The extraction confirms these paths are NotPresentInDocument. While [P9] (Zurich) confirms no transfer charge and [P13] confirms no MVR, there is no documentation in the file confirming absence of guaranteed annuity rates, protected pension ages, protected tax-free cash, or other safeguarded benefits for Zurich, Aviva, Standard Life, or People's Pension. The Scottish Widows response ([P14]) mentions protected tax-free cash (confirmed absent for Scottish Widows), but equivalent confirmation is absent for the other plans being transferred. This is a high-severity omission per the decision logic. Third: Standard Life's payment history ([P16]) shows employer contributions, indicating a former workplace scheme. The report does not address whether this scheme is still active with employer contributions, or whether any employer matching would be lost. If the scheme is dormant this matters less, but the report is silent. Fourth: the immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 post-switch ([P7]) is documented in the switch report but is not addressed in the suitability report, which is a material omission. Fifth: the mid-growth rate discrepancy (2.94% vs 2.95%) is minor and unlikely to be material but is present. The Zurich and combined-plan comparisons in [P3] and [P6] do provide per-plan projections, and the overall structure of the switch report does include alternative comparisons (retain Aviva only, retain Standard Life only, retain Zurich only), which partially addresses the alternatives consideration. The benefits of switching — lifestyling risk, fund alignment at risk level 5, consolidation — are plausible and corroborated by the policy research [P13]. However, the combination of: (a) a self-contradictory and arithmetically wrong Standard Life cost comparison, (b) no safeguarded benefits analysis for any transferring plan, and (c) no discussion of the immediate death benefit shortfall, means the evidence does not fully support the switching justification. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability indicators have been identified in the evidence that would further complicate the switching recommendation, though the client's stated desire to simplify his arrangements ([P13]) is a relevant client-specific factor that supports consolidation.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees

  Potential Concern — severity Low

    Report:   The suitability report asserts, for each of the four arrangements being switched or encashed (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, and Scottish Widows), that 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits.' The report also records that no employer contributions are being lost (all four plans show employerContributionsLost = false). The People's Pension is retained and is not subject to a replacement assertion. The path /replacementAnalysis[]/featuresOrGuaranteesLost is noted as NotPresentInDocument, meaning the report makes no positive claim about other features or guarantees lost beyond the safeguarded-benefits denial.

    File:     Aviva (SP50163762): [P17] states 'This plan doesn't have any guaranteed rates, including annuity rates' and 'The plan doesn't include a Guaranteed Minimum Pension'. No protected tax-free lump sum. No life cover, no waiver of payment. This directly corroborates the report's assertion for Aviva. Zurich: [P15] states 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' This directly corroborates the report's assertion for Zurich. [P1] notes that life cover and waiver benefits are no longer available on new pension plans and raises the question of guaranteed annuity rates, but [P15] definitively confirms none apply. Standard Life (D2301334000): [P4] states 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's)', 'No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP)', 'No Guaranteed Fund at retirement', 'No Reference Scheme Test', and 'As confirmed above there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan.' This directly corroborates the report's assertion for Standard Life. [P23] notes a Differential Fund Discount (Leaver Discount 0.48%) that may not be available from other providers — a feature of value that is not a safeguarded benefit in the regulatory sense but is a favourable charge arrangement that could be lost on transfer. The report does not address this. Scottish Widows (ZU4225575): [P10] states 'there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan.' [P2] shows Guaranteed Minimum Pension = No, Guaranteed Minimum Fund = No, Reference Scheme Test = No, Waiver of premium = No. [P21] shows Guaranteed Annuity Rates = No, Guaranteed Minimum Pension = No. This corroborates the report's assertion for Scottish Widows. Policy research [P3] confirms No GAR, No GMP, No guaranteed growth rates across all plans reviewed. No plan shows active employer contributions being made at point of transfer.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report makes no mention of the Standard Life Differential Fund Discount / Leaver Discount of 0.48% (referenced in [P23]) that may not be available from other providers on transfer. This is a financial feature of the existing Standard Life plan that could be lost on switching and is not addressed in the replacement analysis. It is not a safeguarded benefit in the regulatory sense but it is a potentially advantageous charge arrangement.

      - The report asserts safeguarded benefits are absent for four plans. Four plans have corroborating provider documentation. The fifth plan (People's Pension) is retained so no assertion is required for it — no discrepancy arises there.

      - No discrepancy arises on the employer contributions point: all plans are paid-up with no active employer contributions confirmed by the policy documents, consistent with the report's assertion.

    The report's core assertion — that none of the four ceding arrangements contains safeguarded benefits — is corroborated by explicit provider confirmation for all four plans: Aviva [P17], Zurich [P15], Standard Life [P4], and Scottish Widows [P10] and [P21]. The minimum corroborating category requirement (category E documents) is satisfied for each plan. The employer contributions point is also corroborated: all plans are paid-up with no active contributions. The one genuine gap is the Standard Life Differential Fund Discount (Leaver Discount 0.48%) flagged in [P23]. Provider documentation explicitly warns that 'these low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a feature with financial value attached to the existing Standard Life plan that could be lost on switching. It is not a safeguarded benefit under COBS 9A, but under the broader 'What to look for' criteria for CHK-009 (safeguarded benefits, guarantees or other valuable features that may be lost), it is a relevant feature the report should address. The report is silent on it. This is a Low-severity concern: the Standard Life plan value is £3,002 and the discount is on fund charges, so the monetary impact is limited, and the report does address the overall cost comparison. However, because the provider itself has flagged it as a potentially lost benefit, the omission is a real gap. No vulnerability overlay issues arise that would change this assessment — the vulnerability paths are noted as absent from the extraction, and nothing in the retrieved passages suggests vulnerability factors would alter the conclusion on safeguarded benefits.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report considers the following alternatives: (1) Leave Scottish Widows untouched — rejected because no single multi-asset fund at risk level 5 available (would need two funds, one entirely in money market/cash, leaving 20-30% out of market), no adviser management, no FAD. (2) Leave Aviva untouched (Option 1) — rejected because funds in lifestyling (will move outside agreed risk range), 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution, no adviser management/servicing. (3) Internal fund switch within existing Aviva plan (Option 2) — rejected because no multi-asset funds available, only limited range of other options. (4) Consolidate into People's Pension (Option 4) — rejected because funds do not meet risk profile, 0.06% more expensive plus £4.50 annual admin charge. (5) Leave Zurich untouched (Option 1) — rejected because no risk level 5 funds, blend of risk 7 and risk 2 funds required, no multi-asset funds at risk range, blended funds underperformed available options, no adviser management, no FAD. (6) Internal fund switch within Zurich (Option 2) — rejected because same fund blend issues, not recommended. (7) Leave Standard Life untouched — rejected because lifestyling moves portfolio outside agreed risk range, 0.08% more expensive than recommended solution. The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' as a rejection reason. The charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as '+0.26%' — indicating the new plan is actually more expensive for Standard Life, contradicting the narrative rationale. No alternative to switching the Standard Life plan (e.g. internal fund switch or retaining) other than 'do nothing' appears to be addressed with the same depth as Aviva/Zurich alternatives.

    File:     [P1] corroborates the Scottish Widows rejection reasoning verbatim: 'Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund that aligns with your risk rating of 5. To achieve this level of risk, we would need to combine two funds. One of which is entirely invested in money market (cash) holdings. This would effectively leave around 20-30% of your portfolio out of the market.' [P1] also confirms the People's Pension is retained and Scottish Widows is encashed. [P3] provides the existing arrangements comparison table showing retirement options, fund numbers, servicing adviser availability and other considerations for all five plans. It confirms People's Pension is 'Current workplace scheme', Scottish Widows retirement options are 'Full and partial UFPLS' only (no FAD), Aviva allows FAD, Zurich offers only 'Full and partial UFPLS' (no FAD), Standard Life offers only 'FAD', with no servicing adviser for People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, or Zurich. [P2] corroborates the Standard Life rejection rationale: 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' and lifestyling concerns. However, the extraction metadata records an internal inconsistency: the charges comparison table (page 11) shows Standard Life impact as '+0.26%' (an increase, meaning new plan is more expensive), contradicting the '0.08% more expensive' narrative for Standard Life. [P4] corroborates Zurich rejection reasoning including no risk level 5 funds, blend required, no multi-asset funds, blended performance inferior, no FAD. [P9] (Pension switch report) confirms the three switching plans are Aviva, Zurich, and Standard Life, switching to Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice (SIPP on Aviva Platform). [P10] confirms Aviva existing plan does not allow adviser charging. [P5]/[P6]/[P7]/[P9] provide the switching comparison analysis for the three ceding plans. The file does not contain any document specifically addressing an internal fund switch option for Standard Life as an alternative. No provider documentation from Standard Life addresses whether an internal switch to non-lifestyling funds was possible or not.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states (page 8) that the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as '+0.26%' — indicating the new plan is 0.26% MORE expensive than Standard Life, directly contradicting the narrative cost-saving rationale for that plan.

      - The derived figures confirm EA5's charge row is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002.00), making the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically unreliable regardless of which percentage figure is cited.

      - No alternative to the Standard Life switch other than 'do nothing/leave untouched' is explicitly addressed — unlike Aviva (where both do-nothing and internal fund switch are considered) and Zurich (where both do-nothing and internal fund switch are considered). The report does not address whether an internal fund switch within Standard Life to non-lifestyling funds was possible or impossible.

      - The report does not address featuresOrGuaranteesLost for any of the five existing arrangements (confirmed absent by extraction metadata for EA1–EA5) — there is no explicit statement that no guarantees or safeguarded benefits exist, nor provider documentation confirming this.

      - The costComparison for EA1 (Scottish Widows), EA2 (People's Pension), EA3 (Aviva), EA4 (Zurich), and EA5 (Standard Life) is absent from the replacement analysis section of the canonical model (confirmed by extraction metadata), though partial charge data appears in the charges table.

    The report considers alternatives for each ceding plan and provides plan-specific reasoning in most cases. The Scottish Widows, People's Pension, Aviva, and Zurich alternatives are all addressed with plan-specific rationale corroborated by the file. However, there are two material concerns that survive the guards.

   

    First, and most seriously, the Standard Life cost rationale is internally contradictory. The report's narrative (page 8, corroborated by [P2]) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', framing the switch as delivering a cost saving. The charges comparison table (page 11), however, shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as '+0.26%' — meaning the new plan is 0.26% more expensive than Standard Life, not cheaper. This is not a comparison-methodology artefact; it is a direct contradiction within the report itself, and the derived figures confirm the EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's £3,002.00, making the numerical basis for the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable. The guard for 'Options assessed once for the whole case may legitimately cover several plans' does not explain this discrepancy because the contradiction is between two figures the report itself presents for the same plan. The overall cost comparison (page 11: 0.24% less expensive like-for-like) is computed across the combined portfolio and may mask that Standard Life individually is more expensive on the new platform — but the per-plan narrative claim is demonstrably contradicted by the per-plan table figure.

   

    Second, the report does not address whether an internal fund switch within Standard Life to non-lifestyling funds was possible, unlike the treatment given to Aviva and Zurich where both do-nothing and in-situ switch are explicitly considered and rejected. Given that Standard Life is described as offering 300 funds (per [P3]), the absence of any consideration of an internal switch to non-lifestyling funds within Standard Life is a gap — though this is a lower-severity concern than the cost contradiction.

   

    Third, no features or guarantees lost analysis appears for any plan, and there is no provider documentation in the file confirming that no safeguarded benefits or guarantees exist. The extraction metadata confirms these paths are absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument). Whilst the plans appear to be standard personal pensions (no defined benefit indicators), the absence of any explicit confirmation — either in the report or in supporting provider documents — is a gap.

   

    The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability indicators in the file appear to materially alter this specific assessment of alternatives considered.

   

    Overall: the Standard Life cost contradiction is a genuine mismatch that the guards do not explain, and the missing internal-switch consideration for Standard Life and absence of guarantees/safeguarded benefits analysis add to the concern. The severity is High given the cost contradiction directly undermines the switch justification for that plan, and the charge arithmetic is computed on the wrong fund value.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations unverified

    Report:   The report asserts: (1) No exit penalties on any of the three switched plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life). (2) Aviva existing plan is 0.50% p.a. with annual charge £18.72; switching saves -0.06% / -£2.25 p.a. (3) Zurich existing plan is 0.93% p.a. with annual charge £961.98; switching saves -0.49% / -£506.85 p.a. (4) Standard Life existing plan is 0.18% p.a. with annual charge £186.19; the recommended plan is 0.26% MORE expensive / +£268.94 p.a. (5) Page 8 narrative states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying a saving), contradicting the +0.26% shown in the charges table on page 11. (6) On a like-for-like basis the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than current plans; including adviser fees it is 4.24% more expensive, requiring £4,672 outperformance. (7) Critical yields are stated as 1.9% (low), 4.8% (mid), 7.8% (high) — described as the growth rate required from the new solution to match projected maturity value. (8) Out-of-market risk during transition is disclosed. (9) Scottish Widows encashment under small pots rules estimated to return £170 net. (10) Benefits cited include lower costs (Aviva and Zurich), lifestyling correction, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, and ongoing monitoring.

    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto/switch report document) confirms: (1) Existing pension fund value at switch: £110,185.60; new solution day-one value: £106,880.60, a shortfall of £3,305.00. (2) Critical yields for the overall comparison (Zurich-only section and combined) are 1.9% (low, 0.3% growth), 4.8% (mid, 2.95% growth), 7.8% (high, 5.88% growth) — matching the report's stated figures. (3) Effect of switching at mid growth: -£12,000 / -9.4%; at low: -£10,400 / -9.6%; at high: -£15,000 / -10.0%. (4) Standard Life only comparison: existing RIY 0.6%, new solution RIY 0.6%; effect of switching at mid growth only -£10 / -0.3%; critical yield 6.0% (mid). (5) Aviva only comparison: RIY existing 0.6%, new solution 0.6%; effect of switching at mid -£30 / -0.6%; critical yield 5.9% (high). (6) The switch report does not contain the per-plan annual charge figures (£18.72, £961.98, £186.19) or the percentage differentials (-0.06%, -0.49%, +0.26%) cited from page 11 of the suitability report. These figures are not corroborated by the retrieved evidence. (7) The switch report shows the Zurich plan has a much higher RIY in the new solution (2.1–2.3%) versus existing (0.3%), reflecting materially higher costs in the new plan for the Zurich money specifically. (8) No exit penalties are referenced in the switch report. (9) Features/guarantees lost: not addressed in either the suitability report or the switch report.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. Standard Life charge comparison contradiction: the suitability report's page 8 narrative states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving), while the charges table on page 11 states the recommended plan is +0.26% MORE expensive than Standard Life — a direct internal contradiction that is confirmed by derived figures showing the +0.26% row is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) not Standard Life's (£3,002), making the charge impact figure unreliable.

      - 2. EA5 (Standard Life) charge row computed on wrong fund value: derived figures confirm the £186.19 / 0.18% row and the +£268.94 / +0.26% impact row are arithmetically consistent with Zurich's fund value (~£103,439), not Standard Life's £3,002 — meaning the cost comparison for Standard Life is calculated on the wrong plan value.

      - 3. Per-plan annual charge figures (£18.72, £961.98, £186.19) and differentials (-0.06%, -0.49%, +0.26%) from the suitability report page 11 charges table are not corroborated by any retrieved passage — the switch report does not contain these figures.

      - 4. The switch report shows the Zurich plan's new-solution RIY is 2.1–2.3% versus existing 0.3%, indicating substantially higher costs in the new arrangement for Zurich money, while the suitability report claims a 0.49% per annum saving — these are directionally inconsistent (the switch report shows a net cost increase, not a saving, when expressed as RIY).

      - 5. Immediate fund value reduction at switch: £110,185.60 existing versus £106,880.60 day-one new solution — a £3,305 shortfall — is evidenced in the switch report but does not appear to be disclosed in the suitability report's switching costs section.

      - 6. Critical yield at mid growth is 4.8%, which exceeds the stated mid growth rate of 2.95% (or 2.94% per page 11), meaning the new solution is projected to underperform the existing at mid growth — this is evidenced in the switch report but the suitability report does not appear to address that the critical yield exceeds the mid growth assumption.

      - 7. Features or guarantees lost: the extraction confirms this field is absent from the report for all five existing arrangements (EA1–EA5) — there is no analysis of whether any safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features are lost on transfer from any ceding plan.

      - 8. Page 11 narrative states 'like-for-like 0.24% less expensive' but 'including adviser fees 4.24% more expensive / £4,672 outperformance required' — the two figures measure different things and are both present but the switch report does not corroborate the like-for-like 0.24% figure.

      - 9. The suitability report states critical yield of 1.9% (growth rate required from new solution to match projected maturity value) but the switch report labels the 1.9% critical yield in the context of the overall combined comparison, while the Zurich-only section shows a 0.3% critical yield at low growth — the basis and scope of the cited critical yields need to be read carefully.

      - 10. Scottish Widows encashment (£211.32, EA2): the report states £170 net under small pots rules but no switch report analysis or provider documentation is available to corroborate the tax calculation or encashment terms.

    The check reveals several material concerns. First and most serious: the Standard Life charge comparison (EA5) is computed on the wrong fund value. Derived figures confirm that both the existing annual charge row (£186.19 at 0.18%) and the impact row (+£268.94 at 0.26%) are arithmetically consistent with Zurich's fund value (~£103,439), not Standard Life's own £3,002. This means the cost comparison for Standard Life is fundamentally unreliable. Separately, the suitability report contains a direct internal contradiction between page 8 (Standard Life is 0.08% more expensive, implying a saving) and the page 11 table (+0.26%, a cost increase) — a finding already flagged in the extraction's internalInconsistencies. The switch report provides RIY data for the Zurich plan showing the new solution RIY is 2.1–2.3% versus the existing 0.3% — a material cost increase in the new arrangement for Zurich's money — while the suitability report claims a 0.49% annual saving. These are directionally inconsistent. The non-financial benefits cited (lifestyling correction, risk alignment, multi-asset fund at correct risk level) could in principle justify a net cost increase for Zurich under the guard, but the report does not acknowledge the RIY increase or the fact that the critical yield (4.8% at mid growth) exceeds the mid growth assumption (2.95%), meaning underperformance against the existing arrangement is the projected outcome at mid growth. The £3,305 immediate shortfall at the point of switch is evidenced in the switch report but not disclosed in the suitability report's switching costs section. No analysis of features or guarantees lost is present for any of the five ceding plans — this is confirmed as absent by the extraction (NotPresentInDocument for EA1–EA5 featuresOrGuaranteesLost), representing a highest-severity gap per the severity guidance. The per-plan charge figures in the suitability report's page 11 table are not corroborated by any retrieved passage. Taken together: the Standard Life cost comparison is computed on the wrong plan's fund value, the critical yield exceeds the mid growth rate without acknowledgement, the immediate fund value shortfall is undisclosed, and features/guarantees lost are unaddressed for all plans. These are not explained by any false-positive guard. The non-financial benefits guard could partially explain the cost increase for Zurich but does not cure the absence of safeguarded benefits analysis or the wrong-fund-value error for Standard Life. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this analysis beyond what is already recorded.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that none of the five existing arrangements carry safeguarded benefits being given up (all five isBeingGivenUp = false). It also asserts that none of the five arrangements carry exit penalties, stating 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' for each.

    File:     [P13] (Scottish Widows/People's Pension): Scottish Widows confirms 'there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan', Market Value Adjustment/Reduction not applicable, and no MVR. [P12] (Policy Research): Market Value Reduction shown as 'No' for all five arrangements. [P6] (Policy Research table): confirms transfer values equal current values for all five plans, consistent with no MVR or penalty applying. [P14] (Fact Find): Fields for GMP, Enhanced Tax Free Cash, Guaranteed Annuity Rate, Applicable Penalties, EFI/Loyalty Bonus, Terminal Bonus, Guaranteed Growth Rates are present but left blank/unpopulated for the plans shown. [P8] and [P11]: Standard Life plan value documents confirm plan values but contain no mention of guarantees or penalties. The pension switch report passages [P1]-[P10] contain no reference to any guaranteed annuity rates, protected tax-free cash, GMP, or other safeguarded benefits attaching to any of the three switching plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life).

    Discrepancies:

      - The Fact Find [P14] shows fields for GMP, Enhanced Tax Free Cash, Guaranteed Annuity Rate, Applicable Penalties, EFI/Loyalty Bonus, Terminal Bonus and Guaranteed Growth Rates left blank/unpopulated — these are not positively confirmed as nil; they are simply absent, which means the report's assertion that no safeguarded benefits exist is not fully corroborated by a completed fact-find for all plans.

      - No provider-level confirmation for the Aviva plan (EA3) or the Zurich plan (EA4) positively confirming absence of guarantees or safeguarded benefits is present in the evidence pack — only the Scottish Widows/People's Pension plan has an explicit provider confirmation of no guarantees [P13].

      - The report asserts no exit penalties for all five arrangements; the policy research [P12] confirms no MVR for all five, but no provider letter or discharge confirmation explicitly stating zero exit charges for the Aviva or Zurich plans appears in the pack.

    The report asserts uniformly that no safeguarded benefits are being given up and no exit penalties apply across all five existing arrangements. This assertion is partially corroborated: [P12] and [P13] together confirm no MVR for any plan and no guarantees on the Scottish Widows/People's Pension plan. The pension switch report [P1]-[P10] proceeds on the basis that the three switching plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) carry no such features, and the comparison is conducted without any reference to guaranteed annuity rates or protected benefits, which is consistent with them not existing. However, no explicit provider-level documentation from Aviva or Zurich confirming absence of guarantees or safeguarded benefits appears in the pack. The Fact Find fields for GMP, GAR, Enhanced TFC and penalties are blank rather than positively nil-confirmed for those plans. The guard notes this sub-check only applies where G9.3 identified a benefit actually being given up — G9.3's finding would determine whether this is a live concern. On the available evidence the Scottish Widows plan has positive provider confirmation of no guarantees; the Aviva and Zurich plans lack equivalent provider confirmation, leaving the report's assertion unsupported (though not contradicted) for those two plans. Given Zurich (EA4) holds the dominant value at £103,439.24 — representing roughly 88% of the transferred funds — the absence of provider-level confirmation that no safeguarded benefits attach to it is the most material gap. The Standard Life plan also lacks such confirmation but its value (£3,002) is immaterial. No vulnerability factors identified in the file appear to alter this analysis. The outcome is a potential concern at moderate severity: the report's no-guarantees assertion is plausible and consistent with the switch report's approach, but is unsupported by provider documentation for the two largest switching plans.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report considers multiple alternatives for each ceding plan and rejects each with plan-specific reasons. For Scottish Widows: no single multi-asset fund at risk level 5; achieving that risk would require combining two funds leaving ~20–30% in money market (cash) holdings; no adviser management; no FAD. For Aviva (existing): lifestyling will move portfolio outside agreed risk range over time; plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended; no adviser management. In-situ Aviva fund switch rejected because there are no multi-asset funds within the existing plan. For Zurich: no risk level 5 multi-asset funds; blended funds have not performed as well as Aviva options; internal fund switch not recommended as main priority is consolidation. For Standard Life: plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution; lifestyling funds will move portfolio outside risk range; no adviser management. Consolidation into People's Pension rejected: funds do not meet risk profile; plan is 0.06% more expensive plus a £4.50 annual admin charge; no adviser management; no FAD unless fund value rises to £10k. A new Aviva Pension Portfolio was recommended as the receiving scheme. The report also presents a charges table (page 11) which the canonical model notes shows '+0.26%' impact for Standard Life (i.e. new plan is more expensive for Standard Life), contradicting the page 8 narrative that says 0.08% saving.

    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto) provides plan-by-plan alternative comparison tables for Aviva, Standard Life and Zurich individually, showing projected maturity values at low/mid/high growth rates against the new solution. For the overall combined comparison (all three switching pensions) the new solution produces lower projected values across all growth rates (e.g. mid-rate: existing £127,000 vs new solution £115,000, a £12,000 or 9.4% decrease at mid-growth). Per-plan: Aviva plan switch produces a small decrease (mid: £4,280 vs £4,260, £20 decrease); Standard Life switch also produces a small decrease (mid: £3,440 vs £3,430, £10 decrease); Zurich switch produces a material decrease (mid: £127,000 vs £125,000, £2,000 or 1.6% decrease). The switch report states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' (i.e. no safeguarded benefits identified). The Zurich policy information explicitly states: 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' The Scottish Widows policy documentation confirms no GMP, no Guaranteed Minimum Fund. The Aviva policy documentation confirms FAD and UFPLS are available on the existing Aviva plan, consistent with the report's claim it allows FAD. The Standard Life policy confirms UFPLS is not facilitated. The policy research table confirms People's Pension does not offer FAD unless fund value increases to £10k, and Scottish Widows offers only UFPLS. The charges comparison table in the switch report shows the Standard Life impact as '+0.26%' — meaning the new solution is MORE expensive than Standard Life on a standalone basis — directly contradicting the suitability report's narrative that Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'. The EA5 charge arithmetic derived figures confirm the Standard Life row in the charges table was computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) not Standard Life's (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable. The overall combined comparison (including adviser charges) shows the new solution requires £4,672 more outperformance (4.24%) than the existing pensions, which the report discloses but does not appear to be reconciled with the claimed cost savings for Standard Life specifically.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report (page 8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact for Standard Life as '+0.26%' — indicating the new plan is MORE expensive on a Standard Life standalone basis. These are directly contradictory.

      - The derived figures confirm that the EA5 (Standard Life) charge impact row of '+0.26%' and the EA5 annual charge figure were both computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,438–£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002, making any cost comparison for Standard Life based on this table arithmetically unreliable.

      - The combined switch comparison shows the new solution is projected to produce lower values than the existing pensions across all growth rates (e.g. mid: £127,000 vs £115,000), which is disclosed but the report's per-plan cost-saving narrative for Standard Life is not reconciled with this overall deterioration.

      - The report asserts the Aviva existing plan does not allow adviser management/servicing, but the Aviva policy documentation shows the existing Aviva plan is a personal pension — the report's existing arrangements table shows 'Allows Servicing Adviser: No' for Aviva, which is consistent; however this is a minor label point rather than a factual conflict.

      - No features or guarantees lost section is populated in the report (extraction confirms 'featuresOrGuaranteesLost' absent across all five plans as NotPresentInDocument), though the switch report states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products', and the Zurich provider letter and Scottish Widows documentation confirm no safeguarded benefits — this absence is therefore evidenced rather than unexplained silence for those two plans. Aviva and Standard Life provider documentation does not explicitly list a 'no safeguarded benefits' statement in the passages provided, though neither policy information document references any guarantees.

      - No cost comparison at plan level is present in the report for EA1 (People's Pension), EA2 (Scottish Widows) or the retained arrangements — though these are not being switched into the new plan, so this is less material.

    The key finding is the direct internal contradiction between the suitability report's narrative on page 8 (Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution') and the charges comparison table on page 11 which shows the Standard Life impact as '+0.26%' — meaning the new solution is more expensive for Standard Life, not cheaper. This is confirmed by the derived figures, which show the Standard Life row in the charges table was computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002. This means the charges table for Standard Life is arithmetically wrong, having been computed on the wrong plan's fund value. The consequence is that the cost-saving justification for the Standard Life switch is unsubstantiated: the narrative figure (0.08% saving) contradicts the table figure (+0.26% increase), and the table itself is based on the wrong fund value. This is a High severity issue per the decision logic ('a switch justified on cost savings that the report's own comparison table contradicts'). For the remaining plans, the alternative analysis is generally well-supported. The Scottish Widows rejection reasons (no risk level 5 single multi-asset fund, no adviser management, no FAD) are corroborated by the policy research table and Scottish Widows documentation. The Aviva in-situ switch rejection (no multi-asset funds, lifestyling risk) is stated in the report and consistent with the switch report's appendix showing limited fund range. The Zurich rejection (no risk level 5 multi-asset funds, blended fund underperformance, consolidation objective) is corroborated by the switch report and policy research. The People's Pension consolidation rejection (funds don't meet risk profile, more expensive, no FAD below £10k) is corroborated by the policy research table. The switch report confirms 'No benefits were listed for the existing products', and the Zurich provider letter explicitly confirms no GMP, no safeguarding, no guaranteed benefits, no MVR — this corroborates the report's silence on lost guarantees for Zurich. Scottish Widows documentation confirms no GMP/Guaranteed Minimum Fund. However, for Aviva (EA3) and Standard Life (EA5), the file does not contain an equivalent explicit 'no safeguarded benefits' confirmation in the passages provided, though neither policy document references any such features — this is a minor gap rather than a contradiction. The overall combined comparison in the switch report shows the new solution underperforms the existing pensions on projected maturity values at all growth rates (mid: £12,000 or 9.4% decrease), with an adviser-charge-inclusive required outperformance of £4,672 (4.24%). The report discloses this and justifies it on non-cost grounds (consolidation, risk alignment, adviser management, FAD flexibility). The guard on consolidation as a valid client objective applies here — CHK-002 objectives include the client's expressed desire to consolidate pensions and gain better control. The alternatives considered and rejected are plan-specific and client-specific, which satisfies the requirement for alternatives analysis. The principal concern is the Standard Life cost comparison contradiction, which is a High severity finding. The vulnerability overlay: no evidence of vulnerability drivers that would materially alter this assessment has been identified in the passages provided.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts multiple benefits of switching for the three plans being transferred (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life): (1) Lower costs — Aviva existing plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution; Standard Life existing plan is 0.08% more expensive than recommended solution; Zurich saving of 0.49% per annum. (2) Lifestyling funds will eventually move outside agreed risk range. (3) Greater investment choice. (4) Multi-asset fund selection (especially for Zurich where risk level 5 funds are unavailable). (5) Adviser charging facilitation. (6) Ongoing monitoring. (7) Enhanced retirement flexibility. The report also states that including adviser fees the recommended plan requires £4,672 outperformance in the first year and a critical yield of 4.8% at mid-growth rate of 2.94%. The net benefit conclusion for the combined switch states lower costs, greater investment flexibility, and continued professional oversight justify the switch. For Standard Life specifically, the cost-saving claim of 0.08% is cited as a reason to switch.

    File:     The pension switch report (category G) confirms: Aviva existing plan charges 0.50% with the impact of the recommended alternative being -0.06% (saving), consistent with the report. Zurich existing plan charges 0.93% with the impact of -0.49% (saving), consistent with the report. Standard Life existing plan charges 0.18% with the impact shown in the charges table as +0.26% (the recommended plan is MORE expensive, not cheaper). The switch report also shows that in the all-plans combined comparison, the new solution ranks 5th out of 10 on projected maturity value at mid growth (£125,000 vs £127,000 for the existing solution). The Zurich-only alternative comparison shows the new solution produces £12,000 or 9.4% less at mid growth. The all-plans-combined comparison shows the new solution is £2,000 or 1.6% less at mid growth. The Zurich policy info confirms no transfer value/early retirement charge and no fund switch charge. Category E (Zurich Policy Info) notes that Zurich plans may have guaranteed annuity rates but the box for 'Yes' is not checked in the transfer questionnaire. The switch report confirms the new solution is ranked below keeping the existing Zurich plan alone. The like-for-like cost comparison shows the new plan is 0.24% less expensive, but including adviser fees it is 4.24% more expensive, requiring £4,672 outperformance.

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life cost claim: the report (page 8) states the existing Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges table in the suitability report (page 11) and the canonical model show the impact as +0.26% — the recommended plan is 0.26% MORE expensive than Standard Life, contradicting the stated cost-saving rationale for this plan.

      - The derived figures confirm EA5 (Standard Life) annual charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89 implied) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison in the table unreliable and based on the wrong plan value.

      - The report claims 'lower costs' as a net benefit conclusion for the combined switch including Standard Life, but the Standard Life individual plan comparison shows a cost increase, not a saving.

      - The switch report ranks the new solution 5th (£125,000) behind retaining the existing solution (£127,000) at mid growth, meaning at a like-for-like level the switch produces a lower projected outcome; the report does not explicitly address this ranking or explain why the lower-ranked solution is nonetheless recommended.

      - The report states the critical yield is 4.8% at mid-growth rate of 2.94% (page 18 shows 2.95%), a minor inconsistency between narrative and table.

      - The report states the combined pension value is 'approximately £110,000' on page 3, but the arithmetic sum of all five plans is £116,998.47; the £110,185 new plan value matches only the three switching plans, not the five-plan total.

      - No analysis of features or guarantees that might be lost is present in the report for any of the three switching plans (extraction confirms featuresOrGuaranteesLost NotPresentInDocument for EA1–EA5).

      - No plan-specific cost comparison is present in the report for EA1 (People's Pension) or EA2 (Scottish Widows), though these are not among the three switching plans so may not be directly relevant to the cost-justification question.

    The central issue under G9.8 is whether claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits are supported by evidence. The requirement bites where the recommended plan is more expensive. For Aviva and Zurich, the switch produces cost savings (-0.06% and -0.49% respectively), so the 'higher costs justified by benefits' question does not arise for those two plans — the guard applies and no finding is needed on cost justification for them. For Standard Life (EA5), however, the report states '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' as a reason to switch (i.e. claims a cost saving), but the charges table in the same report shows +0.26% (the new plan is more expensive). The derived figures confirm this +0.26% row is computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,438) rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002, making the Standard Life charge comparison arithmetically unreliable. Whichever figure is correct, at least one of the two stated figures is wrong: either the 0.08% saving on page 8 or the 0.26% increase on page 11. The internal inconsistency is a material concern because 'lower costs' is cited as a primary justification for switching Standard Life. If the table is correct (new plan is 0.26% more expensive), the lower-cost rationale is entirely absent for this plan and a cost-increase justification was required but not provided. The report also does not address what features or guarantees might be lost on any of the three switching plans — the extraction confirms this is NotPresentInDocument for all five arrangements. The Zurich policy info (P5) references possible guaranteed annuity rates but the questionnaire checkbox is not marked 'Yes', so it is not definitively established that no guaranteed rates exist on the Zurich plan; however this is not confirmed either way in the file. The switch report evidence (P8) shows the new solution ranks 5th out of 10 products, below the existing combined solution (£127,000 vs £125,000 at mid growth), and the Zurich-only alternative comparison (P3/P4) shows the new solution produces £12,000 less at mid growth — this context is relevant because the report claims benefits justify any additional cost, yet the switch report itself shows the new solution underperforms on projected maturity value. The report does acknowledge the 4.8% critical yield requirement and £4,672 first-year outperformance requirement, and asserts this is achievable based on past fund performance, but no evidence in the file corroborates that the recommended funds have historically achieved 4.8%+. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that materially alter this analysis. The primary finding is the Standard Life cost contradiction — a cost saving is claimed as justification but the report's own charges table (and derived figures) show a cost increase, with the table itself computed on the wrong plan's fund value, rendering the comparison unreliable. This is a High severity concern per the guidance ('a switch justified on cost savings that the report's own comparison table contradicts').

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts four net-benefit conclusions: (1) encashing the Scottish Widows small pot under small pots rules delivers immediate accessible value and would not detract from the retirement plan (estimated net receipt £170); (2) switching Aviva to the new Aviva platform gives lower costs (existing 0.06% more expensive), greater investment flexibility, and professional oversight; (3) switching Zurich gives greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, and professional oversight (no cost saving claimed); (4) switching Standard Life gives lower costs (existing 0.08% more expensive), greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, and professional oversight. The People's Pension is retained because employer and client contributions continue and transferring would lose employer contributions. Rationales for switching include: Aviva lifestyling funds will move the portfolio outside the agreed risk range; the new scheme offers wider investment choice; adviser charges can be deducted directly; Scottish Widows does not offer a single multi-asset fund at risk rating 5; Scottish Widows does not allow adviser management. The charges comparison table (page 11) shows Aviva existing 0.50% vs impact -0.06%; Zurich existing 0.93% vs impact -0.19% (EA4) and Standard Life existing 0.18% vs impact +0.26% (EA5, per extracted internal inconsistency).

    File:     [P8] Aviva policy info confirms the plan is invested in 'Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2' with a Lifestage Investment Approach (auto-switching). No guaranteed rates, no Guaranteed Minimum Pension, no protected tax-free lump sum. Value £3,744.36. [P3] Zurich policy info explicitly states: 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' Drawdown options are not available on the Zurich plan but it can be transferred. [P9] Standard Life policy info confirms transfer value available; no specific guarantees flagged in retrieved text. [P14/P10] Scottish Widows confirms: 'Money4Life Plans (Plans starting with ZU) do not support ongoing advisor charging, the transferring of Servicing Rights or Commission'; 'no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan'; employer and employee contributions are ongoing. [P16] File note confirms: Scottish Widows discounted as 'no servicing and reduced flexibility'; Aviva alternative discounted because only mixed asset fund is risk rated 4 (does not meet ATR); Zurich internal switch discounted because no risk-5 multi-asset funds, blend used has not performed as well; Standard Life discounted as all funds in 20-60% equities range are more expensive than Aviva offer. [P5/P6] Pension switching report (Defaqto) shows new solution ranked 5th by maturity value (£125,000) against existing solution ranked 2nd (£127,000); Zurich standalone ranked 1st (£128,000); the new solution produces a lower projected maturity value than simply staying with the existing pensions. [P20] Pension switch report summary confirms comparison was conducted and recommendation is to consolidate into Aviva Platform Pension Portfolio Choice SIPP. [P21] Suitability report comparison table shows Scottish Widows does not allow servicing adviser; Standard Life does allow servicing adviser. [P22] Charges comparison table confirms Aviva existing 0.50%/£18.72, impact -0.06%; but for Standard Life the impact column shows +0.26% whereas the page 8 narrative states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' — these are contradictory. The derived figures confirm EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) not Standard Life's (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8 narrative, a cost saving in favour of switching). The charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase — i.e. the new plan is MORE expensive for Standard Life, not cheaper). These are directly contradictory.

      - The derived figures confirm that the Standard Life (EA5) charge rows in the comparison table are computed using Zurich's current fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), rendering the entire Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically unreliable.

      - The Pension switching report (Defaqto, P5/P6) ranks the new solution 5th by projected maturity value (£125,000) against the existing combined solution ranked 2nd (£127,000). The report asserts 'lower costs' and net benefit from switching but the independent comparison tool shows the new solution produces a lower projected maturity value than retaining the existing pensions — a material contradiction of the cost/benefit rationale.

      - The report claims switching Aviva gives 'lower costs' and the existing Aviva plan is '0.06% more expensive'. The Defaqto report ranks Aviva standalone 3rd (£126,000 projected), also higher than the new solution (£125,000), which is consistent with the new solution being slightly cheaper on charges but also slightly worse in projected outcome — the net-benefit narrative of 'lower costs therefore better outcome' is weakened by the projection evidence.

      - The report's net-benefit conclusion for the Zurich switch does not claim a cost saving but the charges table shows a -0.19% impact (a saving), which is directionally consistent; however no discrepancy here — the report simply did not claim a cost saving for Zurich.

      - The report does not address the fact that the new solution has a lower projected maturity value (£125,000) than the existing solution (£127,000) per the Defaqto switching report, nor does it explain why this is outweighed by other benefits for the client.

      - The Scottish Widows confirmation (P14/P10) states the plan 'does not support ongoing advisor charging' — this corroborates the report's no-adviser-management rationale. No discrepancy on this point.

      - The Aviva policy info (P8) confirms the Lifestage Investment Approach is active — this corroborates the report's lifestyling rationale. No discrepancy on this point.

      - The Zurich policy info (P3) confirms no guarantees, no GMP, no protected tax-free cash — this corroborates the report's implicit assertion that no safeguarded benefits are lost on the Zurich transfer. No discrepancy on this point.

      - The report states the new plan is overall '0.24% less expensive' on a like-for-like basis but '4.24% more expensive' including adviser fees (per internal inconsistency noted in extraction). The report presents both figures but does not clearly reconcile them for the client; the monetary difference is £264 vs £4,672 — a material gap. This is partially addressed in the charges section but the net-benefit conclusions do not acknowledge that including adviser fees the arrangement is materially more expensive.

    The replacement rationale rests on several planks: (1) cost savings, (2) greater investment flexibility and adviser management capability, (3) removal of lifestyling risk, (4) consolidation. The file evidence is mixed in its support.

   

    Supporting the rationale: The Aviva policy info [P8] confirms lifestyling is active, directly corroborating the lifestyling risk argument. The Scottish Widows confirmations [P10, P14] confirm no adviser charging is supported, corroborating that rationale. The Zurich policy info [P3] confirms no safeguarded benefits, GMP or protected tax-free cash, so no safeguarded benefits analysis was needed and none being present is not itself a gap. The file notes [P16] record that alternatives (internal fund switches, retaining at existing providers) were considered and discounted with reasons. The Defaqto comparison [P5, P20] provides documented alternative comparison including a stakeholder pension option.

   

    Contradicting the rationale: The most serious discrepancy is the Standard Life cost comparison. The report's narrative (page 8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' — i.e. switching saves money. The charges comparison table shows +0.26% (new plan is more expensive). The derived figures confirm this table row was computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), making the entire Standard Life cost comparison wrong regardless of which direction the error runs. The cost-saving rationale for the Standard Life switch is therefore unsupported and potentially inverted.

   

    A further material concern is that the Defaqto switching report [P6] shows the new solution (£125,000 projected at mid-growth) underperforms the existing combined solution (£127,000) and also underperforms a standalone Zurich retention (£128,000) and Aviva retention (£126,000). The report asserts net benefit from lower costs and better outcomes, but the switching report's own projections show the new solution produces a lower projected maturity value. The report does not explain this discrepancy or argue why non-cost benefits outweigh the projected value shortfall.

   

    The '4.24% more expensive including adviser fees' figure versus '0.24% cheaper on a like-for-like basis' is disclosed in the charges section but is not reconciled in the net-benefit conclusions, which state 'lower costs' without the qualifier that this is before adviser charges.

   

    The Standard Life cost comparison error is the highest-severity issue: the reported rationale states a cost saving that the report's own charges table contradicts, and the derived figures confirm the table was computed on the wrong fund value. This is a direct internal contradiction of a replacement rationale claim. The projected maturity value shortfall versus the existing solution, unaddressed in the report, is a further material omission.

   

    No vulnerability flags are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment — the file notes [P18] record capacity for loss and ATR considerations but no vulnerability characteristics that would heighten the concern about the replacement rationale specifically.

   

    The guard regarding charges rationale being contradicted by a charges schedule showing the opposite applies here and confirms this is a genuine finding, not a spurious mismatch.

  [G9.10] Material replacement-related information omitted from the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts (page 5) that none of the four ceding plans being switched contain any safeguarded benefits ('This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits'), that no exit/switching charges apply to any of the five arrangements ('No charges for transfer and/or switching'), and that no employer contributions are being lost on any transfer. For Standard Life (EA5), the page 8 narrative states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', implying a cost saving from switching. The charges comparison table on page 11 states the new Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive on a like-for-like basis (£264 underperformance tolerance) but 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees (£4,672 outperformance required). The critical yield table (page 18) labels the mid growth rate as 2.95%; page 11 refers to 2.94%.

    File:     P11 (Zurich policy info) states: 'If this plan has capital units, there will be an exit charge applied if the fund is transferred. This exit charge is a standard term of the contract... The charge is capped at 1% from age 55.' P13 (Zurich) confirms a 'Transfer value and early retirement charge' exists described as 'the difference between current and transfer values' but then states 'There are no deductions taken from the current value if benefits are transferred or taken. The initial set up charges have already been paid.' P11 also records £0.00 employer payments and £0.00 personal regular payments to the Zurich plan, and £2,344.58 former protected rights transfer payments and £12,419.11 former protected rights payments. P1/P2/P3/P4/P5/P6/P7/P8/P10 (People's Pension) show ongoing employer and employee contributions to the People's Pension — which is retained, not switched. P16 (Standard Life) confirms no bid-offer spread, 100% allocation, no loyalty bonus, no platform charge, is a defined contribution plan, and the client can restart contributions at any time; no protected tax-free cash. P9 (Standard Life) confirms current value £3,002.02. P20 (policy research file, category G) records 'No' for Market Value Reduction for all plans reviewed. P22 (pension switch report, category G) shows a critical yield table with mid growth rate labelled 2.95%, and the effect of switching at all three growth rates as a decrease (e.g. mid: £12,000 or 9.4% decrease; low: £10,400 or 9.6% decrease; high: £15,000 or 10.0% decrease). P22 also records existing pension fund value at point of switching as £110,185.60. The charges comparison table extracted in the canonical model shows EA5 (Standard Life) charge impact as +0.26% — an increase — contradicting the page 8 narrative of a 0.08% saving. The derived figures confirm EA5's existing annual charge and impact percentage are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438–£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002, rendering the EA5 cost comparison unreliable.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. Standard Life (EA5) cost comparison contradiction: page 8 narrative states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges table on page 11 shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase). These are directly contradictory.

      - 2. EA5 charge arithmetic error: derived figures confirm that both the EA5 existing annual charge (£186.19 at 0.18%) and the EA5 impact (£268.94 at 0.26%) imply a fund value of approximately £103,438–£103,439, which is Zurich's (EA4) current value, not Standard Life's £3,002. The EA5 cost comparison rows have been computed on the wrong plan's fund value, making the entire EA5 cost/benefit comparison unreliable.

      - 3. Zurich exit charge risk not addressed: P11 discloses that capital units carry an exit charge (capped at 1% from age 55). The report states 'No charges for transfer and/or switching' for Zurich. P13 clarifies that for this specific plan 'There are no deductions taken from the current value if benefits are transferred', which is consistent with the report — but the capital units exit charge language in P11 creates ambiguity that the report does not address or explain.

      - 4. Mid growth rate label discrepancy: page 11 refers to a mid-growth rate of 2.94%; the critical yield table on page 18 (and P22) labels it as 2.95%.

      - 5. The switch comparison in P22 shows the effect of switching as a projected decrease at all three growth rates (mid: -£12,000 / -9.4%), with critical yield at 4.8% (mid). The report must justify switching despite a projected value reduction; the only justification offered is simplification and cost. The Standard Life cost justification is undermined by the arithmetic error identified in discrepancy 2.

      - 6. People's Pension (EA1/EA2) ongoing employer contributions: P1–P8/P10 show live employer and employee contributions being paid to the People's Pension. The report records this plan as retained (not switched), and employer contributions lost is recorded as false for all plans — consistent with retention. However, the report does not explicitly address whether employer contributions would be lost if the People's Pension were ever consolidated in future, and the cashflow ambiguity (People's Pension possibly omitted from cashflow modelling) is noted.

      - 7. Zurich plan contains former protected rights (£2,344.58 former protected rights transfer payments; £12,419.11 former protected rights payments per P11). The report does not address whether any restrictions or residual protected rights considerations apply to the transfer of these funds, beyond the generic 'no safeguarded benefits' statement.

    The most significant issue is the Standard Life (EA5) cost comparison. The report's page 8 narrative states the existing Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', which would justify switching on cost grounds. However, the charges table on page 11 shows the impact of switching as +0.26% — meaning the new plan is more expensive, not cheaper. This is an internal contradiction within the report itself. The contradiction is further compounded by the derived figures, which confirm that the EA5 charge rows were computed using Zurich's fund value (approximately £103,438) rather than Standard Life's actual value of £3,002. This is a fundamental arithmetic error: the Standard Life cost comparison is unreliable because it was modelled on the wrong fund value. Under the severity guidance, a switch justified on cost savings that the report's own comparison table contradicts is rated High. The EA5 cost justification therefore fails both on the face of the table and on the arithmetic underpinning it.

   

    On safeguarded benefits: the report's generic statement ('This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits') appears four times and is not plan-specific. P16 (Standard Life) confirms no protected tax-free cash. P11/P13 (Zurich) confirm the plan contains former protected rights but no safeguarded benefits in the COBS 9A sense, and P13 confirms no deductions on transfer for this specific plan. P15 (Scottish Widows) shows a small plan being encashed. The no-safeguarded-benefits assertions are broadly consistent with the provider documentation, though the generic rather than plan-specific wording is a presentation weakness rather than a factual contradiction.

   

    On exit penalties: the report states no exit charges. P13 (Zurich) confirms no deductions on transfer for the specific plan, consistent with the report. P11 raises the capital units exit charge as a general product feature but P13 clarifies it does not apply here. P20 confirms no MVR for all reviewed plans. The nil exit penalty assertion is supported by the available provider evidence.

   

    On employer contributions: the People's Pension (retained) shows live employer contributions in P1–P8/P10. Since this plan is retained rather than switched, no employer contributions are lost by the advice as given. The employer contributions lost = false assertions are consistent with the file for the plans actually being switched.

   

    On former protected rights in the Zurich plan: P11 records £14,763.69 of former protected rights payments. The report does not address any residual considerations for these funds beyond the generic no-safeguarded-benefits statement. While former protected rights no longer carry annuity purchase requirements post-2015, the absence of any plan-specific commentary on this point, given the material proportion of the Zurich fund these represent, is a gap.

   

    The mid growth rate discrepancy (2.94% vs 2.95%) is marginal and unlikely to be material.

   

    Vulnerability overlay: the extraction notes that vulnerability assessment outcomes are absent from the canonical model (NotPresentInDocument). No vulnerability characteristics are evidenced in the retrieved passages that would materially alter this assessment.

   

    Overall: the EA5 cost comparison is both internally contradicted and arithmetically unreliable, constituting a material gap in the replacement justification for the Standard Life plan. The overall outcome is Potential Concern at High severity.
